Adamantinoma

Pathology · 1 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rare<300 cases documented · <1% of malignant primary bone tumours

Ageyoung adults 20–40 yr · 75% in 2nd–4th decades

Sitemid-tibial diaphysis, anterior cortex (tibia 80–90%) · synchronous ipsilateral fibula 10–50% · other long bones rare

Naturelow-grade malignant · aetiology unknown

Osteofibrous dysplasia association

OFD linkcontroversial · once thought precursor → now doubted · some regard as a continuum

Why it mattersOFD benign → observe · adamantinoma malignant → wide resection

Genes & lab markers

Cytokeratin (keratin) +epithelial nests stain positive · marks epithelial differentiation

3Presentation

Symptomspain of months to years · indolent course

Signsanterior tibial bowing or palpable firm mass · no soft-tissue mass, no lymphadenopathy

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raymultiple sharply circumscribed lucencies ("soap bubble") with interspersed sclerosis · eccentric, intracortical, lytic · anterior mid-tibia

Evolutionserial films show growth and erosion through the cortex → favours adamantinoma over OFD

Periosteal reactiontypically none (unlike other primary bone tumours)

MRIcortical erosion / destruction of anterior tibial cortex

Histology

Biphasicepithelial + fibrous mesenchymal cells

Epithelial nestspalisading or glandular pattern · basaloid, spindle, tubular, squamous patterns (basaloid + tubular commonest) · keratin +

Stromabackground fibrous (spindle-cell) stroma · may have nuclear atypia, mitoses

Histology — which pattern is which?

Histology Diagnosis
Biphasic glandular epithelial cells + spindle cells ✓ Adamantinoma
Woven trabeculae with osteoblastic rimming, fibrous stroma ("Chinese letters") Osteofibrous dysplasia
Trabeculae without osteoblastic rimming in fibrous stroma Fibrous dysplasia
Multinucleated giant cells, stromal nuclei = giant-cell nuclei Giant cell tumour
Herringbone pattern, pleomorphic cells Fibrosarcoma

Differential of a tibial diaphyseal lesion

Tibial diaphysis Wide resection alone
Adamantinoma Yes Yes
Osteofibrous dysplasia Yes No (observe)
Chondrosarcoma (conventional) — Yes
Parosteal osteosarcoma — Yes

Ewingmedullary, aggressive periosteal reaction, small round blue cells

Chondromyxoid fibromaanterior proximal tibia, medullary, thins cortex

Osteoid osteomacortical, <1 cm, sclerotic rim

5Management

Treatment — wide resection

Anterior tibial cortical lytic lesion
  • Child, benign trabeculae with rimming (OFD)→OBSERVE
    • Bowing / impending fracture→BRACE
  • Adult, epithelial nests (adamantinoma)→WIDE EN BLOC RESECTION
    • Failed resection/reconstruction→AMPUTATION (salvage)

Wide-margin en bloc resectionstandard of care · prevents recurrence + metastasis

Reconstructionintercalary resection → allograft or intercalary megaprosthesis · amputation usually unnecessary

No chemo / no radiotherapylow-grade, not effective for local control

Not indicatedobservation · curettage + graft · external beam radiation · chemo + excision

6Complications

Metastasislungs ~25% (Orthobullets) · up to 20% (OB explanation) · late → long-term follow-up

Local recurrencehigh unless wide margin · uncommon after negative-margin excision

X vs Y

Adamantinoma Osteofibrous dysplasia
Age Adult 20–40 Child (<10)
Behaviour Low-grade malignant Benign
Imaging Cortical, may erode through cortex, progresses Eccentric intracortical lytic, anterior bowing
Histology Epithelial nests (keratin +) in fibrous stroma Woven bone with osteoblastic rimming · no epithelial nests
Treatment Wide resection Observe · brace if bowing · surgery delayed to maturity (recurs)

How it's asked

"Adult, anterior mid-tibial soap-bubble cortical lesion, epithelial nests in fibrous stroma — diagnosis?"adamantinoma
✗ Wrong options: OFD · osteomyelitis · periosteal OS (no osteoid) · fibrous dysplasia

"Histology seen only in adamantinoma, not OFD?"biphasic glandular epithelial cells surrounded by spindle cells

"Best treatment?"wide en bloc resection + reconstruction
✗ Wrong options: observation · bracing · chemo/radiation · AKA first

"Epithelial nests stain for?"cytokeratin

"Site of metastasis?"lungs (~25%) · late → long follow-up

Aneurysmal Bone Cyst

Pathology · 2 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Age75% <20 yr · first 2–3 decades

Sitelong bones >60% (femur, tibia commonest) · metaphysis · spine 15% (posterior elements — lamina, spinous process) · pelvis posterior elements

Footmetatarsal (metaphysis) and calcaneus commonest (✗ Wrong options: calcaneal UBC = incidental)

Naturebenign, locally aggressive/destructive, may extend into soft tissue · same sites as telangiectatic OS

Primary vs secondary

Primary ABCneoplasm · USP6 rearrangement

Secondary ABCnot a neoplasm (no translocation) · arises in another tumour ~30%: GCT, chondroblastoma, fibrous dysplasia, chondromyxoid fibroma, NOF

Genes & lab markers — USP6

GeneUSP6 (TRE2/TRE17) on 17p13 ↑ (ubiquitin-specific protease) (TO)

Translocationt(16;17)(q22;p13) → CDH11 promoter (16q22) drives USP6

Frequency"invariably" found → neoplastic origin (TO) · only ~70% show USP6 ↑ (OB) — read the options

Key useUSP6 ↑ separates ABC from telangiectatic OS (no USP6) and GCT

Gene / translocation Tumour (distractors)
USP6 – CDH11 ✓ ABC
ASPL–TFE3 t(X;17) Alveolar soft part sarcoma
EWS–ATF1 t(12;22) Clear cell sarcoma (OB text: "clear cell chondrosarcoma")
EWS–FLI1 t(11;22) Ewing sarcoma
EWS–WT1 t(11;22) Desmoplastic small round cell tumour
EWS–CHN t(9;22) Extraskeletal myxoid chondrosarcoma
PAX3–FKHR t(2;13) Alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma

3Presentation

Symptomspain + swelling, slow-growing · may present with pathologic fracture

Spineneurologic deficit · even cauda equina (posterior-element ABC)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayexpansile, eccentric, lytic, metaphyseal · bony septae ("bubbly") · thin rim of periosteal new bone · no matrix

Widthcan expand wider than the physis ✓ (UBC and GCT not wider than physis)

MRI / CTmultiple fluid–fluid levels (best on axial/sagittal T2) · may expand into soft tissue

Fluid levels ≠ diagnosticalso in GCT, chondroblastoma, telangiectatic OS, vascular tumours (TO) · primary ABC = fluid levels throughout, no solid component (TO)

Spine X-ray"winking owl" sign (loss of pedicle) → order MRI
✗ Wrong options: scoliosis curve alone · Scheuermann kyphosis alone

Histology

Lakes of bloodcavernous blood-filled spaces without endothelial lining

Cyst wallsbenign giant cells + spindle cells · thin strands of woven bone · histiocytes, hemosiderin · villous projections, clefts

Biopsyrequired to confirm (needle biopsy acceptable)

Differential

Bubbly lytic X-ray Lakes of blood (histology) Curettage + grafting
ABC ✓ ✓ ✓
UBC ✓ — —
NOF ✓ — —
GCT — — ✓
Chondroblastoma (epiphyseal) — — ✓
Chondromyxoid fibroma — — ✓
Osteoblastoma — — ✓
Telangiectatic OS — ✓ —

5Management

Treatment algorithm

Aneurysmal bone cyst
  • Acute pathologic fracture→NONOPERATIVE FRACTURE CARE
    • Healed, cyst persists/grows→CURETTAGE + GRAFT
  • Symptomatic, no fracture→EXTENDED CURETTAGE + ADJUVANT + GRAFT
  • Expendable bone (fibula, rib)→RESECTION
  • Spine with neural compression→MARGINAL RESECTION
    (avoid wide resection — risks permanent neuro injury)

Curettage & adjuvants

Standardextended (aggressive) curettage + bone grafting (allograft / substitute) (TO — FNB 2019)
✗ Wrong options: aspiration + methylprednisolone · curettage + cement · aspiration + autograft

Adjuvantsphenol, liquid nitrogen, argon beam, high-speed burr, hydrogen peroxide, bone cement → kill residual cells, ↓ recurrence

Sclerotherapy3% polidocanol via J-needle (aspirate, break septa, scrape wall) → "current standard of care, surpassing extended curettage" (TO) · OB: curettage + burr + adjuvant or polidocanol both suitable (>90% cure) — read the options

Intralesional calcitonin + methylprednisolonehigh healing rates reported (TO)

Embolizationspine and pelvis (limited access) (TO) · preop → ↓ blood loss · alone may resolve lesion

Radionuclide ablationevidence for axial ABCs

Proximal femur, painfulcurettage + grafting (structural support)
✗ Wrong options: observation → fracture risk · denosumab (GCT only) · proximal femoral replacement

Not used

Chemotherapyno role

Radiationseldom used · malignant transformation, damage to physes, gonads, cord

Phenol alonenot used (adjuvant only) · RFA / observation — not for this (osteoid osteoma)

Wide resection + chemonot for this (osteosarcoma) · wide/en bloc resection nearly eliminates recurrence but sacrifices function → reserve for expendable bones

6Complications

Recurrence risk factors

Local recurrence5–50% · up to 25% (Orthobullets) · spine 25–30%

Risk factors✓ young age, ✓ open physes, stage, type of removal / margin

Not a factorchemo, radiation sensitivity, graft material, tumour necrosis (OS prognosis), giant cells, embolization

X vs Y

ABC UBC Telangiectatic OS GCT
Age <20 Child, open physis Adolescent Skeletally mature 20–40
Site Eccentric metaphysis Central metaphysis, prox humerus / femur, calcaneus Metaphysis Epiphysis–metaphysis
Width Wider than physis Not wider than physis — Not wider than physis
MRI Fluid levels, no solid part Single fluid, no levels Fluid levels + solid nodular part ± secondary ABC
Histology Lakes of blood, benign giant cells Thin fibrous lining, giant cells, hemosiderin Lakes of blood + pleomorphic malignant cells, osteoid Giant-cell nuclei = stromal nuclei
USP6 Yes No No No
Treatment Curettage + adjuvant + graft Steroid injection / observe Chemo + wide resection Curettage + adjuvant · denosumab

How it's asked

"Expansile eccentric metaphyseal lesion, fluid–fluid levels, lakes of blood — diagnosis?"ABC
✗ Wrong options: GCT despite giant cells · UBC · NOF

"Gene in ABC aetiology?"USP6 (17p13)
✗ Wrong options: ASPL · ATF1 · EWS · PAX3

"True of primary ABC?"USP6 rearrangement on chromosome 17 (TO)
✗ Wrong options: posterior vertebral body · fluid levels confirm diagnosis

"Management of ABC (FNB)?"extended curettage + bone grafting (TO)
✗ Wrong options: aspiration + methylprednisolone

"ABC with acute pathologic fracture — next step?"nonoperative fracture management
✗ Wrong options: ORIF · open biopsy

"Spinal ABC with cauda equina in a child?"marginal resection
✗ Wrong options: wide resection · aspiration + steroid

"Large ABC of the fibula?"resection alone
✗ Wrong options: + chemo/radiation

"Pubic ramus ABC treatment?"surgery alone (± embolization)

"Factor associated with local recurrence?"open physes / young age
✗ Wrong options: chemo response · graft type

"Spine X-ray needing MRI?"winking owl sign
✗ Wrong options: scoliosis curve · Scheuermann

"Curettage + grafting is NOT used for?"osteofibrous dysplasia (✓ for GCT, ABC, chondroblastoma, CMF)

Angiosarcoma

Pathology · 3 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Demographicselderly · male > female

Originmalignant, aggressive tumour of vascular endothelium

Bone involvement<10% · of those, 60% long bones

Risk factors

Classic associationschronic vascular stasis (lymphoedema) · trauma · polyvinyl chloride (PVC) exposure

Genes & lab markers

CD31endothelial marker + · sensitive for angiosarcoma

3Presentation

Symptomsinsidious pain

Hallmark signoverlying skin changes (central necrosis) · hand: nail-fold lesions + multiple palmar masses

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raybone invasion · eccentric, purely lytic, metaphyseal/diaphyseal · no matrix

MRIstudy of choice for the soft-tissue mass

Angiographydiffuse multifocal involvement

CT chestlung metastases

Histology

Patternmalignant cells lining vascular channels · variable anaplasia · abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, large nuclei, mitoses

5Management

Wide surgical resectiontreatment of choice · relatively insensitive to chemo + radiation (Orthobullets summary: resection with radiation)

Amputationfor local control of a very aggressive tumour · forequarter amputation reported for the upper limb

Adjunctsradiation ± chemotherapy depending on extent/metastasis

6Complications

Prognosispoor · high local failure → amputation often needed

Spreadlymphatic spread (unusual for sarcoma) · lung metastases common

How it's asked

"PVC exposure, nail-fold + palmar lesions, lung mets, CD31 + — diagnosis?"angiosarcoma
✗ Wrong options: epithelioid sarcoma · synovial sarcoma · chondrosarcoma · osteosarcoma

"Risk factors?"chronic lymphoedema / vascular stasis, PVC, trauma

"Endothelial immunomarker?"CD31

Biopsy Principles

Pathology · 4 / 66

1Basics

Purpose & prerequisites

Purposeconfirm a suspected diagnosis · not a substitute for history, exam, labs, imaging

PrerequisitesCBC, platelets, coagulation · cross-sectional imaging of local anatomy · staging before biopsy (new lesion + prior cancer → CT chest/abdomen/pelvis + bone scan)

Whothe treating (definitive) surgeon / centre capable of diagnosis + treatment · biopsy tract resected in continuity with tumour

Golden ruleall tissue exposed, and any haematoma, is contaminated → must be resected at definitive surgery

4Diagnosis & Classification

Indications for biopsy (criteria)

Biopsy indicated Biopsy not needed
Aggressive bone or soft-tissue lesion Asymptomatic latent or symptomatic active bone lesion that looks entirely benign
Soft tissue >5 cm, deep to fascia, or over bone / NV structures Soft-tissue lesion completely benign on MRI (lipoma, haemangioma)
Unclear diagnosis in a symptomatic patient · indeterminate MRI
Solitary bone lesion with history of carcinoma

Types of biopsy

Type Specimen Use / fact
Fine needle aspiration (FNA/FNAC) Cytology only (separated cells) · no architecture or stroma Carcinoma · not sarcoma · no role in bone tumours (paucicellular, no IHC, done by pathologist unaware of incisions) (TO) · high false-negative
Core (Tru-cut) Cytology + stroma / architecture Soft-tissue sarcoma · 85–95% accurate · CT-guided core increasingly first-line
Incisional (open) Large sample Lowest non-diagnostic rate · after non-diagnostic core
Excisional Whole lesion Small (<3 cm), superficial (to fascia) soft-tissue masses only

Open vs needleopen → more contamination → higher local recurrence after definitive surgery (TO)

Never divide the sampleheterogeneous tumours → discordant grades · for second opinion, review slides/blocks (TO)

Frozen sectionconfirms adequate viable tissue · definitive diagnosis not required at that time · send sample for culture too

5Management

Open incisional biopsy technique

Step Do Don't
Incision Longitudinal in extremity, in line with future resection, no larger than needed Not transverse
Plane Intramuscular — through one muscle belly / involved compartment · biopsy the soft-tissue mass of a bone lesion Avoid intermuscular / internervous planes (contaminate 2 compartments) · do not raise flaps (TO)
Structures Stay away from NV bundle Do not expose neurovascular structures, violate joint or go through quadriceps / rectus femoris (morbid resection)
Bone Oval cortical window
Tourniquet May be used · release before closure No compressive exsanguination
Haemostasis Meticulous · close pseudocapsule Haematoma = contaminated
Drain Exit in line with incision, close to it No multiple drains, no drain away from incision

Biopsy approach by site

Site Approach
Distal femur Through vastus medialis or vastus lateralis (posteromedial/posterolateral border) — medial or lateral acceptable
Proximal humerus Anterior deltoid (not deltopectoral)
Distal humerus Lateral triceps / brachialis
Wrist Dorsal 2nd compartment
Iliac wing Through superior abductors
Supra-acetabular Smith-Petersen through TFL / rectus femoris
Femoral head / neck Transtrochanteric osseous approach
Tibia Subcutaneous border
Posterior thigh mass Direct posterior, drain in line

Non-diagnostic biopsy & referral

Suspected sarcoma / aggressive lesion
  • Community hospital→REFER TO ORTHOPAEDIC ONCOLOGIST before biopsy
  • Deep soft-tissue mass→CORE NEEDLE (CT-guided) ± CULTURE
    • Non-diagnostic (necrosis)→OPEN BIOPSY, same intramuscular tract
  • New bone lesion + prior cancer→RESTAGE, then BIOPSY
    (no nail / hemiarthroplasty / radiation before tissue diagnosis)

Repeat coreallowed but not in a new direction (contaminates another compartment)

Chronic destructive monoarthritis (endemic TB region) — open biopsy of joint + bone for histology (granulomas) + AFB/fungal culture
✗ Wrong options: empirical antibiotics · steroid trial · fusion first

Expanding mass with fluid + solid partsthink sarcoma, not haematoma → core biopsy
✗ Wrong options: compression · IR drain · excise first

6Complications

Poorly performed biopsy consequences

Bad biopsymultiple tracts + haematoma spreading through compartments → limb salvage impossible → chemotherapy + amputation (osteosarcoma)

Radiationno: osteosarcoma is radio-resistant

How it's asked

"True statement about bone tumour biopsy?"open biopsy → higher local recurrence than needle (TO)
✗ Wrong options: divide sample · raise flaps · FNAC preferred

"Important factor in a proper biopsy?"longitudinal incision
✗ Wrong options: intermuscular planes · multiple drains · expose NV structures · avoid tourniquet

"Principle that does NOT apply?""tourniquet should not be used" — tourniquet allowed, no exsanguination

"Distal femur biopsy approach?"through vastus medialis or lateralis (intramuscular)
✗ Wrong options: rectus femoris · VM–RF interval · VL–biceps interval · posterior

"Lateral distal femur lesion?"lateral longitudinal intramuscular
✗ Wrong options: intermuscular · arthroscopic

"Proximal calf deep mass?"longitudinal incision through the involved (medial) compartment

"Where to put the drain?"in line with the incision

"Core biopsy shows necrosis only — next?"open biopsy through the same lateral intramuscular tract
✗ Wrong options: FNA · core in new direction · resection

"Lung-cancer survivor, new subtrochanteric lesion?"biopsy
✗ Wrong options: prophylactic nail · radiation

"Destructive humeral lesion at a community hospital?"refer to orthopaedic oncologist
✗ Wrong options: biopsy there

"Painless growing thigh mass, sarcoma-like MRI — initial management?"CT-guided core needle biopsy (+ CT chest/abdomen/pelvis)

"High-grade OS after two outside biopsies + leg haematoma?"chemotherapy + amputation

Bone Infarct

Pathology · 5 / 66

1Basics

Pathophysiology

Spectrumsame disease spectrum as osteonecrosis · medullary lesion

Mechanisminterruption of blood supply by intrinsic/extrinsic factors (atherosclerosis, arteritis, thrombosis)

Sitemetaphysis or diaphysis of long bones (femur, tibia)

Healingprogressive but incomplete resorptive replacement → thick calcified fibro-osseous wall

Risk factors

Risk factor Bone infarct?
Sickle cell disease ✓
Thalassemia ✓
Gaucher disease ✓
Steroid use (chronic prednisone) ✓
Connective tissue disorders ✓
Trauma ✓
Infection not a risk factor

3Presentation

Usually asymptomaticincidental on films taken for other reasons (twisted knee, fall)

Painif painful → seek another aetiology

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raymedullary sheet-like central lucency + surrounding sclerosis with serpiginous border → "smoke up the chimney"

MRIkey: central signal = normal marrow · T1 peripheral low-signal rim (granulation tissue, sclerosis), may enhance · T2 double line: inner bright granulation ring + outer dark sclerotic ring · acute infarct ill-defined ↑ T2

Bone scancold early · hot in late resorptive / revascularisation phase

Biopsyusually unnecessary · mineralised necrotic marrow

5Management

Observationasymptomatic, no treatment required

6Complications

Malignant transformationrare · MFH / undifferentiated pleomorphic sarcoma at infarct site

X vs Y

Bone infarct Enchondroma
Matrix Serpiginous peripheral sclerotic rim, "smoke up the chimney" Rings and arcs (chondroid) throughout
MRI Central normal marrow fat Lobulated T2-bright cartilage
Risk factors Sickle, Gaucher, steroids None

How it's asked

"Incidental medullary metaphyseal lesion, serpiginous sclerotic border — diagnosis?"bone infarct
✗ Wrong options: enchondroma · metastasis · osteosarcoma

"NOT a risk factor for this lesion?"infection (✓ sickle cell, thalassemia, Gaucher, chronic steroids)

"Treatment?"observation

Bone Tumor Staging Systems

Pathology · 6 / 66

1Basics

Prognostic variables

Prognosis depends onoverall stage · metastasis (incl. skip / discontinuous lesions in same bone) · histologic grade · tumour size · tumour type

Two systemsMSTS (Enneking) — most useful to orthopaedic surgeons · AJCC — adds size, depth, nodes

Enneking limitationonly for tumours of mesenchymal origin

Compartment & grade

Intracompartmental (T1)bone tumour confined within the cortex

Extracompartmental (T2)extends beyond the cortex

Gradeby % cellular atypia · low grade = low metastatic potential (parosteal OS) · high grade = intramedullary OS, Ewing, dedifferentiated chondrosarcoma

4Diagnosis & Classification

MSTS (Enneking) — malignant (Roman numerals)

Stage Grade Site Metastasis
IA Low T1 intracompartmental M0
IB Low T2 extracompartmental M0
IIA High T1 intracompartmental M0
IIB High T2 extracompartmental M0
III Any T1 or T2 M1 (regional or distant)

Commonest presenting stageIIB (e.g. high-grade OS with soft-tissue extension, no mets)

MSTS (Enneking) — benign (Arabic numerals)

Stage Behaviour Examples
1 Latent NOF, enchondroma
2 Active ABC, UBC, chondromyxoid fibroma, chondroblastoma · GCT without cortical breakthrough
3 Aggressive GCT (cortical breakthrough, soft-tissue extension)

AJCC — bone sarcoma

Stage Grade Size / feature Metastasis 5-yr survival (Orthobullets)
IA Low ≤8 cm None 98%
IB Low >8 cm None —
IIA High ≤8 cm None 82%
IIB High >8 cm None 82%
III Any Skip (discontinuous) lesions in same bone None 52%
IVA Any Any Lung —
IVB Any Any Non-pulmonary (other bone) or nodes 30%

AJCC — soft-tissue prognosis factors

Gradelow G1–G2 · high G3–G4

SizeT1 <8 cm · T2 >8 cm (as listed in Orthobullets)

NodesN0 / N1 · distant mets → automatically stage IV

5Management

Limb salvage principles & contraindications

Three strikes rule4 components of a viable limb: bone, nerves, vessels, soft-tissue envelope · 1–2 resected → salvageable · 3 involved → salvage not worth considering (TO)

Barriers (relative, not absolute)poorly placed biopsy · major vascular involvement · encasement of major motor nerve · pathological fracture (TO)

Two of four components involvednot a contraindication (TO)

Nerves sacrificed during proximal humerus resection (axillary + radial) — continue with endoprosthesis (TO) · axillary usually sacrificed anyway · radial → later tendon transfers (or repair same sitting) · amputation indicated for intra-op tumour spillage, not nerve loss

How it's asked

"High-grade distal femur OS with soft-tissue extension, no mets — MSTS stage?"IIB

"Enneking IIB means?"high grade, extracompartmental, no metastases

"Most common presenting stage — which patient?"high-grade OS extending into soft tissue, no mets
✗ Wrong options: confined chondrosarcoma = IIA · lung mets = III

"OS distal femur + biopsy-proven contralateral femur lesion — AJCC?"IVB (non-pulmonary metastasis)
✗ Wrong options: III = skip lesion in same bone

"GCT without cortical breakthrough — Enneking?"2 (benign active)
✗ Wrong options: Roman numerals = malignant

"Limb salvage contraindication — all EXCEPT?"two of four components involved (✓ bad biopsy, ✓ path fracture, ✓ motor nerve encasement) (TO)

"Axillary + radial nerve sacrificed during humeral OS resection — next?"proceed with planned endoprosthesis (TO)
✗ Wrong options: amputation

Chemotherapy

Pathology · 7 / 66

1Basics

Apoptosis mechanism

Primary mechanismconventional chemo kills cancer cells by apoptosis (programmed cell death) · targets rapidly dividing cells

Side effectsgut lining, bone marrow, hair, skin (also rapidly dividing)

Targeted agentstyrosine kinase inhibitors: imatinib (CML) · gefitinib (EGFR; lung, breast) · erlotinib (EGFR; NSCLC, pancreas)

Roleeliminates lung micrometastases

Therapy Mechanism
Doxorubicin neoadjuvant chemo ✓ Apoptosis (blocks topoisomerase II, DNA/RNA synthesis)
Phenol in GCT bed Lysis of residual cells (not apoptosis)
TNF-α inhibitor (RA) Immune modulation
Colchicine (gout) Immune / crystal inhibition
Vancomycin Cell-wall synthesis inhibition

Genes & lab markers

MDR (multi-drug resistance) genepumps drug out of cell → very poor prognosis · in 25% primary, 50% metastatic lesions

Necrosis >98% after chemo — good prognostic sign (osteosarcoma)

5Management

Indications

Chemo is integral (with surgery)osteosarcoma (intramedullary + periosteal) · Ewing / PNET · MFH (UPS) · dedifferentiated chondrosarcoma

Controversialsoft-tissue sarcoma

Schedulepreop 8–12 weeks → surgery → maintenance 6–12 months

Agents, toxicities & antidotes

Agent Dose-limiting / classic toxicity
Doxorubicin (Adriamycin) ✓ Cardiomyopathy / CHF (cumulative dose) · ~7%
Ifosfamide Encephalopathy (neurotoxicity)
Cyclophosphamide Myelosuppression, urotoxicity
Bleomycin Pulmonary fibrosis

Doxorubicinanthracycline antibiotic, cytostatic · delayed LV wall thinning → dilated cardiomyopathy · dexrazoxane protective

Extravasated agent Antidote
Mechlorethamine / cisplatin Sodium thiosulfate
Doxorubicin / epirubicin Dexrazoxane
Vinca alkaloids (vincristine, vinblastine) Hot compress + hyaluronidase
Other vesicants Cold compress

How it's asked

"Major (dose-limiting) side effect of doxorubicin?"cardiomyopathy / cardiac toxicity
✗ Wrong options: pulmonary fibrosis (bleomycin) · myelosuppression/urotoxicity (cyclophosphamide) · encephalopathy (ifosfamide) · hearing loss · neuropathy

"Pharmacologically induced apoptosis occurs with?"doxorubicin neoadjuvant chemotherapy
✗ Wrong options: phenol in GCT · anti-TNF · colchicine · vancomycin

"True of apoptosis?"primary mechanism by which chemotherapy kills cancer cells

"Drug protecting against doxorubicin cardiotoxicity?"dexrazoxane

Chondroblastoma

Pathology · 8 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rare~1% of osseous neoplasms · M:F 2:1

Age80% <25 yr · 10–25 typical (2nd decade) · average diagnosis 19–23 · rare after physeal closure

Synonym"Codman tumour" (TO)

Siteepiphysis of immature long bones · may cross the physis into the metaphysis · knee (distal femur, proximal tibia) commonest → proximal humerus distant third · proximal femur
✗ Wrong options: metaphysis (TO)

Epiphyseal equivalentsapophysis (greater tuberosity, greater trochanter) · patella · talus, calcaneus · flat bones

Mnemonic "ECG"Epiphysis: Chondroblastoma, Giant cell tumour (+ clear cell chondrosarcoma) (TO)

Pathophysiology

Benign, locally destructive proliferation of immature cartilage cells (chondroblasts) · from epiphyseal plate / secondary ossification centre (controversial)

Matrixchondroid matrix · hyaline cartilage rarely produced

Natural historyprogressive bone destruction · does not heal spontaneously

Secondary ABCup to 1/3 (15–32% on imaging)

Genes & lab markers

Geneticshistone H3.3 mutations · chromosome 5 and 8 abnormalities

IHCS-100 + · SOX9 + · vimentin, neuron-specific enolase + (GCT, ABC: no S-100)

BloodsCBC, ESR, LDH normal (vs osteomyelitis, Ewing)

3Presentation

Symptomsprogressive pain (often night pain, not relieved by NSAIDs) · joint stiffness · limp

Signslocal muscle atrophy · tenderness · ↓ ROM · effusion

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray viewsAP, lateral, oblique · chest radiograph (lung mets) at diagnosis

X-raygeographic, well-circumscribed round/oval lytic epiphyseal lesion with thin sclerotic rim · may cross physis · 1–4 cm, lobular · expansile cortical remodelling · metaphyseal periosteal reaction (no periosteum over epiphysis) · effusion

Calcificationstippled matrix calcification, often only on CT (25–45%)

MRIextensive bone-marrow + soft-tissue oedema around the lesion — distinguishing feature (TO) · T1 intermediate · T2 heterogeneous · fluid levels = secondary ABC · oedema within treated lesion → recurrence

Bone scannot required · ↑ uptake, especially adjacent uninvolved bone

CT chestif chest film suspicious

Soap bubbleclassically GCT, but chondroblastoma also in DD (TO) · DD: ABC, GCT, chondroblastoma, enchondroma, CMF, fibrous dysplasia, ameloblastoma, plasmacytoma, chordoma

Benign lesions with marrow oedema (TO)osteoid osteoma, osteoblastoma, chondroblastoma, LCH
✗ Wrong options: osteochondroma · enchondroma · GCT

Histology

Cellssheets of polygonal mononuclear chondroblasts, well-defined borders · "cobblestone" arrangement

Nucleilarge, central, longitudinal groove → "coffee-bean" · scarce mitoses

Calcificationfine pericellular "chicken-wire" dystrophic calcification

Alsoscattered multinucleated osteoclast-type giant cells (almost always) · islands of amorphous chondroid matrix

Recurrencemay show atypia → not malignant transformation

Differential of an epiphyseal lesion

Lesion Key differentiating finding
Chondroblastoma Skeletally immature, progressive pain, matrix mineralization, extensive peritumoral oedema
Giant cell tumour Skeletally mature, 20–40, closed physes · starts in metaphysis → epiphysis · pathologic fracture common · giant-cell nuclei = stromal nuclei · S-100 negative
Clear cell chondrosarcoma 3rd–5th decade, >4 cm, less oedema, malignant · clear cells
Brodie abscess Immature, often metaphyseal · penumbra sign on MRI · neutrophils
Intraosseous ganglion Middle age · no periosteal reaction · cystic, less oedema
Eosinophilic granuloma Variable appearance
Brown tumour >50, CKD / hyperparathyroidism
ABC <20, multicystic, fluid levels · not typically epiphyseal
Osteoblastoma 2nd–3rd decade, rarely epiphyseal, mineralization, oedema
Osteoid osteoma Diaphyseal, NSAID-relieved night pain, nidus on CT
TB arthritis Epiphyseal possible · granulomas

5Management

Treatment algorithm

Chondroblastoma (does not heal spontaneously)
  • Most lesions→EXTENDED INTRALESIONAL CURETTAGE + ADJUVANT + GRAFT
  • Small (~1 cm) lesion→RADIOFREQUENCY ABLATION
  • Rib / aggressive pelvic lesion with soft-tissue extension→EN BLOC RESECTION
  • Destructive aggressive recurrence (rare)→ENDOPROSTHESIS / AMPUTATION
  • Lung metastases
    • Solitary / oligometastases→RESECTION
    • Numerous→DENOSUMAB

Curettage & bone grafting

Standard of careintralesional curettage + local adjuvant + bone graft (or PMMA)

Approachthrough cortical window · extend with high-speed burr · spare the physis if possible

Adjuvantsphenol, hydrogen peroxide, argon beam, cryotherapy / liquid nitrogen

Graftfill cavity · structural osteochondral graft for weight-bearing surfaces

RFAfirst-line option for small lesions (~1 cm) (TO) · recurrence ≈ curettage · success on MRI = necrosis beyond margin + resolution of oedema

Not indicatedobservation (no medical therapy, no spontaneous healing) · denosumab (GCT) · chemo · radiation · wide resection (non-expendable bone) · steroid injection · TKA

6Complications

Local recurrence10–15% (OB review 14–18%) · surgery and RFA alike · purely epiphyseal lesions recur more · due to inadequate resection · surveillance X-rays 5 yr

Limb length discrepancyphyseal damage by tumour or treatment → contralateral epiphysiodesis vs lengthening

Degenerative joint diseasesubchondral/cartilage damage → THA/TKA in adulthood

Lung metastasis — chest radiograph

"Benign" pulmonary metastasis<1% (Orthobullets) · 2% (one OB explanation) · like GCT · indolent → resect

Chest X-raythe benign lesion that needs a chest film at screening
✗ Wrong options: enchondroma · NOF · osteochondroma · UBC

Malignant transformationexceedingly rare · median age 52 · permeative growth, atypia, no osteoid · wide resection

X vs Y

Chondroblastoma GCT Clear cell chondrosarcoma
Age 10–25, open physis 20–40, closed physis 20–40 / 3rd–5th decade
Site Epiphysis (± crosses physis) Metaphysis → epiphysis, subchondral Epi-metaphysis (proximal femur/humerus)
MRI oedema Extensive Less Less
Histology Chondroblasts, coffee-bean nuclei, chicken-wire Ca Giant-cell nuclei = stromal nuclei Clear cells, malignant
Marker S-100, SOX9 H3F3A, RANKL —
Treatment Curettage + adjuvant + graft / RFA Curettage + adjuvant · denosumab Wide excision
Chondroblastoma Chondromyxoid fibroma
Site Epiphysis Metaphysis (anterior proximal tibia)
Histology Polygonal chondroblasts, chicken-wire Ca Stellate cells in chondroid/myxoid/fibrous lobules

How it's asked

"Statement NOT true of chondroblastoma?""commonly involves metaphysis" (✓ Codman tumour, ✓ RFA for small lesions, ✓ chicken-wire Ca) (TO)

"15-yr-old, epiphyseal proximal tibial soap-bubble lesion?"chondroblastoma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: GCT (20–40) · CMF (metaphyseal) · osteoid osteoma (diaphyseal)

"Benign tumour with extensive surrounding marrow/soft-tissue oedema on MRI?"chondroblastoma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: GCT · enchondroma · osteochondroma

"Adolescent, epiphyseal lytic lesion, S-100 +, normal bloods — histology?"chondroblasts with chicken-wire calcification
✗ Wrong options: GCT · ABC · Ewing · osteomyelitis

"16-yr-old, medial heel / calcaneal epiphyseal-equivalent lesion?"chondroblastoma
✗ Wrong options: GCT · CMF (stellate cells) · osteoblastoma

"18-yr-old, greater tuberosity lesion?"apophyseal chondroblastoma

"Best treatment?"extended intralesional curettage + bone graft ± adjuvant
✗ Wrong options: denosumab · chemo · observation · radiation · wide resection · TKA

"Benign lesion requiring chest radiograph at initial work-up?"chondroblastoma
✗ Wrong options: enchondroma · NOF · osteochondroma · UBC

"Recurrence rate after curettage?"10–15% · higher if purely epiphyseal

Chondromyxoid Fibroma

Pathology · 9 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rare<1% of bone tumours · benign but painful, locally aggressive

Demographicsmales · 2nd–3rd decades (10–30) · up to 75 yr

Sitemetaphysis of long bones · proximal tibia commonest (around knee: proximal tibia, proximal fibula, distal femur) · pelvis (ilium) · foot/ankle, hands, wrist (distal radius)

Originmay arise from physeal remnants

Genes & lab markers

Chromosome 6 (q13)genetic rearrangement

3Presentation

Historylong-standing pain, months–years · may be incidental

Symptomspain + mild swelling · activity pain → rest pain

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raylytic, eccentric, metaphyseal · sharply demarcated, narrow zone of transition · scalloped sclerotic rim · lobulated / bubbly · cortical expansion · calcification rare · 2–10 cm

vs NOFradiographically often indistinguishable · CMF larger and active past skeletal maturity

MRIT1 low, T2 high, lobular growth · distinct margin

CTlow attenuation, peripheral bone rim, subtle matrix

Bone scan↑ uptake

Histology (diagnostic criteria)

Histology mandatory for diagnosis

Low power — biphasichypercellular lobules of fibromyxoid tissue (spindle/stellate cells, hyperchromatic nuclei; giant cells + fibrovascular tissue between lobules) + hypocellular chondroid areas

High powermyxoid stroma with stellate cells · may have pleomorphic bizarre nuclei

Differential

Radiographic DD Histologic DD
ABC · chondroblastoma · NOF Chondroblastoma · enchondroma · chondrosarcoma
Lesion Distinguishing feature
CMF Stellate cells, spindle cells, myxoid-cartilaginous matrix
NOF Storiform bland fibroblasts, scattered giant cells
Enchondroma Calcifications · bland hyaline cartilage, pyknotic nuclei
GCT Radiolucent, not bubbly · giant-cell nuclei = stromal nuclei
Chondrosarcoma (conventional) Older (50+) · uncommon in hands/feet · cortical erosion, soft-tissue mass
Atypical cartilaginous tumour (= grade 1 extremity chondrosarcoma, WHO) · like large enchondroma + endosteal scalloping

5Management

Intralesional curettage + bone grafting (or PMMA)mainstay

Not indicatedobservation (painful) · chemo + wide excision · radiotherapy · radical resection

6Complications

Local recurrence~25% (Orthobullets) · 20–30% · 20–25% (OB)

Negative prognostic factorschildren · more lobulated, abundant myxoid tumour

Metastasisnot reported

X vs Y

Site Metaphysis (proximal tibia) Epiphysis
Histology Stellate cells, lobules of fibromyxoid tissue Polygonal chondroblasts, chicken-wire Ca
Calcification Rare Common (CT)
Lung mets None reported <1%

How it's asked

"Adolescent wrist/foot pain, eccentric lytic metaphyseal lesion with sclerotic rim, spindle/stellate cells in myxoid matrix?"chondromyxoid fibroma
✗ Wrong options: NOF (storiform) · enchondroma · chondrosarcoma · osteosarcoma

"21-yr-old, large lobulated eccentric metaphyseal lesion with sclerotic rim?"CMF
✗ Wrong options: NOF · ABC · atypical cartilaginous tumour

✗ Wrong options: chemo + wide excision · radiotherapy

Chondrosarcoma

Pathology · 10 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rank2nd commonest malignant primary bone tumour (after OS) · commonest primary bone sarcoma in the US (OB) · commonest primary bone malignancy of the hand though rare there (TO)

Ageconventional 40–75, most >50 · peak primary 40–60, secondary 25–45 (TO) · slight male predominance

Sitepelvis (25%), proximal femur, distal femur, proximal humerus, scapula, ribs · proximal/axial · metaphyseal or diaphyseal (epiphyseal only = clear cell)

Location rulecartilage tumours of hands/feet mostly benign · flat bones (pelvis, scapula, ribs, sternum) malignant until proven otherwise · subperiosteal or joint-lining cartilage almost always benign (TO)

Primary vs secondary

Primarysporadic · conventional >90% (central intramedullary 99%, periosteal ~1%) · dedifferentiated <10% · clear cell <5% · mesenchymal <1%

Secondaryfrom pre-existing benign cartilage lesion · osteochondroma >80% of secondary · younger (3rd–4th decade) · usually low grade · ~40% of MHE secondaries in ilium · histologically = conventional

Malignant transformation risk

Precursor Orthobullets OB explanation TO
Solitary osteochondroma <1% 1% <1% (~1% lifetime)
Solitary enchondroma 1–9% 1% 1%
MHE 5–10% ~10% 1–10% · ~5% lifetime
Ollier disease 25–30% 30% 5–50% · ~25% by age 40
Maffucci syndrome >50% ~100% (highest) 25–100%

Maffuccimultiple enchondromas + soft-tissue haemangiomas (and lymphangiomas) · also pancreatic/hepatic adenocarcinoma, ovarian, glioma/astrocytoma

Axial/pelvic osteochondromamore likely to transform than peripheral

Other precursors (TO)synovial chondromatosis, CMF, periosteal chondroma, chondroblastoma, prior radiation, fibrous dysplasia, Paget (OS + chondrosarcoma) · not chronic osteomyelitis → squamous cell carcinoma (Marjolin)

Genes & lab markers

IDH1 / IDH2 mutations — primary + secondary conventional chondrosarcoma and enchondromas

HEY1–NCOA2 fusion — mesenchymal chondrosarcoma

t(12;22)listed for clear cell (Orthobullets) · clear cell: S-100 +, PAS + (glycogen → clear cytoplasm)

Telomerase activity ↑ (RT-PCR) — directly correlates with recurrence
✗ Wrong options: MHC II · gadolinium uptake · apoptosis · technetium uptake

3Presentation

Paincommonest · pain attributable to the lesion (night pain, not activity related) separates low-grade CS from enchondroma (vs pain from cuff tear / meniscus)

Pelvisslowly growing mass · bowel/bladder obstruction from mass effect

Secondarynew pain or growth of an osteochondroma after skeletal maturity

Dedifferentiatedpathologic fracture ~50%

4Diagnosis & Classification

Work-up & referral

Incidental chondroid lesion on MRI (meniscus work-up) — first get orthogonal radiographs → often simple enchondroma
✗ Wrong options: CT CAP · whole-limb MRI · core biopsy · curettage

Suspicious lesion at community hospitalrefer to orthopaedic oncologist
✗ Wrong options: observe · curettage · chemo/RT

StagingCT chest (lungs = main site) + MRI whole bone + bone scan
✗ Wrong options: CT abdomen/pelvis (bone sarcomas rarely go to viscera) · SPEP/UPEP (myeloma)

Unknown lytic lesion >40 yrCT chest/abdomen/pelvis (carcinoma work-up)

Biopsycore needle or open (gold standard) · target area of ↓ T2 signal (focus of dedifferentiation) · TO: needle biopsy not indicated for cartilage tumours (heterogeneous) · diagnosis = clinical + radiographic + histologic correlation

Key radiology findings

X-rayorthogonal views mandatory · usually >4 cm · lytic with "rings and arcs" / "popcorn" calcification (punctate, comma-shaped) · seen on X-ray ~70%

Aggressive signsendosteal scalloping >2/3 cortex · cortical destruction · periosteal reaction · bone expansion · soft-tissue mass · permeative borders · higher grade → less mineral, more lytic

CTbest for low-grade cartilage lesions (cortical involvement) · matrix seen more easily · deep endosteal scalloping → CS · 90% cortical breach

MRIlobulated, T1 low–intermediate, T2 high (cartilage water) with low-signal septa · "rings and arcs" septal enhancement · defines soft-tissue extent

Bone scanvery hot in all grades · uptake > ASIS characteristic (enchondroma < ASIS)

Secondary from osteochondromacartilage cap >2 cm (≥2 cm) on MRI/CT in a mature patient · lysis of calcification · cortical destruction · cap 14 mm, no growth, mild symptoms → serial imaging + exam
✗ Wrong options: FNA · chest CT · bone scan · wide excision

Enchondroma vs low-grade chondrosarcoma (diagnostic criteria)

Enchondroma Chondrosarcoma
Age <50 >50
Size <5 cm >5 cm
Site Hands/feet common · axial/pelvis rare Axial/pelvis common · hands/feet rare
Pain No (or other pathology) Yes, attributable to lesion
Soft-tissue mass No Yes
Cortical destruction No Yes
Periosteal reaction No Yes
Endosteal scalloping <2/3 cortex >2/3 cortex
Expansion No Yes
Bone scan < ASIS > ASIS

Histology & grading (conventional)

Graded bycellularity + cytological atypia (nuclear size, hyperchromasia), myxoid stroma · mitoses rare, not used (TO)

Diagnostic of malignancypermeation and entrapment of pre-existing bone trabeculae · hypercellularity, plump nuclei, >occasional binucleate cells

Any malignant osteoid→ chondroblastic osteosarcoma, not CS (TO)

Grade Share Histology Metastasis 5-yr survival
1 60% Like enchondroma · bland hyaline, low cellularity, binucleate · needs clinico-radiological correlation Almost never 90%
2 25% Plump bi-/tri-nucleated cells, ↑ cellularity, atypia, myxoid change 10–15% 60–70%
3 5% Hypercellular permeative stroma, little hyaline, myxoid, pleomorphic, mitoses · "blue balls" >50% 30–50%
Dedifferentiated ("4") ~10% Bimorphic High <20% (10% at 10 yr)

WHO 2020grade 1 extremity CS = atypical cartilaginous tumour (ACT) · axial cartilage tumours = separate, more aggressive category

Subtypes

Subtype Age Site Imaging Histology Treatment / prognosis
Conventional 40–75 Pelvis, prox femur, humerus Rings & arcs, scalloping Hyaline, graded 1–3 Wide resection alone
Dedifferentiated ~60 As conventional Bimorphic: aggressive unmineralized lytic mass next to mineralized chondroid · path fracture Low-grade cartilage abruptly next to high-grade spindle sarcoma (OS > fibrosarcoma > MFH) · not admixed Wide resection ± chemo · <20% 5-yr, 10% 10-yr
Clear cell 20–40 Epiphysis · 2/3 humeral/femoral head Well-defined lytic epi-metaphyseal, thin sclerotic rim, mixed lucent/sclerotic, no periosteal reaction · mimics chondroblastoma Large vacuolated clear cells, giant cells · ~50% low-grade CS areas · S-100, PAS + Wide resection (no chemo) · 10-yr ~80% · mets ~15%
Mesenchymal 20–40 Flat bones (ilium, ribs, skull) · 30% soft tissue Moth-eaten / permeative, extraosseous Small round blue cells + hyaline islands · staghorn (haemangiopericytomatous) vessels · HEY1–NCOA2 Wide resection + chemo ± RT · <50% 5-yr
Periosteal (juxtacortical) 2nd–4th decade Surface, metaphysis femur/humerus Juxtacortical mass, rings & arcs · cortex eroded/saucerized (vs periosteal chondroma) Low grade Wide resection · indolent
Secondary 3rd–4th decade Ilium (MHE) Cap >2 cm As conventional As primary · usually low grade

Differential of chondrosarcoma

Malignant, older patient Similar chondroid histology Sacral lesion, older Wide resection alone
Chondrosarcoma ✓ ✓ ✓ ✓
Metastasis · lymphoma · myeloma · MFH ✓ — ✓ —
Enchondroma / Ollier / Maffucci · periosteal chondroma · MHE — ✓ — —
Parosteal OS · adamantinoma — — — ✓
Chordoma — — ✓ ✓

Imaging distractorsbone infarct (smoke up the chimney, cold, asymptomatic) · fibrous dysplasia (ground glass) · osteosarcoma (younger, cloud-like bone) · plasmacytoma (uniformly T2 bright, cold scan) · Ewing (lytic, no mineral)

5Management

Treatment algorithm

Chondrosarcoma (chemo- and radio-resistant)
  • Grade 1 / ACT, extremity, confined to bone→EXTENDED CURETTAGE + ADJUVANT + GRAFT ± FIXATION
    • Asymptomatic, no aggressive features→OBSERVE
  • Grade 2–3, or ANY pelvic/axial lesion→WIDE RESECTION ALONE
  • Mesenchymal→WIDE RESECTION + CHEMO (± RT)
  • Dedifferentiated→WIDE RESECTION ± CHEMO
  • Surgically inaccessible→RADIOTHERAPY

Treatment — wide resection (chemo/radioresistant)

Why resistantlow mitotic rate, poor perfusion / low blood supply, chondroid/myxoid matrix limits drug diffusion, few cells in growth phase

Wide excisionbetter survival than marginal or intralesional · grade 2 distal femur → wide excision
✗ Wrong options: curettage · marginal · chemo first · hip disarticulation

Pelvic CSinternal hemipelvectomy or hindquarter amputation (✗ Wrong options: + chemo · + RT) · wide resection alone (✗ Wrong options: brachytherapy)

Proximal fibula CSwide resection + reconstruct LCL and biceps femoris (transfer to lateral tibia, staple) → prevents varus instability + flexion weakness
✗ Wrong options: curettage + liquid N₂ + cement (GCT) · denosumab (GCT) · + chemo (OS)

Ollier patient with CSwide resection only

Clear cell after "benign" curettagewide resection alone
✗ Wrong options: chemo · curettage · forequarter

Lung metastasesresect if possible (TO)

Low-grade lesion — extended curettage

ACT / grade 1 extremityextended intralesional curettage + high-speed burr + adjuvant + bone-void graft ± prophylactic fixation · same recurrence as wide resection, better function

Adjuvantscryotherapy / liquid nitrogen, argon beam, phenol, ethanol, hydrogen peroxide

Pelvis / axial grade 1wide excision (higher recurrence + metastasis, poor biopsy concordance)

Not for ACTneoadjuvant RT 50–60 Gy · proximal humeral resection + megaprosthesis (old standard) · forequarter amputation

Dedifferentiated chondrosarcoma

Treatmentwide resection + endoprosthetic reconstruction · chemo possible role (controversial) · RT palliative/adjuvant only

IM nail through dedifferentiated CSseeds entire femur → amputation
✗ Wrong options: proximal femoral replacement · chemo · RT

Curettage + cement + THA, histology dedifferentiatedresect whole bed → hemipelvectomy

Pathologic fracture + high-grade CSnext test CT chest

6Complications

Local recurrencegrade 1: rare after wide resection, 5–15% after curettage + adjuvant · grade 2: margin dependent · grade 3: 25% recurrence, >30% mets · high-grade recurrence → mets + ↓ survival

Prognostic factors

Most importanthistological grade (TO — FNB 2016)
✗ Wrong options: site · matrix calcification · age

Poor prognosishigh grade · axial / proximal (pelvis) · advanced age · inadequate margins · ↑ telomerase (recurrence)

Not prognosticgender · negative margins, younger age, tumour-centre biopsy = favourable

Spine/sacrum CSworse local control/survival with increasing age

How it's asked

✗ Wrong options: infarct · OS · Ewing · FD · plasmacytoma

✗ Wrong options: de novo · homogeneous · curettage · adolescents

✗ Wrong options: synovial sarcoma (keratin glands) · metastatic prostate · periosteal OS

"Dedifferentiated CS with path fracture — staging test?"CT chest
✗ Wrong options: CT CAP · SPEP/UPEP

✗ Wrong options: chondroblastoma · GCT

"Highest malignant risk?"Maffucci > Ollier > MHE > solitary lesions

"MHE — malignant histology?"low-grade chondrosarcoma (EXT1 worst)

"Secondary CS NOT seen in?"chronic osteomyelitis (→ SCC) (✓ Paget, ✓ synovial chondromatosis, ✓ osteochondroma) (TO)

"FALSE about CS (FNB 2024)?"source gives no answer · "clear cell CS is predominantly metaphyseal" is false (it is epiphyseal) (TO)

"Most important prognostic factor?"histological grade (TO)

"Poor prognostic factor in pelvic CS?"pelvic location
✗ Wrong options: female · negative margins · younger age

"Recurrence most directly related to?"↑ telomerase activity

"Incidental 'possible chondrosarcoma' on knee MRI, no images — next?"orthogonal radiographs

"Adult osteochondroma, 14 mm cap, stable?"serial imaging + exam (cap ≥2 cm = worry)

Chordoma

Pathology · 11 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rankcommonest primary malignant spinal tumour in adults · commonest primary malignant tumour of the sacrum (TO) · 1–4% of primary bone tumours

DemographicsM:F 3:1 · >50 yr (5th–6th decade) · middle-aged Caucasian men

Sitesacrum + coccyx 50% (S2–S3 predilection) · spheno-occipital (skull base) 35% · mobile spine 15%

Pathoanatomy

Originmalignant transformation of residual notochordal cells (same lineage as nucleus pulposus) → midline location

Behaviourslow-growing, locally aggressive, low-grade malignant · diagnosis often delayed

Genes & lab markers

Keratin (pan-cytokeratin) +separates from chondrosarcoma (keratin −)

EMA + (TO) · S-100 weakly +

3Presentation

Paininsidious low back / hip pain, worse sitting

Bowel/bladderconstipation, faecal incontinence, bladder change · UTI symptoms (1/3) · cauda equina 10% (TO)

Motor/sensory deficitrare (most lesions S1 or distal) · S1–S2 lesion may lose L5–S1 function · radiculopathy (sciatic) up to 1/3

Rectal exam>50% palpable

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayhard to see (overlying bowel gas) · large lytic lesion crossing the midline (>50%) → SI joints, ilium

CTmidline bone destruction + soft-tissue mass · calcifications in the soft-tissue component

MRIbright on T2 · soft-tissue extension

Biopsy & histology

Biopsy requiredMRI alone insufficient (lymphoma, chondrosarcoma mimic) · transrectal biopsy contraindicated

Grosslobular, gelatinous

Histologyphysaliferous cells: large, foamy, vacuolated (eosinophilic) cytoplasm, vesicular nucleus — pathognomonic · cords/lobules in mucoid matrix separated by fibrous septa · distinct nodules

Differential

Sacral lesion, older patient Keratin + Similar X-ray Wide resection alone
Chordoma Yes Yes Yes Yes
Chondrosarcoma Yes No Yes Yes
Metastatic carcinoma Yes Yes Yes —
Lymphoma of bone Yes — Yes —
Myeloma · MFH Yes — — —
Adamantinoma (tibia) — Yes — Yes
Synovial sarcoma · epithelioid sarcoma — Yes — —

Other sacral tumoursmyeloma, GCT, ABC, metastasis

5Management

Treatment & local recurrence

Standardwide-margin en bloc resection · sacrifice sacral roots to get margins · wide tumour-free margin ∝ tumour-free survival

Radiationadd if margin not achieved (positive margins) · inoperable tumours

TO answersacral chordoma → wide resection alone (TO) · chemo- and relatively radio-resistant; RT/SRS for recurrence
✗ Wrong options: + EBRT · + chemo · curettage + bisphosphonate

Mobile spineen bloc corpectomy / total vertebrectomy → best local control · needs anterior + posterior reconstruction

Not indicatedchemotherapy (chemoresistant) · debulking (intralesional) · RT alone · observation

Radiosurgery (CyberKnife)

Stereotactic radiosurgeryrecurrent/residual chordoma and chondrosarcoma after failed surgery + fractionated RT (TO)

CyberKnifeextracranial → sacral chordoma ✓ · Gamma knife — intracranial only (TO)

Proton–photon beammay help recurrence

Sacral nerve root sparing & function

Roots preserved / resected Bowel & bladder
Bilateral S3 preserved Near-normal (Orthobullets) · S3 most critical
Both S2 preserved (at least) Near-normal (OB)
Unilateral S2, S3, S4 (± S5) preserved Near-normal ✓ (OB answer: unilateral S2–S5)
Bilateral S4–S5 resected Near-normal (S2–S3 splanchnics kept)
Bilateral S2–S5 resected Complete loss
Bilateral S1 / L5 only Not enough

Pelvic splanchnic nervesS2–S4 (S3 predominance) → parasympathetic to pelvic viscera

6Complications

Local recurrence~50%, margin dependent → close surveillance
✗ Wrong options: nodal · liver · bone mets · malignant transformation

Metastasis30–50%, late (lung, rarely bone) · OB: 30–50% already metastatic at diagnosis

Survival60% 5-yr, 25% long-term (25–50%) · local extension may be fatal

Poor prognosis (TO)high histological grade · volume >10 cm / 200 cm³

Post-oploss of bowel/bladder function (root sacrifice)

Chordoma Chondrosarcoma
Origin Notochord, midline Cartilage, often eccentric (pelvis)
Cells Physaliferous Atypical chondrocytes in lacunae
Keratin / EMA + −
S-100 Weak + +
Treatment Wide resection ± RT (margin +) Wide resection

How it's asked

"Sacral lytic lesion, 58-yr-old, lobules with vacuolated physaliferous cells?"chordoma
✗ Wrong options: chondrosarcoma · metastatic RCC · CMF · GCT

"All true of sacral chordoma EXCEPT?""MRI is sufficient without biopsy" (✓ notochordal like nucleus pulposus, ✓ mets 30–50%, ✓ motor deficit rare, ✓ resection ± RT)

"Treatment of sacral chordoma?"wide resection alone (✗ Wrong options: + EBRT · + chemo) (TO) · OB: wide surgical excision (✗ Wrong options: chemo · debulking · RT alone)

"L5 en bloc corpectomy is right for which histology?"chordoma
✗ Wrong options: osteomyelitis · myeloma · LCH · lung adenocarcinoma

"Why surveillance after resection?"high local recurrence

"Keratin + and S-100 + sacral mass?"chordoma

"Tumour treated by CyberKnife?"chordoma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: vertebral osteoblastoma · sacral OS · GCT

"Near-normal bowel/bladder if which roots spared?"unilateral S2–S5 (or both S2 / bilateral S3)

"Bilateral S4–5 vs bilateral S2–5 resection?"S4–5 patient near-normal, S2–5 patient loses function

Conventional Intramedullary Osteosarcoma

Pathology · 12 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology & genetics

Rankmost common primary sarcoma of bone · commonest non-haematologic primary bone malignancy · ~20% of primary bone cancers (TO)

Commonest primary malignant tumour of bone overallmultiple myeloma (TO — FNB 2021)

Agebimodal: 1st peak 2nd decade (10–14), 75% <20 · 2nd peak >65 (secondary: Paget, infarcts, prior osteochondroma/osteoblastoma, radiation)

Sex / raceM:F 1.5:1 · ↑ Black, Hispanic

Sitemetaphysis of long bones (10% diaphyseal, <1% epiphyseal) · distal femur > proximal tibia > proximal humerus > axial (spine/sacrum) · fastest-growing physes

Childhood tumour facts (TO)commonest tumour of infancy / extracranial / abdominal: neuroblastoma · commonest solid tumour of childhood: brain tumour · commonest malignancy: leukaemia (ALL) · commonest benign bone lesion: NOF · commonest benign bone tumour: osteochondroma · commonest malignant bone tumour: osteosarcoma

Genes & lab markers

Association Gene / inheritance Other features
Hereditary retinoblastoma RB1 (13q14), germline · 500–1000× risk (TO) Somatic RB1 loss in 60–70% of sporadic OS
Li-Fraumeni TP53 (17p13), AD Breast, brain, adrenocortical cancer · somatic TP53 in 20–50%
Rothmund-Thomson RECQL4, AR Poikiloderma (sun-sensitive rash), alopecia, juvenile cataracts, dental anomalies, short stature · OS, fibrosarcoma, gastric adenoCa, BCC/SCC
Bloom BLM, AR UV facial rash, short stature · OS, leukaemia, lymphoma
Werner WRN, AR "Adult progeria" — premature ageing, cataracts, osteoporosis
Fibrous dysplasia — Polyostotic > monostotic

Pathogenesis (TO)RB1 + TP53 pathway inactivation nearly universal, via complex structural variants / chromothripsis · no pathognomonic translocation · amplification MDM2, CDK4, MYC · (✗ Wrong options: MDM2/CDK4 = primary driver of conventional OS (that is low-grade central / parosteal) · RECQL4 in sporadic elderly OS)
✗ Wrong options: like Ewing

Not OS genesEWS-FLI1 (Ewing) · EGFR, IGF-R · VEGF (angiogenesis; overexpression = poor prognosis)

Labs↑ ALP and ↑ LDH → aggressive disease, poor prognosis · very high LDH may mean mets

IHCSATB2 + (all OS subtypes) (TO)

3Presentation

Symptomspain + swelling, night/rest pain · often blamed on sports injury ("red herring" fall) · median 4 months to diagnosis · may have fever, weight loss

Exammass, tenderness, ↓ ROM, NV compression · pain with axial loading

Pathologic fracture~10%

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral, whole bone)metaphyseal medullary + cortical destruction · mixed blastic/lytic (may be purely either) · "sunburst" / "hair-on-end" · Codman triangle · cloud-like / fluffy osteoid mineral · soft-tissue mass · usually doesn't cross physis

MRI (± contrast, entire bone + joint above and below)soft-tissue + NV involvement · resection level · skip metastases (= stage III) · gold standard local study

CT chestrequired at presentation (lung mets)
✗ Wrong options: chest X-ray (less sensitive) · CT abdomen/pelvis (adult unknown-lesion work-up)

Bone scan (Tc-99m)very hot · bone mets, skip lesions · superior to PET in OS (PET better in Ewing) · PET-CT misses lung nodules <5–9 mm

FNB 2024 image (Codman triangle, distal femur)"least likely appearance?" — answer not given · onion peel (Ewing) and stippled calcification (chondroid) don't fit OS · sunburst + Codman do (TO)

Staging work-up

LocalMRI of whole involved bone (e.g. knee + tibia) · Systemic — CT chest + whole-body bone scan

Pre-chemo baselineCBC, BMP, LFTs, UA · echocardiogram (doxorubicin) + hearing screen (cisplatin)

Not neededbone marrow biopsy (Ewing) · skeletal survey (myeloma / NAT) · lymph node biopsy · PFTs · contralateral MRI · angiography · reactive-zone biopsy · EXT1/2 testing (MHE)

Biopsyrequired · by/after discussion with definitive surgeon · core needle preferred · sample peripheral soft-tissue part · (✗ Wrong options: FNA · biopsy by IR at outside hospital (path fracture → sling, analgesia, stage, refer) · empirical antibiotics without biopsy)
✗ Wrong options: necrotic centre · Codman triangle = reactive bone

Histology — diagnostic criteria

Two criteriatumour cells produce osteoid + stromal cells frankly malignant (atypia, high N:C, abnormal mitoses)

Hallmark"lacy" osteoid / woven bone laid down directly by pleomorphic spindle cells

Conventional subtype Matrix Mimic
Osteoblastic (commonest) Abundant lace-like osteoid, plasmacytoid cells (TO) —
Chondroblastic Malignant hyaline cartilage + focal osteoid Chondrosarcoma (any osteoid → OS)
Fibroblastic Herringbone/storiform collagen, sparse osteoid Fibrosarcoma, UPS
Giant-cell-rich Many giant cells GCT (no atypia/osteoid)

MSTS staging

CommonestIIB (high grade, extracompartmental, no mets) · 90–95% high grade, penetrate cortex early

Metastasis at presentation20–25% (Enneking III) · lung ~80% (haematogenous) > bone 16% · bone mets without lung mets rare (5%)

Skip metastasesintraosseous (same bone, usually proximal) or trans-articular · 2–6% (OB) · 10% (Orthobullets) · prognosis = lung mets

Osteosarcoma subtypes

Location Grade Subtype Key feature
Intramedullary High Conventional (this topic) Mixed tissues
High Telangiectatic Purely lytic, ABC-like blood lakes · malignant cells in septa
High Small cell OS + Ewing look · SATB2 +, CD99 − / focal, no EWSR1
Low Low-grade central (FD-like, desmoplastic-fibroma-like) Mimics fibrous dysplasia · MDM2/CDK4 amplification
Surface Low Parosteal Posterior distal femur, "stuck-on" · middle-aged females (3rd–4th decade) · MDM2/CDK4
Intermediate Periosteal Diaphysis femur/tibia · chondroblastic lobules, sunburst
High High-grade surface Like conventional, on surface · medullary involvement common
Intracortical — Rarest bony type —
Extraskeletal High Soft-tissue OS <5% · treat like STS

Differential

Lesion Imaging Histology
Osteosarcoma Mixed blastic/lytic, Codman, soft-tissue mass Lacy osteoid, malignant stroma
Osteomyelitis Lags 2 wk, lucency, sequestrum/involucrum Neutrophils (acute), lymphocytes (chronic)
Eosinophilic granuloma "Great mimicker", vertebra plana Langerhans cells, eosinophils, no atypia
Ewing Permeative, moth-eaten, onion skin Small round blue cells, t(11;22)
Dedifferentiated CS Bimorphic Cartilage + high-grade spindle
Fibrosarcoma Lytic, >55 yr Herringbone spindle cells
Lymphoma of bone Diaphyseal, moth-eaten, 35–55 yr Mixed round cells
Osteoblastoma Posterior spine, nidus Benign osteoblasts, giant cells
Myositis ossificans Mature periphery Mature bone, no atypia
UPS — Spindle cells, no osteoid

5Management

Treatment — neoadjuvant chemotherapy & wide resection

High-grade intramedullary OS (stage IIB)
  • First → NEOADJUVANT MAP CHEMO (8–12 wk)→RESTAGE
  • Resectable → WIDE RESECTION (limb salvage)→ADJUVANT CHEMO 6–12 mo
    • Positive margin→ADDITIONAL RESECTION ± RT
  • Child, distal femur / epiphysis / joint contaminated→ROTATIONPLASTY
  • Functional + disease-free limb impossible→AMPUTATION

Sequenceneoadjuvant chemo → wide resection → adjuvant chemo · surgery 3–4 wk after last preop dose · chemo restarts within ~2 wk of surgery
✗ Wrong options: surgery + RT · chemo + RT

MAPhigh-dose Methotrexate (leucovorin rescue) + Adriamycin (doxorubicin) + cisPlatin (US standard) · ± ifosfamide · 3-drug > 2-drug (AP) by ~10% survival despite toxicity (TO) · EURAMOS-1: adding ifosfamide + etoposide (MAPIE) for poor responders → no benefit
✗ Wrong options: vincristine/carboplatin regimens (Ewing)

Agent Mechanism Toxicity
Methotrexate Dihydrofolate reductase inhibitor Myelosuppression, mucositis
Doxorubicin Topoisomerase II inhibitor Cardiotoxicity → echo
Cisplatin Covalent DNA binding Renal failure, hearing loss, neurotoxicity → audiogram
Ifosfamide DNA alkylating Renal failure, haemorrhagic cystitis

Chemo effectivenesssame preop or postop (OB)

Radiationradio-resistant (NF-κB inhibits JNK-mediated apoptosis) · only palliation, unresectable sites, close margins (pelvis/spine) · ECRT — extracorporeal 50 Gy single fraction to sterilise and re-implant bone (TO) · extraskeletal OS radiosensitive (exception)
✗ Wrong options: curative for sacral OS (TO)

Pathologic fracturelimb salvage still possible if stabilised (ex-fix/cast) and good chemo response (healing, ↓ pain/swelling, ↑ ossification, loss of tumour blush on angiogram)

Limb salvage principles

Biopsy tractresected en bloc with specimen, never exposed (TO)
✗ Wrong options: routine sacrifice of popliteal vessels · keep capsule/pseudocapsule · cut at visible tumour edge

Bone cut2–3 cm beyond intramedullary extent on T1 MRI (TO) · marrow sampled at cut

Limb salvage achievable85–90% · survival = amputation

Contraindications (TO)NV bundle encasement · path fracture with massive haematoma contaminating compartments · no clean margin · infection

Amputation indicationsfunctional + disease-free limb impossible (only absolute) · NV encasement · growing on chemo next to NV bundle · bad biopsy · infected tumour

Physeal-sparingnot when tumour crosses physis (knee OS usually sacrifices articular surface) · transepiphyseal resection only if epiphysis free

Resection margins (Enneking)

Margin Plane
Intralesional Through tumour · tumour at marrow margin = intralesional
Marginal Through reactive zone / pseudocapsule
Wide Cuff of normal tissue 360° ("orange rind")
Radical Whole compartment (e.g. entire femur)

Rotationplasty

Indicationsdistal femur / knee / proximal tibia / hip sarcomas in young children · joint contamination · epiphyseal involvement · failed salvage with intact leg · PFFD · avoids LLD

Requirementfunctioning, uninvolved sciatic nerve

Techniqueresect segment, rotate leg 180°, fix tibia to femur · ankle → knee · gastrocsoleus = quadriceps, anterior compartment = hamstrings · keep sural branches

WinkelmannA (ankle = knee): AI distal femur, AII proximal tibia · B (knee = hip): BI proximal femur sparing hip, BII involving hip, BIII total femur

Outcomehighest self-reported function, fewest revisions in pre-adolescents · cosmesis is the drawback
✗ Wrong options: endoprosthesis (loosening, LLD) · allograft (fracture, infection) · AKA (energy cost)

Megaprosthesis outcomes

Modular endoprosthesisgold standard reconstruction · rotating hinge (ligaments sacrificed) · cemented if poor bone or early chemo · press-fit for long survivorship

Henderson failure types (TO)1 soft-tissue · 2 aseptic loosening · 3 structural · 4 infection (5–15%) · 5 tumour recurrence

Infection2–10% (Orthobullets) · 12–19% at 5–10 yr (proximal tibia) · risks op time ≥8 h, drain ≥14 d · ~50% → amputation

PARITY trial5-day ≠ better than 1-day antibiotics · flaps ↓ infection · NPWT → ↑ reoperation, not infection · longer op time → ↑ complications · primary malignant tumour → higher short-term reoperation than benign aggressive

Aseptic loosening~6% · commonest failure · tibia commonest mechanical failure

Proximal tibial EPR + medial gastrocnemius flapgood function (flexion 98–110°, lag <10°) but infection, extensor mechanism failure (3–17%), reoperation significant · patella alta up to 70%

Allograftfracture at host–graft junction, nonunion (chemo, RT, ECRT risk factors)

Extraskeletal osteosarcoma

vs conventional<5% of OS · older (5th–6th decade) · nearly always high grade · worse survival (~50% vs ~75%) · chemo unproven · radiation is part of standard treatment (wide resection + RT, like STS)

6Complications

Prognostic factors (necrosis, MDR, margins, metastasis)

Most importantstage / metastasis at diagnosis

Chemo response>90% necrosis = good (5-yr ~85%)
✗ Wrong options: 60% or 70% necrosis = poor

Huvos grade Necrosis Response
I 0–50% Poor
II 51–90% Poor (e.g. 88% → continue MAP)
III 91–99% Good
IV 100% Complete

Poor prognosismets · skip lesions · <90% necrosis · age >40 · male · axial/pelvic site · large size (≥5 cm) · MDR / P-glycoprotein (25% primary, 50% mets) · VEGF · ↑ ALP / ↑ LDH · vascular involvement · positive margins · radiation-induced or Paget OS

Not poor (TO)✓ >90% necrosis is good · urinary heat-shock protein, serum proteoglycan C, unchanged DNA ploidy listed as poor-prognosis distractors

Local recurrencemost directly surgical margins
✗ Wrong options: site, size, grade, neoadjuvant chemo

Radiation-induced OS (TO)worse prognosis than conventional · Arlen criteria: different prior histology, in radiation field, long latency, histological proof

Situation 5-yr survival
Localised, standard treatment ~65% (extremity 65%, pelvis 55%)
Good chemo response 85%
Pulmonary mets at diagnosis 15–20% (resectable 15–25%)
Late (>1 yr) lung mets + thoracotomy ~30%
Bone mets Incurable · <5% long term
Paget OS <15% long term

Pelvic OSlarger, more mets, local recurrence up to 44% despite negative margins

Metastasectomycomplete resection of primary + mets → 5× long-term survival

Surveillance (NCCN)q3 mo yr 1–2 · q4 mo yr 3 · q6 mo yr 4–5 · yearly after · exam, imaging of site + chest, ± bone scan/PET, ALP

X vs Y

Conventional OS Parosteal OS Periosteal OS
Grade High Low Intermediate
Age 10–20 3rd–4th decade, F Slightly older
Site Metaphysis, medullary Posterior distal femur, surface Diaphysis femur/tibia
X-ray Sunburst, Codman Stuck-on, heavily ossified, central > peripheral Sunburst on surface, saucerized
Histology Lacy osteoid, malignant stroma Bland spindle cells, parallel trabeculae Chondroblastic lobules
Treatment Chemo + wide resection + chemo Wide resection alone Chemo (NCCN) + wide resection
Osteosarcoma Myositis ossificans
Ossification Dense centre Mature periphery (zonal)
Histology Atypia, lacy osteoid Mature bone, bland fibroblasts

✗ Wrong options: + etoposide · vincristine

"Parosteal vs intramedullary OS treatment?"parosteal: wide resection alone · intramedullary: neoadjuvant chemo then resection

✗ Wrong options: younger · low grade · better survival

"Most common site of parosteal OS?"back of distal femur (TO)

✗ Wrong options: small cell strongly CD99 + · parosteal high grade on tibia · jaw OS more mets (TO)

"Most common tumour in children?"brain tumour (TO)
✗ Wrong options: neuroblastoma · OS

Dermatofibrosarcoma Protuberans

Pathology · 13 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence1% of soft-tissue sarcomas · rare, low-grade fibrogenic cutaneous sarcoma arising in the dermis

Demographics20–50 yr (peak 4th decade) · male (3:2) · African Americans 2:1

Sitetrunk (47%) > proximal extremities > head/neck (14%)

Variants

Bednar tumourpigmented · 7.5× more common in African Americans · melanin-containing dendritic cells

Fibrosarcomatous (DFSP-FS)5–15% · herringbone pattern · more aggressive, poor prognosis

Genes & lab markers

t(17;22)COL1A1–PDGFB fusion → PDGF-β overexpression → target for imatinib

3Presentation

Massslowly enlarging, painless, firm irregular nodules · dark red / blue discoloration · early pink/violet plaques with telangiectatic skin

Lateulceration, attachment to deep structures · symptoms 6 months to 30 years · often misdiagnosed and excised before sarcoma suspected

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

MRIextent + depth · preop for large, atypical, recurrent lesions

CXRpulmonary mets in high-risk cases (recurrence, fibrosarcomatous)

CTbone involvement / mets · US — local + nodal monitoring · PET — metastatic monitoring

Histology

Biopsy required

Classicuniform fibroblasts in a storiform pattern around inconspicuous vessels · diffusely infiltrates dermis/subcutis · interdigitates with fat ("honeycomb") at deep margin

5Management

Treatment — wide excision

Resectable DFSPwide excision (with frozen sections) · 2–3 cm margins (2.5–3 cm skin cuff) including deep fascia

After "lumpectomy"wide tumour bed re-excision
✗ Wrong options: observation · marginal resection · RT alone · chemo

Adjuvant RT (50–70 Gy)positive margins, large tumours, or when wide excision → major cosmetic/functional deficit

ImatinibPDGF-receptor tyrosine kinase inhibitor · unresectable, recurrent, metastatic adult DFSP · 65% response · no response without t(17;22)

Not indicatedintralesional excision + RT · neoadjuvant chemo · Mohs referral (OB answer: wide excision)

6Complications

Local recurrence commonbody 15.7% · head/neck 51.8%

Distant metastasis rare (<5%)lung (haematogenous) · usually after multiple local recurrences

Poor prognosisregional nodes · fibrosarcomatous change · ↑ mitoses, cellularity, aneuploidy, TP53 overexpression · age >50

X vs Y

Classic DFSP Fibrosarcomatous DFSP Bednar
Pattern Storiform Herringbone Storiform + melanin dendritic cells
Behaviour Low grade Aggressive, can metastasize Low grade

How it's asked

"45-yr-old, leg mass with skin ulceration, storiform spindle cells?"dermatofibrosarcoma
✗ Wrong options: Ewing/PNET · extraskeletal myxoid CS · myxoid liposarcoma · synovial sarcoma (deep, rarely ulcerate)

"Shoulder skin mass after excisional biopsy shows DFSP — next?"wide tumour-bed resection

"Abdominal wall nodular mass, t(17;22), herringbone areas?"wide excision (± RT)
✗ Wrong options: Mohs · marginal · neoadjuvant chemo

"Systemic agent for unresectable DFSP?"imatinib (only if t(17;22))

"Tumour treated with chemo + RT (in a list of chemo/RT-resistant tumours)?"DFSP (vs classic chondrosarcoma resistant to both)

Differential Groups

Pathology · 14 / 66

4Diagnosis & Classification

Workup of incidental lesion

Principlea radiograph suggesting a potentially malignant process needs complete staging before any treatment, even if it looks like GCT

Next stepcross-sectional imaging (MRI) of the limb · then chest imaging, labs, ± bone scan → sarcoma centre referral if needed

Severe pain, insists on treatment todayadmit for parenteral analgesia + tumour work-up
✗ Wrong options: outpatient opioids

Not appropriate firstintra-articular steroid injection · physiotherapy + NSAIDs · hip radiograph

Starting treatment before oncologic work-upcan be devastating (contamination, lost limb salvage)

Aggressive-looking bone lesion on X-ray
  • First→MRI OF THE LIMB (local staging)
  • Then→CHEST IMAGING + LABS ± BONE SCAN
  • Then → BIOPSY AT TREATING CENTRE→DEFINITIVE TREATMENT

How it's asked

"50-yr-old, obese, knee pain, aggressive proximal tibial lesion, wants treatment today — next?"MRI of the lower extremity
✗ Wrong options: steroid injection · opioids · PT + NSAIDs · hip X-ray

Enchondromas

Pathology · 15 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rank2nd commonest benign cartilage lesion (osteochondroma 1st)

DemographicsM = F · 20–50 yr (3rd–4th decade)

Sitemedullary, metaphysis or diaphysis · hand 60% > feet · distal femur 20% > proximal humerus 10% > tibia · metaphyseal when first seen → appears diaphyseal as bone grows

Handcommonest primary bone tumour of the hand (42% in small tubular bones)
✗ Wrong options: periosteal chondroma · subungual exostosis · osteoid osteoma

"Most common" list (TO)

Category Answer
Tumour of the hand (overall) Squamous cell carcinoma (TO keyed answer)
Bone tumour of hand bones Enchondroma
Malignant bone tumour of hand bones Chondrosarcoma
Tumour of bone Metastasis
Primary bone tumour Myeloma > OS > chondrosarcoma
Benign bone tumour Osteochondroma
True benign bone tumour Osteoid osteoma
Lesion of bone Fibrous cortical defect
Malignant bone tumour, 1st decade Ewing
Radiation-induced tumour OS > fibrosarcoma > MFH
Most radio-/chemosensitive Ewing
Bone tumour of mandible Ameloblastoma
Malignant bone tumour of chest wall Chondrosarcoma (chest wall)

Pathophysiology

Originincomplete endochondral ossification · physeal chondroblasts escape into the metaphysis and proliferate
✗ Wrong options: intramembranous

Genes & lab markers

IDH1 / IDH2enchondroma, Ollier (and chondrosarcoma)

Ollier / Maffuccisporadic, no inheritance pattern (TO) · (✗ Wrong options: GNAS (McCune-Albright) · p53 · chromosome 6q13 (CMF) · PGE2/COX (osteoid osteoma))
✗ Wrong options: AD (that is MHE/EXT)

3Presentation

Asymptomaticincidental on films (long bones, foot)

Pathologic fracturetypical in the hand after minor trauma

Painuncommon · usually another cause (cuff tendinopathy, cervical radiculopathy, ankle sprain, vascular claudication) · rest/night pain → think chondrosarcoma

Syndromesshortening, bowing, angular deformity · bluish angiomas (Maffucci)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (usually diagnostic)well-defined, lucent, central medullary lesion, 1–10 cm, calcifies over time · "popcorn" stippling, rings, arcs, whorls · endosteal erosion <50% (<2/3) of cortex · hands/feet: expansion + thinning, may be purely lytic

MRInot needed for diagnosis · lobular, T2 bright (water), no marrow oedema, no periosteal reaction · "sled-runner tracks" · medullary fill >90% → chondrosarcoma

Bone scan↑ uptake (remodelling) but < ASIS (only 20% > ASIS) · identifies polyostotic disease

CToptional, for endosteal scalloping

Skeletal surveyif polyostotic suspected (Ollier dysplasia, Maffucci phleboliths)

Histology

Grossblue-grey lobulated hyaline cartilage, scattered calcification

Long boneshypocellular, bland mature hyaline cartilage ("blue balls") separated by normal marrow · encased by lamellar bone · abundant matrix, no myxoid component · small bland chondrocytes in lacunae, no pleomorphism/mitoses/binucleation

Hand / small tubular bones / fibulahypercellular, mild atypia → not malignant

Biopsyrarely helpful · sampling error, can't separate from low-grade CS (SLICE study: poor reliability)

Enchondroma vs chondrosarcoma

Enchondroma Chondrosarcoma
Age 20–50 >50
Size <5 cm >5 cm
Pain None / other cause Rest + night pain
Endosteal scalloping <50% (<2/3) cortex >2/3 cortex
Cortex Intact Thickened, destroyed, periosteal reaction
Soft-tissue mass No Yes
MRI No oedema Oedema, >90% medullary fill
Bone scan < ASIS > ASIS
Histology Hypocellular, lacunar cells, normal marrow between Hypercellular, plump binucleate nuclei, permeation

Bone infarctsmoke up the chimney, low T2 (vs bright T2 enchondroma)

Enneking staging

Enchondroma = Enneking stage 1 (latent) (with NOF)
✗ Wrong options: ABC, CMF, chondroblastoma = 2 · GCT = 3

Cartilage-origin tumours (TO)

WHOosteochondroma, enchondroma, chondroblastoma, CMF all cartilage tumours → "all are cartilage tumours EXCEPT" → none (TO) · WHO 5th ed. moved chondroblastoma + CMF to benign (no intermediate cartilage tumour now)

5Management

Latent lesion — observation vs low-grade chondrosarcoma

Enchondroma
  • Asymptomatic / pain from another cause→OBSERVE, X-rays at 6 + 12 mo (3–6 mo)
  • Growth / change on serial X-ray, true lesional pain→EXTENDED INTRALESIONAL CURETTAGE + GRAFT
  • Hand pathologic fracture→CURETTAGE + GRAFT (immediate = delayed)
  • Aggressive features (scalloping >2/3, mass)→REFER ORTHO ONCOLOGY, treat as chondrosarcoma

Observationvast majority · serial radiographs · long-term follow-up for syndromes · no biopsy, MRI, CT, bone scan or CT chest/abdomen (even with prostate cancer history if benign-looking)

Curettage + graft (or cement) indicationschange on serial X-rays · symptomatic · pathologic fracture · suspicious for low-grade CS · large lesion at risk of re-fracture · local recurrence unusual

Not indicatedchemo, RT (radiation-induced sarcoma), RFA, wide resection + arthroplasty (over-treatment), amputation

Pain from another source

Treat the real causeshoulder pain with overhead activity → cuff (PT + observe lesion) · deltoid paraesthesia + elbow flexion weakness → cervical spine X-rays (C5) · ankle sprain instability → functional rehab, proprioception · walking pain relieved by standing still, ABI 0.8 → vascular claudication

Hand enchondroma & pathologic fracture

Painful/growing thumb lesioncurettage + bone grafting
✗ Wrong options: amputation · chemo · RT

Pathologic fracturecurettage + grafting · immediate = delayed (immobilise then curette) for consolidation, DASH, recurrence, complications — only difference is longer recovery with delayed · void fillers: autograft, calcium phosphate/sulfate, PMMA, coralline HA

Hand chondrosarcomawide resection / ray resection

6Complications
