Eosinophilic Granuloma

Pathology · 16 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Age80% <20 yr · commonly <10 · M:F 2:1

Siteskull, ribs, clavicle, scapula, mandible, pelvis · spine (thoracic commonest) · diaphysis of long bones

Natureself-limiting, benign histiocytic lesion (Langerhans cell histiocytosis, LCH) · reticuloendothelial system

LCH spectrum

Form Age Features Prognosis
Eosinophilic granuloma Children/adolescents Single, self-limited bone lesion Excellent · spine lesions resolve spontaneously
Hand-Schüller-Christian >3 yr Chronic disseminated · triad: multiple lytic skull lesions + diabetes insipidus + exophthalmos · visceral (lung, liver, spleen, skin, nodes) → abdominal pain Depends on chemo response · worse with more extraskeletal disease
Letterer-Siwe <3 yr Fulminant · skin rash from birth, multiple lesions Fatal in early childhood

Genes & lab markers

Geneticsno clear inheritance or locus

CD1a + Langerhans cells · Birbeck granules on EM

3Presentation

Bonepain + swelling · limp (pelvis / lower limb)

Spinelocal or diffuse back/neck pain · progressive kyphosis · radiculopathy if aggressive

HSCthirst / polydipsia (DI) · exophthalmos · weight loss, skin lesions, abdominal pain

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

"Great mimicker"DD osteomyelitis, leukaemia, lymphoma, fibrous dysplasia, Ewing

Diaphysiswell-defined intramedullary lytic "punched-out" lesion · cortex thinned/expanded/destroyed · periosteal reaction possible

Metaphysisup to, not through, the physis · less central

Spinevertebra plana (flattened vertebral body) · ↑ kyphosis

Skullmultiple punched-out lytic lesions

MRIadjacent soft-tissue mass · Bone scan — ↑ uptake · Whole-body PET/CT — staging if multifocal · skeletal survey finds extra lesions

Vertebra plana — differential (TO)

Cause Vertebra plana?
Eosinophilic granuloma / LCH ✓ Commonest
Ewing sarcoma Yes
Lymphoma / leukaemia Yes
Gaucher disease Yes
ABC Yes
Infection (spondylitis) Yes
Metastasis (neuroblastoma) ✓ occasionally
Lyme disease No

Histology

Langerhans cellsmononuclear histiocyte-like cells, oval nuclei with prominent nuclear groove ("coffee-bean"), eosinophilic cytoplasm

StainCD1a +

EMBirbeck granules — tennis-racquet shaped cytoplasmic organelles, diagnostic
✗ Wrong options: Langerhans cell itself · plasma cell · Kupffer cell

Backgroundeosinophils (bilobed nuclei), mixed inflammatory cells, giant cells

No nuclear atypia, no atypical mitosesseparates from Ewing, lymphoma, metastatic neuroblastoma

Differential

Destructive lesion, young Multiple lesions, young Observation alone
EG ✓ — (HSC ✓) ✓ (self-limited)
Osteomyelitis · OS · Ewing · desmoplastic fibroma ✓ — —
Lymphoma · leukaemia ✓ ✓ —
FD · Ollier/Maffucci · MHE · NOF/Jaffe-Campanacci — ✓ ✓
Haemangioendothelioma — ✓ —

5Management

Eosinophilic granuloma
  • Isolated, stable→OBSERVATION ± protected weight bearing, repeat X-ray 6 wk
    • Symptomatic→CORTICOSTEROID INJECTION (± after curettage)
  • Spine, no neuro deficit→BRACE / COLLAR + OBSERVE
    • Instability / neuro compromise→LOW-DOSE RT (600–800 cGy)
    • Progressive deformity despite brace (~10%)→SURGICAL CORRECTION
  • Impending fracture / articular surface at risk→CURETTAGE + GRAFT
  • Disseminated HSC→CHEMOTHERAPY

Treatment — observation

Observationself-limited · heals spontaneously or after biopsy · femoral diaphysis: crutches + repeat X-ray in 6 wk
✗ Wrong options: wide excision · chemo · RT alone · curettage first

Cervical spine EG, no neuro deficitcervical collar + observation
✗ Wrong options: antibiotics · chemo/RT · resection

Bracingprevents kyphosis · corrects deformity 90% · vertebrae regain ~50% height

Low-dose RT 600–800 cGyspinal instability / neurology · lesions not amenable to injection/surgery

Chemotherapyonly diffuse/disseminated HSC

Surgical decompressionrare cord compression

X vs Y

EG / LCH Ewing Osteomyelitis
Cells Langerhans cells, coffee-bean nuclei, eosinophils Small round blue cells, t(11;22) Neutrophils
Marker CD1a, Birbeck CD99 Culture
Atypia None Malignant None
Treatment Observe Chemo + local control Antibiotics ± debridement

How it's asked

"5-yr-old, skin lesions, limp, weight loss, lytic femoral lesion with histiocytes?"eosinophilic granuloma
✗ Wrong options: neuroblastoma · Ewing · ALL · fifth disease

"2-yr-old, thirst, exophthalmos, lytic skull lesions?"LCH (Hand-Schüller-Christian)
✗ Wrong options: abuse · NF · metastatic neuroblastoma · adamantinoma

"6-yr-old, limp, skull + pelvic lesions, thirst, abdominal pain?"Hand-Schüller-Christian
✗ Wrong options: McCune-Albright · Maffucci · Ollier

"10-month-old, multiple skeletal lesions + rash since birth, CD1a +?"LCH
✗ Wrong options: osteomyelitis · Ewing · lymphoma · neuroblastoma

"Vertebra plana — EM structure (tennis racquet)?"Birbeck granule

"7-yr-old, femoral diaphyseal EG?"crutches + repeat X-ray in 6 weeks

"7-yr-old, cervical EG, no neuro deficit?"cervical collar + observation

"Causes of vertebra plana — all EXCEPT?"Lyme disease (✓ EG, Ewing, lymphoma) (TO)

Epithelioid Sarcoma

Pathology · 17 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rankcommonest soft-tissue sarcoma of the hand and wrist (others in hand: MFH, synovial sarcoma, clear cell sarcoma)
✗ Wrong options: chondrosarcoma · rhabdomyosarcoma · myxoid liposarcoma · haemangiopericytoma

Demographicsadolescents + young adults 10–35 (10–40) · M:F 2:1

Sitehand/wrist, fingers · forearm, buttock/thigh, knee, foot

Depthsuperficial → ulcerates, mimics skin carcinoma, rheumatoid nodule, granuloma · deep → attached to tendons, tendon sheaths, fascia

Spread

Regional lymph nodescommon · unique, more frequent than other sarcomas (lungs still the commonest overall site)

Spreads along lymphatics and fascial planes · 30% metastasized at presentation

Lymphatic sarcomas — "RACES"Rhabdomyosarcoma · Angiosarcoma · Clear cell sarcoma · Epithelioid sarcoma · Synovial sarcoma
✗ Wrong options: liposarcoma · fibrosarcoma · leiomyosarcoma · UPS

Genes & lab markers

Geneticsunknown

IHCkeratin +, EMA +, vimentin +, CD34 +

3Presentation

Masssmall (3–6 cm), firm, painless, slow-growing nodule · upper extremity · may ulcerate

Historyoften a previous "benign" excision → recurrence (misdiagnosed)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raycalcification 10–20% · adjacent bone erosion

MRIindeterminate · T1 dark, T2 bright · tendon-sheath nodule

Histology

Patternnodular with central necrosis in granulomatous-looking areas → mimics necrotizing granuloma · chronic inflammatory cells at margins

Cellsepithelioid ovoid/polygonal cells, eosinophilic cytoplasm + spindle cells · dense hyalinized collagen · pleomorphism rare

Differential

Lesion Distinguishing feature
Epithelioid sarcoma Young adult, nodules with central necrosis, keratin +
Rheumatoid nodule Similar histology · older, RA
Squamous cell carcinoma Keratin pearls
Angiosarcoma (soft tissue) Well-formed vascular channels
Epidermal inclusion cyst Benign appearance, low recurrence
Clear cell sarcoma / carcinoma Clear cells
BPOP (Nora lesion) Reactive HO, hands/feet, 3rd decade

5Management

Wide excision + adjuvant radiotherapyall operable tumours

Sentinel lymph node biopsyat excision → regional nodal staging

Inadequate excisionhigh rate of multiple recurrences → re-excision of tumour bed

Amputationmultiple recurrences, to stop spread

Unplanned excision — re-resection

Unplanned ("whoops") marginal resectionresidual tumour in 50–60% → repeat staging + re-resection of tumour bed (± pre/post-op RT)
✗ Wrong options: PT · AFO · tendon transfer · nerve exploration first

6Complications

Prognosisextremely poor · lymph node + lung metastasis · recurrence

How it's asked

"28-yr-old, recurrent ulcerating thumb mass, nodules with necrosis?"epithelioid sarcoma
✗ Wrong options: rheumatoid nodule · SCC (keratin pearls) · angiosarcoma · inclusion cyst

"Slowly growing finger nodule, nodular necrosis, no clear cells?"epithelioid sarcoma
✗ Wrong options: clear cell sarcoma/carcinoma · BPOP

"Commonest soft-tissue sarcoma of the hand?"epithelioid sarcoma

"Sarcoma most likely to spread via lymphatics?"epithelioid sarcoma (RACES)

"Site of metastasis more common than in other sarcomas?"regional lymph nodes → sentinel node biopsy

"Foot drop after outside marginal excision of popliteal epithelioid sarcoma?"staging + re-resection of tumour bed

Ewing's Sarcoma

Pathology · 18 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rank2nd commonest primary malignant bone tumour in children (OS 1st) · 3% of paediatric malignancies, 10% of primary malignant bone tumours · ~3/million · commonest malignant bone tumour of the 1st decade · rhabdomyosarcoma = commonest paediatric soft-tissue sarcoma

Age5–25 · 80–90% <20 · peak 10–15

Sex / raceM:F 1.5:1 · rare in African Americans and Asians

Siteeven split axial vs appendicular · metadiaphysis of long bones ~50% (femur, tibia, humerus) · pelvis ~25% · scapula, ribs · soft tissue rare

Diaphysis myth (TO)only ~50% diaphyseal, 50% metaphyseal → "predominantly diaphyseal" is false

Originunknown, probably neuroectodermal (PNET family) · cell of origin likely in marrow

Genes & lab markers

t(11;22)(q24;q12)EWS–FLI1 fusion (aberrant transcription factor: EWS activation domain + FLI ETS DNA-binding domain) · 85–95% · detect by PCR / FISH

Variantst(21;22) EWS–ERG (10–15%) · t(7;22)

t(11;22) presencedoes not alter clinical course (TO)

Translocation / fusion Tumour
t(11;22) EWS–FLI1 ✓ Ewing
t(X;18) SYT–SSX Synovial sarcoma
t(12;16) TLS–CHOP Myxoid liposarcoma
t(12;22) EWS–ATF1 Clear cell sarcoma
t(9;22) EWS–CHN Extraskeletal myxoid chondrosarcoma
t(2;13) PAX3–FKHR Alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma
t(X;17) ASPL–TFE3 Alveolar soft part sarcoma
RB, p53 Osteosarcoma (✗ Wrong options: Ewing)

Labs↑ ESR, ↑ WBC, anaemia, ↑ LDH → mimics infection, marks extensive disease

ImmunostainsCD99 / MIC2 + (95%) · vimentin + · PAS + (glycogen) · NSE +, S-100 +, Leu7 (CD57) + · cytokeratin −, reticulin − (+ in lymphoma), neurofilament − (+ neuroblastoma), myoglobin − (+ embryonal rhabdo)

CD45 (LCA)listed + in Orthobullets chart · TO/OB: lymphoma is LCA +, Ewing LCA − (OB calls LCA "CD34") — read the options

3Presentation

Paincommonest, worse at night · >50% symptomatic >6 months (delay worse in pelvis/axial)

Infection mimicswelling, erythema, warmth, fever (~20–25%), weight loss, ↑ WBC/ESR

Massmay be impalpable until large · limp, ↓ ROM

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raylarge destructive diaphyseal/metadiaphyseal lesion · ill-defined permeative, moth-eaten · lytic ± reactive bone · "onion skin" (lamellated) or "sunburst" / "hair-on-end" periosteal reaction · Codman triangle · soft-tissue mass >80%

MRInext step after suspicious X-ray · soft-tissue extent, marrow, NV relation · T1 low–intermediate, T2 high heterogeneous, heterogeneous enhancement · monitors chemo response

Bone scanvery hot · skip/distant mets (10%) · PET better than bone scan for Ewing mets (vs OS)

CT chestrequired for lung mets

Work-up (MRI, bone marrow biopsy)

Bone marrow biopsyrequired for Ewing (marrow mets), not routine for OS
✗ Wrong options: MRI · CT · bone scan · protein electrophoresis (done for both or neither)

OrderX-ray → MRI → staging (CT chest, bone scan, bone marrow biopsy) → biopsy by treating surgeon

Not appropriateno further work-up · arthrogram · pelvic US · chiropractor · cast / rest and repeat X-ray · IV antibiotics / I&D when ESR/CRP normal

Histology

Grossgrey-white, necrosis, haemorrhage · liquid, "pus-like"

Microsheets of monotonous small round blue cells, high N:C ratio, scant cytoplasm · pseudorosettes (Homer-Wright) · no stroma

Small round blue cell tumours — by age

Age Likeliest
<5 yr Metastatic neuroblastoma, leukaemia
5–10 yr Eosinophilic granuloma
5–30 yr (peak 5–15) Ewing
>30 (15–40) Lymphoma
>40–50 Myeloma, metastatic small cell lung Ca
2nd–3rd decade (rare) Small-cell OS, mesenchymal chondrosarcoma

Round-cell tumoursEwing, PNET, neuroblastoma, embryonal rhabdomyosarcoma, EG, lymphoma, myeloma, round-cell liposarcoma · not leiomyosarcoma = spindle cells, cigar nuclei

TBsmall round cells (lymphocytes) but periarticular, joint

Staging

MSTSalmost all IIB or III · 26–28% macrometastases at presentation · sites lung 50%, bone 25%, marrow 20%

Differential

Destructive, young Small round cells Wide resection + chemo
Ewing ✓ ✓ ✓
Osteosarcoma ✓ — ✓
Lymphoma · leukaemia · EG ✓ ✓ —
Osteomyelitis · desmoplastic fibroma ✓ — —
Neuroblastoma · myeloma — ✓ —
Dedifferentiated CS — — ✓

Osteomyelitis vs Ewingmixed inflammatory infiltrate (neutrophils, plasma cells, lymphocytes, histiocytes) → debridement + antibiotics · Ewing = uniform round cells, no inflammation

5Management

Treatment — chemotherapy with surgery or radiation

Ewing sarcoma (chemo needed in ALL — presume micromets)
  • Resectable / expendable site → NEOADJUVANT CHEMO → WIDE RESECTION→ADJUVANT CHEMO
    • Positive margin / poor necrosis / mets→+ ADJUVANT RT
  • Unresectable (spine, pelvis) or unacceptable morbidity→CHEMO + RT
  • Huge tumour on NV structures, displaced path fracture, foot/ankle→AMPUTATION

ChemoVAC: vincristine, Adriamycin (doxorubicin), cyclophosphamide (± dactinomycin) alternating with ifosfamide + etoposide (IE) · neoadjuvant 8–12 wk → adjuvant 6–12 mo
✗ Wrong options: methotrexate-based = OS

Surgerypreferred local control when wide margins achievable · negative margins → 5-yr survival ↑ >10% · positive margin → re-resect ± RT · recurrence 5–10% with surgery vs 35% RT alone

Reconstructionvascularized/non-vascularized autograft (fibula, scapula, iliac crest, rib, clavicle), allograft, APC, endoprosthesis, rotationplasty

Radiationradiosensitive (most radio- + chemosensitive bone tumour) but trend away from RT · field = tumour + 2–3 cm, 56–60 Gy · standard = hyperfractionated · indications: unresectable, positive margins, poor chemo response, all pulmonary mets

Extensive pelvic disease + lung metschemo + radiation

Spine with neuro deficit, resection too morbidlocal control by radiation

Outcomes5-yr 82% (no mets) vs 39% (mets) · 10-yr 63% vs 32%

Extraskeletal Ewing sarcoma (TO)

Histology same as skeletal ✓ · adolescents/young adults (✗ Wrong options: <10) · paravertebral muscles, chest wall (✗ Wrong options: thigh) · t(11;22), MIC2 + · chemosensitive (✗ Wrong options: resistant) · same treatment · better prognosis than osseous (OB)

6Complications

Radiation complications (>60%)LLD, joint contracture, muscle atrophy, pathologic fracture, secondary sarcoma

Radiation-induced sarcoma

Secondary bone sarcoma~20% at 20 yr · in radiation field · dose dependent (<60 Gy → <5%) · e.g. lytic lesion 20 yr after RT at age 10
✗ Wrong options: recurrence · infarct · adenocarcinoma

AML / myelodysplasia1–2%, 2–5 yr after chemo (dose-intensive regimens)

DVT risk

↑ VTE in paediatric sarcoma (Ewing) — tumour activates factor X → Xa · no consensus on prophylaxis
✗ Wrong options: haemangioma · MSSA osteomyelitis (MRSA ↑) · NOF · PVNS

Recurrence~20% localized, >60% metastatic · after recurrence <10% 5-yr

Mets curelung only 30% · bone only 20% · lung + bone <15%

Prognostic factors

Most importantmetastasis at diagnosis · lung mets better than bone/marrow · skip mets better than distant

Poor prognosis Threshold
Age >10 (TO) · >14 (Orthobullets)
Size >8 cm / ≥200 mL
Site Pelvis/spine (worst) > proximal > distal (best)
Chemo response <90% necrosis · surgically treated: necrosis = most important (TO)
Labs ↑ LDH (>200 IU/L), ↑ WBC, ↑ ESR, anaemia
Clinical Fever, male sex
Marrow Amount of marrow involvement
Molecular p53 mutation + t(11;22), Ki-67, HER-2/neu

Not prognostic (TO)histological grade (all Ewing high grade) · t(11;22) presence · female sex is not a bad sign

Good prognostic sign (TO)age 7 yr
✗ Wrong options: 12 cm · fever · TLC 14,000

Extra-abdominal Desmoid Tumor

Pathology · 19 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Naturebenign but most locally invasive benign soft-tissue tumour (aggressive fibromatosis) · no metastasis, no malignant change (unless radiation-related)

Demographics25–35 yr (15–40) · F:M 3:1 · ~900/yr in US

Siteshoulder (20%) > chest wall/back (17%) > thigh (13%) · >50% extra-abdominal · sites of previous surgery/scars · multiple lesions in same limb → next one appears more proximally

Association with FAP

Familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP) / Gardner syndromedesmoid in 4–30% (prevalence ~21%) · 10,000× risk
✗ Wrong options: Sinding-Larsen-Johansson · Ollier · Fanconi · McCune-Albright

Gardner featurescolonic polyps + jaw osteomas + epidermoid cysts

FAPautosomal dominant, APC tumour-suppressor gene · ~100% lifetime colon cancer → screen for colon cancer
✗ Wrong options: lung, breast, thyroid, renal

Other associationsDupuytren contracture · Ledderhose disease

Genes & lab markers

APC mutation15% of tumours (APC controls β-catenin)

β-catenin ↑all tumours

Trisomy 8 or 20

IHC +oestrogen receptor-β (100%), somatostatin, cathepsin D, Ki-67, c-Kit · IHC − — ER-α, PR, HER2

Cortical desmoid

Cortical desmoidself-limiting benign fibro-osseous lesion of active adolescents (10–15) · chronic tendon traction · posteromedial distal femur at medial gastrocnemius / adductor magnus insertion · also humerus (pectoralis, deltoid insertions)

Imaginggeographic central lucency, sclerotic margin, "bite-like" cortical irregularity · MRI T1 low–intermediate, T2 high, heterogeneous enhancement

Treatmentactivity modification + PT (self-limiting)

3Presentation

Massenlarging, painless or painful · "rock hard", fixed, poorly circumscribed · limits joint motion

Nerve compressionnumbness, tingling, weakness, stabbing pain

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

MRI — best means to diagnose · excludes multicentric lesions · T1 low, T2 low–medium (collagen) · gadolinium enhances · infiltrates muscle · 5–10 cm · may erode bone

X-rayminimal role · bone erosion, "frond-like" periosteal spicules into tumour

Histology

Grosswhite, gritty, poorly encapsulated

Microwell-differentiated fibroblasts/myofibroblasts, uniform spindle cells with elongated nuclei, occasional mitoses, abundant collagen, infiltrates adjacent tissue

Histology Diagnosis
Uniform spindle fibroblasts, elongated nuclei, collagen ✓ Desmoid
Fibroblasts + Schwann cells, mast cells, wavy nuclei Neurofibroma
Stromal cells, foam cells, haemosiderin giant cells PVNS
Small round blue cells Ewing
Storiform fibroblasts, lipophages, giant cells NOF

5Management

Extra-abdominal desmoid
  • Asymptomatic / minimally symptomatic→ACTIVE SURVEILLANCE, SERIAL MRI (first line)
  • Progressive, inoperable, or can't tolerate chemo→TAMOXIFEN / LOW-DOSE CHEMO
  • Symptomatic / recurrent / failed surveillance→WIDE RESECTION + RT (50–60 Gy)

Active surveillance ("watch and wait")primary strategy (Desmoid Tumor Working Group) · may stay stable or regress spontaneously

Tamoxifenoestrogen-receptor blockade · moderate short-term success with less morbidity · for inoperable lesions / poor chemo candidates · works as lesions commoner + more aggressive in (premenopausal) women

Wide resection + radiotherapysymptomatic or recurrent lesions · 50–60 Gy EBRT · abdominal wall desmoid → surgery if surveillance fails

6Complications

Recurrence

Margin Surgery alone Surgery + RT
Negative 28% 6%
Positive 39% 25%

Highest local recurrence of soft-tissue tumours after wide resection without adjuvant

Prognosisunpredictable · occasional spontaneous regression · no metastasis

How it's asked

"20-yr-old woman, rock-hard calf mass, ER-β +?"extra-abdominal fibromatosis (desmoid)
✗ Wrong options: OS · leiomyosarcoma · angiosarcoma

"Shoulder mass, family with colonic polyps, jaw osteomas, epidermoid cysts — histology?"uniform spindle fibroblasts with elongated nuclei
✗ Wrong options: neurofibroma · PVNS · Ewing · NOF

"Adolescent lacrosse player, posteromedial distal femoral cortical lesion from tendon traction?"cortical desmoid → activity modification + PT

"48-yr-old, 5 cm thigh mass, biopsy → active surveillance with serial MRI?"extra-abdominal desmoid
✗ Wrong options: lipoma · GCT · osteoid osteoma

"Inherited condition associated with desmoids?"FAP

"Desmoid patient — screen for?"colon cancer

"Tumour treated by oestrogen-receptor blockade?"desmoid (tamoxifen)
✗ Wrong options: Ewing · synovial sarcoma · myeloma · GCT

Fibrous Dysplasia

Pathology · 20 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence1 in 5,000–10,000 · ~2.5% of bone lesions, 5% of benign bone lesions

Age75% onset <30 (5–30) · monostotic 10–30 · polyostotic earlier (<10; 60% symptomatic)

SexM = F

Siteany bone · proximal femur commonest · monostotic: ribs 28%, proximal femur 23%, skull/craniofacial, tibia, humerus · polyostotic: often unilateral, femur, skull/face, pelvis, spine · craniofacial: zygomatic-maxillary complex · skull + face generalized = "leontiasis ossea" (TO)

Location in bonemedullary (metaphysis/diaphysis) — not cortical (NOF, OFD, adamantinoma are cortical) (TO)

Pathophysiology

Defectprimitive woven bone fails to remodel to lamellar bone → immature trabeculae in dysplastic fibrous tissue

Natural historyactive in childhood → inactive at skeletal maturity (monostotic grows with skeleton; polyostotic with disease severity)

Genes & lab markers — GNAS / Gs-alpha

Mutationactivating missense GNAS (GNAS1) → Gs-α subunit (20q13) · somatic mosaicism, spontaneous · no vertical transmission · timing → extent

Pathwayloss of intrinsic GTPase → constitutive adenylate cyclase → ↑ cAMP → c-fos ↑ · ↑ WNT/β-catenin → osteoprogenitors fail to mature · ↑ NF-κB, IL-6 → resorption

FGF-23 ↑renal phosphate wasting → hypophosphataemia → osteomalacia (Looser zones)

cAMP pathway question✓ FD
✗ Wrong options: achondroplasia FGFR3 · hypochondroplasia · MHE EXT · NF1 · enchondroma

Gene / defect Condition
GNAS / Gs-α (↑ cAMP) ✓ Fibrous dysplasia, McCune-Albright
DTD (sulfate transporter) Diastrophic dysplasia
CBFA1 / RUNX2 Cleidocranial dysostosis
COL1A1 / COL1A2 Osteogenesis imperfecta
FGFR3 Achondroplasia
Ring chromosome 12 (MDM2) Low-grade osteosarcoma
t(11;22) Ewing

Labsmonostotic: none · polyostotic: thyroid panel, IGF-1, GH, prolactin (MAS screen) · ALP to monitor bisphosphonate; ↑ ALP with malignant change

Associated syndromes

Syndrome Components
McCune-Albright Polyostotic FD + café-au-lait ("coast of Maine", jagged, don't cross midline, trunk) + endocrinopathy (precocious puberty — 85% of girls; thyroid 66%; GH excess; ovarian cysts; neonatal hypercortisolism) · unilateral
Mazabraud Polyostotic FD + intramuscular myxomas
Jaffe-Lichtenstein Polyostotic FD + café-au-lait, no endocrinopathy
Jaffe-Campanacci Multiple NOFs + café-au-lait, no neurofibromas
NF1 Café-au-lait "coast of California" (smooth) + neurofibromas, 17q11.2

3Presentation

Usually asymptomaticincidental

Painhigh-stress sites · ↑ in pregnancy / menses (oestrogen receptors)

DeformityLLD (commonest skeletal deformity) · shepherd's crook (coxa vara, lateral bowing, shortening) · saber shin (anterior tibial bowing) · scoliosis

Craniofacialcosmetic deformity, blindness, hearing loss, airway obstruction, proptosis, sinus occlusion

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayexpansile, central intramedullary, well-defined, smooth cortices · lytic, sclerotic or mixed → "ground glass" · cortical thinning, endosteal scalloping · no periosteal reaction · fills bone over length

"Rind sign"punched-out / ground-glass lesion with thick sclerotic rim

Looser zonespseudofractures (osteomalacia)

Shepherd's crookAP + lateral hip

Spine X-rays (AP/lateral)in polyostotic / MAS (60% spinal involvement, posterior elements; scoliosis 40%) → next step after hot spinal lesions on bone scan
✗ Wrong options: CT · pelvis X-ray · bisphosphonates · endocrine screen

CTground glass 56%, sclerosis 23%, cystic 21% · enhances

MRIT1 dark, T2 variable, moderate heterogeneous enhancement · separates from malignancy

Bone scanwarm until adulthood · screens for polyostotic disease / MAS in children

Diagnosishistory + exam + X-ray usually enough · biopsy if polyostotic/MAS doubt or malignancy

Histology

"Chinese letters" / "alphabet soup"curvilinear irregular woven bone trabeculae in fibrous stroma (fibroblast proliferation)

No osteoblastic rimming (✓ rimming = osteofibrous dysplasia)

Grossyellow/tan-white, gritty/sandy · cartilage in ~10% · cysts in older lesions

Classification

Type Definition Share
Monostotic Single bone 70%
Polyostotic Multiple bones 30%

"Touch-me-not" lesions (TO)

Definitionincidental, X-ray-diagnostic, need no biopsy or intervention · myositis ossificans, cortical desmoid, enchondroma, osteochondroma, FD, NOF, Paget, fibrous cortical defect, intraosseous lipoma, bone island, bone infarct · not osteoblastoma (painful, needs treatment)

Differential

Multiple lesions, young Observation alone Bisphosphonates help
FD ✓ ✓ ✓
EG ✓ ✓ —
Ollier/Maffucci · MHE · NOF/Jaffe-Campanacci ✓ ✓ —
Lymphoma · leukaemia · haemangioendothelioma ✓ — —
Paget — ✓ ✓ (>50 yr, coarse trabeculae)
Metastasis · myeloma — — ✓

Osteofibrous dysplasiacortical, anterior tibia, osteoblastic rimming

Adamantinomaanterior tibial cortex, epithelial nests

Not seen in FD (TO)Harrison groove (rickets) · ✓ rind sign, Chinese letters, café-au-lait

5Management

Fibrous dysplasia
  • Asymptomatic, low fracture risk→OBSERVE (6-monthly exam + X-ray to maturity)
  • Symptomatic pain, polyostotic, low fracture risk→BISPHOSPHONATE (IV, off-label)
  • Fracture within acceptable limits (UE, tibia, child)→IMMOBILISE ± CLOSED REDUCTION
  • Impending / pathologic fracture, weight-bearing→LONG INTRAMEDULLARY NAIL ± OSTEOTOMY
    • IM nail contraindicated→TITANIUM LOCKING PLATE (whole bone) + graft
  • Coxa vara / shepherd's crook→VALGUS (intertrochanteric) OSTEOTOMY + FIXATION

Treatment (pathologic fracture, shepherd's crook, bisphosphonates)

IM nailgold standard (load-sharing) · long nail to protect whole bone · elastic nails if canal small · for recalcitrant pain, impending/actual fracture, progressive deformity

Platetitanium (modulus closer to bone) locking, longest plate, maximum screws · IM device > plate in lower limb

Graft choicecalcium phosphate cement (longest resorption) · cortical allograft > cancellous (slower resorption) · not autograft → resorbed, converts to dysplastic bone

Isolated curettage + graftpoor (resorption, fracture, reoperation) · only low-risk monostotic upper-limb lesions

Polyostotic + femoral neck path fracture (>2/3 diameter)biopsy, curettage, bone grafting + lesion-spanning internal fixation
✗ Wrong options: observation · RT · percutaneous screws (soft lateral cortex)

5-yr-old, painful coxa varavalgus osteotomy + internal fixation
✗ Wrong options: curettage + allograft · observation · spica

Symptomatic adult, no fracture riskbisphosphonate
✗ Wrong options: RFA · HIFU (osteoid osteoma) · steroid injection (UBC) · curettage + autograft

Craniofacialrecontouring / curettage · radiotherapy contraindicated (malignant transformation) · calcitonin unsuccessful

6Complications

Pathologic fracture50% of humeral lesions ≥1 fracture · 50% of monostotic patients · humerus risk: >50% cortical involvement, >30 mm, cystic degeneration · femur: large neck/trochanteric lesion

Coxa vara classification

Type Region Neck-shaft angle Share
1 Neck + trochanter Normal 120–140° 24%
2 Neck + trochanter Valgus >140° 6%
3 Neck + trochanter Varus <120° 7%
4 Lateral bowing of proximal shaft Normal 20%
5 Lateral bowing Valgus 14%
6 Lateral bowing Varus 29%

Coursetypes 1–2 stable, types 3–6 progress · goal: NSA 120–140°, correct bowing, apex-anterior + rotation, total-bone load-sharing fixation

Malignant transformation

Risk ~1%higher in polyostotic (MAS, Mazabraud), after radiotherapy

Tumoursosteosarcoma (commonest) > fibrosarcoma > MFH · usually diaphysis, decades later

Clinicalnew pain, swelling, mass, ↑ ALP · >50% mortality (worse than primary sarcoma)

Scoliosis40% in polyostotic

✗ Wrong options: enchondroma (rings/arcs) · UBC · infarct

"Polyostotic FD, new pain at 39?"osteosarcoma (malignant transformation)

✗ Wrong options: EG · plasmacytoma · spina ventosa

Focal Fibrocartilaginous Dysplasia

Pathology · 21 / 66

1Basics

Naturerare, benign tumour-like bone dysplasia · aetiology unknown

Ageinfants and toddlers

Sitetibia commonest · also humerus, ulna/forearm, phalanx, femur

Associationa variant of infantile tibia vara that can resolve without surgery (vs Blount)

3Presentation

Unilateral tibia varapainless bowing in a walking toddler

Gaitknee hyperextension with lateral thrust

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (pathognomonic)abrupt varus at the metaphyseal–diaphyseal junction · medial cortical sclerosis · lucency in the medial cortex just proximal to it (fibrocartilaginous tissue)

Bone scanshows deformity, not "hot" (not aggressive)

MRIonly in ambiguous cases

Histology

Dense bland fibrous tissuelayers of collagenous fibrous tissue + thick dense fibrocartilage · no malignant features

5Management

Focal fibrocartilaginous dysplasia
  • Most (tibia)→OBSERVATION (± orthosis) — spontaneous correction with physeal growth
  • Persistent / progressive deformity, joint function compromised→CORRECTIVE OSTEOTOMY (or curettage)

Resolution~half of tibial cases resolve spontaneously · much less often in femur and humerus

Osteotomy riskssecondary tibia valgus, neurologic injury

X vs Y

FFCD Infantile Blount
Side Unilateral Often bilateral
Apex Metaphyseal–diaphyseal junction, medial cortical lucency + sclerosis Proximal medial physis / metaphyseal beak
Natural history Often resolves Often progresses

How it's asked

"18-month-old, unilateral bowing, medial metaphyseal cortical lucency, bland fibrous histology?"focal fibrocartilaginous dysplasia
✗ Wrong options: NF · toddler's fracture · NOF · ABC

"Treatment?"observation · osteotomy only if persistent/progressive

Giant Cell Tumor

Pathology · 22 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Naturebenign but locally aggressive · 3–5% of primary bone tumours · 1.7/million

Demographicsfemales (unlike most bone tumours) · 30–50 (Orthobullets) · 20–40 / 22–40 (OB, TO) · skeletally mature, closed physes (immature → metaphyseal)

Multicentric1–2% (TO)

Anatomic distribution

Orderdistal femur > proximal tibia > distal radius > sacral ala · 50% around the knee

Axialsacrum commonest axial site (10% sacrum + spine) · spine → vertebral body
✗ Wrong options: posterior elements

Othersproximal humerus · phalanges of hand · apophysis (like chondroblastoma) · proximal fibula

Not typicaldiaphysis (e.g. tibial shaft)

"Extra-articular GCT" (TO)GCT of tendon sheath (giant cell synovioma) → extremities (hand/wrist flexor tendons) · firm painless nodule · excision

Cells

Cell Identity
Type I — mononuclear stromal cell Neoplastic cell · fibroblast-like, mesenchymal stem-cell features · overexpresses RANKL · carries H3F3A (H3.3 G34W) mutation
Type II — monocyte/macrophage Recruited from blood · giant-cell precursor
Type III — multinucleated giant cell Hallmark · osteoclast-like (TRAP, carbonic anhydrase II, cathepsin K, vacuolar ATPase) · non-neoplastic bystander · express RANK

Genes & lab markers

H3F3A (H3.3 G34W)driver mutation in stromal cells (TO)

RANKL overexpression by stromal cells — key pathogenic pathway → denosumab target

c-myc / p53 alterations — in metastatic lesions (lung mets overexpress c-myc)

Telomeric associations80% · IGF-I/II activity in type II/III cells

Brown tumourmust check serum calcium / PTH (hyperparathyroidism, multiple lesions)

3Presentation

Paininsidious, activity/night/rest · joint-referred · difficulty walking

Signsmass, tenderness, swelling, effusion (periarticular), ↓ ROM, antalgic gait

Pathologic fracturecommon (subchondral collapse)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayeccentric, purely lytic, epiphyseal–metaphyseal, extends to subchondral bone · no sclerotic rim (or partial) · poorly defined transition · expands/breaks cortex · "neocortex" · no matrix · intra-articular extension rare · soap-bubble appearance (TO — FNB 2021)

MRIextent · dark T1 (clear demarcation from fatty marrow), bright T2, avid enhancement · intermediate T2 signal (like lymphoma, Ewing) · fluid levels = secondary ABC

Bone scanvariable / hot

Chest X-ray or CT chestmandatory staging (lung mets)
✗ Wrong options: bone scan · skeletal survey · SPEP/UPEP · MRI brain/spine · mammogram

Biopsy firsteven when imaging looks classic → incisional biopsy before reconstruction (malignancy can mimic)

Histology

Diagnosticnumerous multinucleated giant cells (40–60 nuclei) in a sea of mononuclear stromal cells · giant-cell nuclei identical to stromal nuclei · storiform areas, reactive bone, foam cells, haemosiderin

Secondary ABC10–14% (Orthobullets/OB) · 20% (TO) · GCT has enhancing solid component (primary ABC doesn't)

Campanacci grading (TO)

Grade Behaviour Radiographic features
I Latent Well-marginated, thin rim of mature bone, cortex intact or slightly thinned
II Active Well-defined, no radiopaque rim, cortex thinned + moderately expanded but present · (II + fracture graded separately)
III Aggressive Fuzzy borders, permeative · soft-tissue mass not contained by bone shell

Enneking (benign)most GCT stage 2; aggressive = 3

Differential

Epiphyseal Usually curettage + graft
GCT ✓ ✓
Chondroblastoma (epiphyseal) ✓ ✓
ABC · osteoblastoma · CMF — ✓

Chondroblastomaopen physes, chicken-wire calcification, marked oedema, S-100

Brown tumourmultiple lesions, abnormal calcium

Giant-cell-rich / telangiectatic / fibroblastic OSatypia + osteoid

Chordomasacral midline, physaliferous cells

ABCfluid levels, USP6 · UBC — no cortical destruction

5Management

Giant cell tumour of bone
  • Joint salvageable (most; Campanacci I–II, many III)→EXTENDED CURETTAGE + BURR + ADJUVANT + PMMA/GRAFT ± FIXATION
    (pathologic fracture is NOT a contraindication)
  • Structural loss / collapsed subchondral bone→EN BLOC RESECTION + RECONSTRUCTION
    • Expendable bone (proximal fibula, distal ulna)→RESECTION ± LCL/biceps repair
    • Vertebral body→TOTAL EN BLOC SPONDYLECTOMY
  • Unresectable (sacrum, spine, pelvis) / multiply recurrent→DENOSUMAB ± EMBOLIZATION (RT last resort)
  • Hand with cortical breakthrough→INTERCALARY RESECTION or RAY AMPUTATION

Treatment — extended curettage with adjuvants

Preferred treatmentextended curettage + adjuvants + reconstruction (TO FNB 2024 — key not given; sources' standard)
✗ Wrong options: curettage alone · curettage + graft · primary RT

Techniquelarge cortical window (≥ lesion size) — no curetting under overhanging ridges · head lamp, dental mirror, angled curettes · high-speed burr 1–2 cm in all directions · pulsatile lavage · don't perforate subchondral plate

Most important stepaggressive curettage with high-speed burr (no adjuvant proven superior)

Adjuvantsphenol, liquid nitrogen (cryo), hydrogen peroxide, argon beam, electrocautery, ethanol (= phenol) · burr alone recurrence 12%

FillingPMMA (thermal necrosis; recurrence seen as lucency at bone–cement interface) · graft · ± internal fixation · not DBM + marrow aspirate (higher recurrence)

Treatment Local recurrence
Curettage + graft alone 20–40% (OB: 15–25%)
+ adjuvant 3–10%
High-speed burr only 12%
Overall ~20%

Distal radius, cortices + joint intactcurettage + liquid nitrogen + PMMA
✗ Wrong options: en bloc + allograft · fusion with fibula · RT

Handno breakthrough → curettage + cementing · breakthrough → intercalary resection (free fibula) or amputation (lowest recurrence)

En bloc resection

Lateral condyle with collapsed subchondral bone + fracturecondylar resection + osteoarticular allograft
✗ Wrong options: cement injection · chemo · RT · amputation

Proximal fibulaen bloc resection + repair LCL + biceps femoris to lateral tibia → lowest recurrence, low instability
✗ Wrong options: curettage · structural allograft · denosumab (high recurrence) · observation

Kneehemicondylar osteoarticular allograft or rotating-hinge endoprosthesis · distal radius aggressive → resection + proximal fibular autograft (arthroplasty or arthrodesis) · distal ulna/clavicle → resection alone

Spinetotal en bloc spondylectomy (± staged anterior/posterior, cage/cement) → 10-yr disease-free up to 100%

Denosumab

Drugfully human IgG2 monoclonal antibody against RANKL (receptor activator of nuclear factor kappa-B ligand) (mimics OPG) · FDA approved for unresectable GCT / when resection → severe morbidity, adults + skeletally mature

Option Identity
Denosumab ✓ Human mAb → RANKL
Osteoprotegerin Decoy receptor → RANKL
Infliximab Chimeric mAb → TNF-α
Rituximab → CD20
Trastuzumab → HER2
(none) mAb or decoy → RANK itself

Mechanism (TO)kills/prevents osteoclast-like giant cells · stromal (neoplastic) H3F3A clone survives, quiescent
✗ Wrong options: directly kills stromal cells · halts stromal proliferation · OPG antagonist

Effect85–90% giant-cell destruction · sclerosis, cortical reconstitution, pain relief

Histology after denosumab (TO)loss of giant cells + dense fibro-osseous tissue, new woven bone, stromal spindling (mimics low-grade fibro-osseous lesion)

Dosing (TO)120 mg SC days 1, 8, 15, 29 → every 4 weeks · neoadjuvant 3–6 mo · calcium + vitamin D mandatory

Neoadjuvant downside (TO)sclerotic, ossified margin obscures tumour boundary → ↑ recurrence after curettage (20–30% vs 10–15%)

Stopping (TO)rebound RANKL surge → rapid recurrence + severe rebound hypercalcaemia (3–9 mo) · recurrence 20–60% · mitigate with zoledronic acid, tapering, calcium monitoring

Long-term (TO)1–6% malignant transformation, ↑ with prolonged therapy · ONJ 1–5% · atypical femoral fracture (>2 yr)

Side effectshypocalcaemia (contraindication: severe hypocalcaemia), nasopharyngitis, arthralgia

Bisphosphonatesosteoclast inhibitors · postop → ↓ recurrence 25–30% (TO: used to prevent recurrence) · zoledronic acid stabilises inoperable disease · denosumab > bisphosphonate
✗ Wrong options: calcitonin · teriparatide · methotrexate

Radiation & embolization

Radiationonly inoperable / multiply recurrent (sacrum, large vertebral) · ~15% malignant transformation (megavoltage)

Embolizationunresectable sacral/pelvic GCT, monthly until palliation · preop to ↓ blood loss

Not usedchemotherapy · RFA

6Complications

Lung metastasis & chest CT

"Benign" metastasizing tumourlung mets histologically identical to primary (also chondroblastoma)

Source Lung metastasis rate
Orthobullets 2–4% (1–6% on chest CT)
OB ~2% overall, 6% if recurrent → counsel ~4% · 1–9% · ~5%
TO ~3%
Spine GCT series 13.7%

Risk factorslocal recurrence · aggressive / stage 3 (Campanacci III) (TO) · wrist/hand, distal radius, proximal femur, sacrum/spine · younger · immunocompromised

Timing18–24 mo, most within 3 yr

Treatmentresect (metastasectomy) → ~76–80% disease-free · denosumab · RT

Mortality with lung mets~15% (TO) · 17% (Orthobullets) · 5-yr disease-free 76%

Recurrenceusually benign GCT again (identical histology) · diagnose with CT-guided biopsy · lucency at cement interface

Malignant transformation

Rates<1% (Orthobullets) · 1.4–6.6% (OB) · <5% overall malignancy · radiation → ~15%

Primary vs secondary malignant GCTsecondary after radiation or multiple resections · latency 9 yr after RT, 19 yr spontaneous

Late lytic change years after curettage + biopsy shows high-grade sarcomawide resection + reconstruction (+ medical oncology)
✗ Wrong options: repeat curettage · denosumab

Pathologic fractureperiarticular lesions · cryotherapy (liquid nitrogen) → fracture + vascular injury, wound/nerve problems (phenol too)

Chemotherapy response grading (Huvos)

(osteosarcoma necrosis grading, filed under this topic's TO set)

Huvos grade Tumour necrosis Response
I 0–50% Poor (little/none)
II 51–90% Poor (partial)
III 91–99% Good
IV 100% Complete

88% necrosis → Huvos II, poor responder → continue standard MAP (EURAMOS-1: changing regimen doesn't help) (TO)

Giant cells Nuclei = stromal nuclei Scattered + chondroblasts Haemosiderin, osteoclasts
Labs Normal Normal ↑ Ca, ↑ PTH
Treatment Curettage + adjuvant / denosumab Curettage + graft / RFA Treat hyperparathyroidism

Glomus Tumor

Pathology · 23 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Naturerare benign tumour (hamartoma) of the glomus body · formerly "paraganglioma" · soft tissue and/or bone · malignant cases rare

Age / sex20–40 (3rd–4th decades) · females

Sitehand 75% · subungual 50% · palm, wrist, forearm, foot less often

Boneerosion of distal phalanx in 50% · primary intraosseous rare

Anatomy

Glomus bodyperivascular temperature-regulating structure (arteriovenous shunt) at digit tip / beneath nail

3Presentation

Classic triadparoxysmal pain · exquisite point tenderness · cold intolerance (cold water worsens pain)

Signssmall bluish nodule (often invisible subungually) · nail ridging / discoloration · may show no mass or colour change

Delaydiagnosis often delayed for years

Clinical tests

Test Method Accuracy
Love test Pinhead pressure on the spot → excruciating pain, none just beside it 100% sensitive, 78% accurate
Hildreth test Tourniquet inflation → pain + Love-test tenderness abolished 92% sensitive, 91% specific
Cold sensitivity Cold water exacerbates pain —

Love test diagnostic ofglomus tumour (TO)
✗ Wrong options: trochanteric bursitis · hydatid cyst · SLE

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raypressure erosion of distal phalanx, cortical deformity · intraosseous → cystic erosive change · often normal

MRIestablishes diagnosis · T1 low, T2 high, small enhancing subungual nodule

Histology

Well-defined, no atypia or mitoses · uniform small round cells with dark nuclei and pink cytoplasm (epithelioid glomus cells) surrounding small vascular spaces in hyaline/myxoid stroma · gland-like nests

Differential

Lesion Distinguishing
Glomus tumour Triad, MRI nodule, glomus cells
Raynaud phenomenon Cold sensitivity, no mass
Epidermal inclusion cyst Prior trauma, less point tenderness / cold pain
Osteomyelitis Labs, systemic, X-ray changes
Osteoid osteoma Cortical nidus + sclerosis
Melanoma · SCC · synovial / epithelioid sarcoma Different histology

5Management

Marginal excisioncurative · for symptoms affecting quality of life · complete pain relief

Nail bed reconstructionautologous fat graft for large defects

Locally aggressive / intraosseous destructiveaggressive resection, even amputation suggested

6Complications

Recurrence~20% (Orthobullets) · "uncommon" (benign) — usually incomplete excision

How it's asked

"20-yr-old, painful ecchymotic ring fingertip, eroded phalanx, dark-nucleated round cells?"glomus tumour
✗ Wrong options: melanoma · SCC · synovial/epithelioid sarcoma

"43-yr-old, cold-sensitive fingertip pain, ulnar nail-bed tenderness, normal X-ray, MRI nodule?"glomus tumour
✗ Wrong options: Raynaud · inclusion cyst · osteomyelitis · osteoid osteoma

"Little-finger tip pain worse with cold — histology vascular spaces with epithelioid cells?"glomus tumour
✗ Wrong options: schwannoma · scleroderma · paronychia

"Love test is diagnostic of?"glomus tumour (TO)

"Treatment?"marginal excision

Hemangioma

Pathology · 24 / 66

1Basics

Naturebenign vascular tumour of bone

Sitevertebral bodies and craniofacial bones · pelvis (ilium) · may be multifocal

3Presentation

Usually asymptomaticincidental

Symptomaticpain or pathologic fracture

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings — vertebral haemangioma

Modality Sign
Plain X-ray "Jailhouse" / "jail-bar" / "honeycomb" — lytic body with vertical striations (thickened vertical trabeculae) (TO)
Sagittal / coronal CT "Corduroy" appearance (TO)
Axial CT "Polka dot" (salt-and-pepper) — white dots on black fatty background (TO)
MRI "Mottled" — high T1 (fat) + high T2 (vascular), black punctate/linear signal voids · retains signal on STIR/fat-sat (unlike fatty marrow) (TO)
Bone scan Warm to hot

Biopsyalmost never required (imaging characteristic) (TO)

Why these signsnormal cancellous bone replaced by thickened vertical trabeculae surrounded by fat marrow / vascular lacunae

Histology

Cavernous lesion with numerous thin-walled blood vessels · small vascular channels lined by single layer of endothelium · no giant cells (vs ABC, Paget), no granulomas (vs sarcoid)

Differential of the spine/ilium lesion

Lesion Distinguishing
Haemangioma Organised vertical striations, polka dots
Paget disease Square, enlarged vertebra, thick cortex ("picture frame"), disorganised mineral
GCT Lytic, destructive
Metastasis · myeloma Lytic/sclerotic, no organisation
Chordoma (sacral) Sacrum, midline, physaliferous cells
ABC Fluid levels, giant cells
Sarcoid Granulomas

Soft-tissue haemangioma (forearm)

Phleboliths on X-ray + intramuscular mass, ↑ T1 (fat), heterogeneous T2 + enhancement → soft-tissue haemangioma
✗ Wrong options: synovial sarcoma · liposarcoma · rhabdomyosarcoma

5Management

Haemangioma of bone
  • Asymptomatic→OBSERVATION
  • Symptomatic + accessible→CURETTAGE + BONE GRAFT (intralesional excision)
  • Symptomatic + inaccessible→LOW-DOSE RADIATION 25–40 Gy

How it's asked

"Polka dot sign on CT?"vertebral haemangioma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: clavicle osteomyelitis · cortical OS · lymphoma

"Jailhouse appearance is seen on?"plain X-ray of vertebral haemangioma (TO)

"Corduroy appearance is seen on?"sagittal CT of vertebral haemangioma (TO)

"Mottled appearance on MRI?"vertebral haemangioma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: marrow oedema · CML · metastasis

"Axial CT with mottled/polka-dot vertebra?"vertebral haemangioma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: myeloma · metastasis · LCH

"D12 corduroy on CT (FNB 2024)?"key not given · vertebral haemangioma per the sign (TO)
✗ Wrong options: blastic mets · myeloma · Paget

"L3 vertical striations on sagittal CT?"haemangioma
✗ Wrong options: Paget · GCT · metastasis · chordoma

"30-yr-old, iliac lytic lesion with coarse trabeculae, thin-walled endothelial channels?"haemangioma of bone
✗ Wrong options: ABC · sarcoid · Paget

"7-yr-old, forearm mass with phleboliths, fat on T1?"haemangioma

Hemangioma of Soft Tissue

Pathology · 25 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Naturebenign vascular neoplasm · error in new formation of a vascular segment · hormones modulate · 20% trauma-associated

Demographics<30 yr · M = F

Sitecutaneous, subcutaneous, intramuscular (deep, lower limb) · hand common · usually solitary · diffuse haemangioma = rare childhood form involving a whole limb

Typescapillary (superficial) vs cavernous (deep)

Tissue of originvascular
✗ Wrong options: cartilage, bone, smooth or skeletal muscle

3Presentation

Pain varies with activityengorgement → aching, heaviness, swelling · worse when dependent, standing, exercising, swimming · relieved by rest, elevation, ice

Mass size variesfills after minutes of standing → examine supine and standing · may be warm · birthmark that enlarges

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayphleboliths (calcified thrombi in vessels) · bone erosion · may be normal

MRI with gadoliniumdiagnostic study · separates from AVM and angiosarcoma · T1 iso- to hyperintense (fat) · T2 hyperintense · low-signal foci = flow voids / calcification · heterogeneous vessels + fatty infiltration → "bag of worms" · no necrosis, no rim enhancement, no mass effect

Fat on T1 + calcification on X-ray → diagnostic of haemangioma

Histology

No malignant cells, no pleomorphism · multiple dilated endothelial-lined vascular channels filled with erythrocytes · intramuscular type: lumens infiltrated by (striated) muscle fibres · cavernous: large vessels + fat

Grossred to tan to yellow

Differential

Lesion Distinguishing
Haemangioma Activity-related pain/size, phleboliths, fat on T1
Low-flow venous malformation Congenital, multifocal, infiltrative, adolescence (hormonal), compressible, phleboliths, interspersed fat
AVM High flow · diagnose by exam + imaging (phleboliths, Doppler flow) · multidisciplinary / IR
Synovial sarcoma Painless, calcification, different T1
Lipoma Homogeneous bright T1, suppresses
Desmoid Dark T1 + T2, no fat
PVNS Similar imaging, different pain · haemosiderin giant cells
Parosteal OS Stuck on posterior distal femur
Muscle tear haematoma Should resolve over 3 months

5Management

Soft-tissue haemangioma / vascular malformation
  • First line (incl. children, incidental)→OBSERVE + NSAIDs + COMPRESSION STOCKINGS + ACTIVITY MODIFICATION
  • Large, painful, failed conservative→IR: SCLEROTHERAPY (ethanol, US-guided) or EMBOLIZATION
  • Small hand lesion failing conservative→MARGINAL EXCISION
  • Recalcitrant, large→WIDE RESECTION (high local recurrence)

AVM failing conservative careconsult interventional radiology (coil embolization / sclerotherapy)
✗ Wrong options: CT CAP staging · biopsy · chemo · RT

Not usedchemotherapy · radiation

Recurrence after excisionhigh · deep lesions abut NV structures → often intralesional

6Complications

Kasabach-Merritt syndromeplatelet trapping → consumptive coagulopathy, potentially fatal

Infantile haemangiomasinvolute by age 7

Malignant transformationnone

How it's asked

"30-yr-old, painful posterior knee mass enlarging with activity, calcification + fat on T1?"haemangioma
✗ Wrong options: synovial sarcoma · lipoma · parosteal OS · PVNS

"17-yr-old, enlarging hand birthmark, pain when dependent/swimming, phleboliths?"benign haemangioma
✗ Wrong options: angiosarcoma · epithelioid sarcoma · GCT tendon sheath

"Shoulder mass painful + warm after activity, normal X-ray, fat on MRI?"intramuscular haemangioma
✗ Wrong options: synovial sarcoma · metastasis · haematoma · abscess

"Tissue of origin?"vascular

"Multiple infiltrative 'bag of worms' masses with fat in a teen?"low-flow venous malformation
✗ Wrong options: desmoid · lipoma · MPNST · schwannoma

"Calf mass enlarging at end of day — histology + treatment?"vascular dilations with erythrocytes + striated muscle · observation

"Foot AVM failing stockings + activity change?"consult interventional radiology

"Incidental intramuscular gastrocnemius haemangioma?"observation
✗ Wrong options: wide/marginal resection · RT · chemo

Heterotopic Ossification

Pathology · 26 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Definitionbone in atypical extraskeletal soft tissue · after local trauma, neurologic injury (within 2 months of brain/spinal cord injury), THA/TKA, or spontaneous

DemographicsM:F 2:1 · men with hypertrophic OA, women >65

Commonest locationbetween muscle and joint capsule

Injury Order of joints
TBI / stroke Hip > elbow > shoulder > knee · spastic side · elbow HO commoner after brain injury · knee rare
Spinal cord injury Hip > knee > elbow > shoulder · hip flexors + abductors · medial knee

Subtypesneurogenic HO · traumatic myositis ossificans · fibrodysplasia ossificans progressiva (Münchmeyer)

Pathophysiology

Mechanismactivation/proliferation of mesenchymal stem cells → osteoprogenitors · genetic predisposition · experimental HO linked to BMP expression

Risk factors

Risk factor Detail Incidence
Injury Severity Score Highest direct correlation with severe HO after knee dislocation (✗ Wrong options: GCS · timing · number of ligaments · open reconstruction) 11%
TBI Spastic limbs · prolonged coma (20–30 yr), prolonged ventilation 11%
Spinal cord injury Complete > incomplete 20%
Decubitus ulcers + SCI/TBI — 70%
Burns Local and remote —
Amputation through zone of injury Blast mechanism worst 63%
Antegrade femoral nail Piriformis entry 25%
Distal femoral traction pin Large Steinmann pin → quadriceps HO (rare) (✗ Wrong options: proximal tibia, calcaneus, radius, metacarpal) Rare
Acetabular approach Extended iliofemoral > Kocher-Langenbeck > ilioinguinal · combined approaches → HO is the commonest complication 25%
THA Psoas tenotomy, cementless (debris, marrow spillage) · Smith-Petersen and Hardinge > transtrochanteric > posterior (lowest) · DA "bikini" incision ↓ high-grade HO (4% vs 12%) 53% (significant 5%) · symptomatic ~7%
TKA Anterior femoral notching, quadriceps trauma, periosteal stripping, MUA, high lumbar BMD · ankylosing spondylitis (~20%) —
Other DISH, hypertrophic OA, previous HO —
Not proven Open fractures · fluorosis (TO) —

THA risk groups (TO)high: previous HO, hypertrophic post-traumatic arthritis, men with hypertrophic OA · moderate: AS, DISH, Paget, Parkinson, RA, unilateral hypertrophic OA

Genes & lab markers

Marker Use
ALP >250 IU/L ↑ early, nonspecific · ↑ at 12 wk post-op predicts HO · cannot determine maturity (OB) · normalization used in timing (Orthobullets)
CRP Tracks inflammatory activity better than ESR · normalization ≈ maturity
ESR >35 mm/h At 12 wk after THA predicts HO
CK Extent of muscle involvement

3Presentation

Painless loss of ROM → ADL interference · ankylosis

Earlywarm, painful, swollen joint, effusion, fever (mimics infection/DVT) · CRPS association

Complicationsnerve impingement (ulnar neuropathy at elbow) · contractures → decubitus ulcers · skin maceration · pathologic fracture of osteoporotic bone · TKA: quadriceps snapping, patellar instability

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Modality Role
Triphasic bone scan Best for early diagnosis, most used · decreasing activity = maturing
Ultrasound Early hip HO — echogenic surface + acoustic shadow
X-ray Not early (calcium appears 7–10 days; may be normal ~3 wk) · maturity = cortex + sharp demarcation + trabeculae · Judet views for hip
CT (3D) Preoperative planning · characterize synostosis

Histologymature trabecular (lamellar) bone + fatty marrow

Brooker classification (HO after THA)

Class Description
1 Islands of bone in soft tissue
2 Spurs from pelvis/femur, ≥1 cm between opposing surfaces
3 Spurs, gap <1 cm
4 Apparent bony ankylosis

Brooker 1–2don't affect THA outcome · 3–4 → pain, ↓ ROM

5Management

Prophylaxis (radiation, indomethacin)

Indomethacin75 mg/day for 10 days to 6 weeks · proven for distal biceps repair (radioulnar synostosis)
✗ Wrong options: 7.5 mg

Radiationsingle 700 cGy · 4 h preop or within 72 h postop · <550 cGy ineffective · blocks mesenchymal → osteoprogenitor differentiation
✗ Wrong options: 500 cGy · 2 wk preop · fractionated after

Bisphosphonatesnot effective: only delay mineralization of osteoid · HO forms when stopped

Distal bicepssingle-incision technique → lower synostosis rate than two-incision

Not prophylaxisPTH 1-34, VAC chemo

Timing of excision & maturity

Established, symptomatic HO
  • Immature (<6 mo, bone scan active, ALP/CRP raised)→OBSERVE, PT, re-evaluate
  • Mature + severe ROM loss / function→WIDE EXPOSURE + EXCISION (CT 3D first)
    • Post-op→INDOMETHACIN (5 d) and/or RT + EARLY GENTLE ROM
  • HO after THA/TKA, Brooker 3–4→EXCISION (± revision)
Setting Wait before excision
General trauma 6 months
Spinal cord injury 1 year
TBI 1.5 years
THA 6–18 months to mature

Maturityradiographs: sharp cortical margins ✓ · marked ↓ bone scan activity + normal ALP (Orthobullets) · (✗ Wrong options: time alone · bone scan always negative · ALP (OB)) · some data: early = late results

Elbow HO 2 months after TBI with mild ulnar symptomscontinue, re-evaluate in 3 months
✗ Wrong options: excision ± transposition · RT · isolated release

Terrible triad, synostosis at 3 monthsawait maturation → CT 3D in 3 months
✗ Wrong options: indomethacin/RT now · radial head excision

Revision THA, 18 months, painful restricted hipexcision
✗ Wrong options: PT · alendronate · indomethacin · RT (none treat established HO)

Observation caseHO 4 months after THA (still maturing)
✗ Wrong options: mature lesions with functional loss

6Complications

Surgicalhaematoma/bleeding · infection (↑ in arthroplasty with HO) · fracture of osteoporotic bone · AVN with extensive stripping

Recurrence∝ severity of neurological injury

How it's asked

"Highest correlation with severe HO after knee dislocation?"Injury Severity Score

"Amputee at highest HO risk?"through the zone of injury after blast (propane explosion, transfemoral)

"Traction pin causing HO?"distal femur (quadriceps)

"NOT shown to increase HO?"open fractures (✓ ventilation, SCI, blast amputation, burns)

"NOT a risk factor after THR?"fluorosis (✓ previous HO, hypertrophic OA, DISH) (TO)

"Commonest complication of combined-approach acetabular fixation?"HO

"Ankylosing spondylitis TKA — likely complication?"HO

"DA THA 'bikini' vs longitudinal incision?"lower HO
✗ Wrong options: less LFCN injury · more wound problems

"What could have prevented hip HO after head injury + fracture fixation?"700 cGy within 72 h of surgery

"Prevent radioulnar synostosis after distal biceps repair?"indomethacin 75 mg daily × 6 wk

"True about elbow HO 4 months post-injury?"radiographs assess maturity
✗ Wrong options: early excision ↓ recurrence · ALP for maturity

"Painful Brooker 3–4 HO 18 months after revision THA?"excision

"Elbow HO 2 months after TBI with mild ulnar paraesthesia?"re-evaluate in 3 months

"Terrible triad, forearm stuck at 3 months?"allow maturation, CT 3D in 3 months

"Which HO patient can simply be observed?"4 months after THA

"Brooker classification is for?"HO around hip after THR (TO)

Impending Fracture & Prophylactic Fixation

Pathology · 27 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology & prognosis

Commonest site of all bone metastasesspine

Commonest site of pathologic fracture from metastasisproximal femur (femur = commonest long bone; stress risers)
✗ Wrong options: distal femur · humerus · thoracic spine

Upper limb20% of skeletal mets · 50% of those in the humerus

Shortest survival after pathologic fracturelung (and melanoma): median <6 months, 5-yr <5%
✗ Wrong options: thyroid · prostate · breast · renal

Why fix prophylactically

Prophylactic > fixing an actual fractureshorter operation · less morbidity · quicker recovery

Fracture-risk predictorssignificant functional pain · >50% cortical destruction · formal systems: Harrington and Mirels

4Diagnosis & Classification

Mirels scoring

Purposequantify risk of impending pathologic fracture in a neoplastic bone lesion (TO)
✗ Wrong options: spinal operability · Scheuermann

Score 1 2 3
Site Upper limb Lower limb Peritrochanteric
Pain Mild Moderate Functional
Lesion Blastic Mixed Lytic
Size (fraction of cortical thickness) <1/3 1/3–2/3 >2/3

Threshold>8 → prophylactic fixation (Orthobullets, TO) · "8 or higher" (OB) · one OB explanation says ">9" — read the options · ≤8 painful → radiation

Worked examples

Case Score Action
Breast mets, proximal humerus, moderate pain, blastic, 1/3–2/3 1 + 2 + 1 + 2 = 6 Radiation
RCC, humerus, severe (functional) pain, lytic, >2/3 1 + 3 + 3 + 3 = 10 Fix
Myeloma, peritrochanteric, functional pain, lytic, >2/3 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 = 12 Prophylactic fixation + RT

Harrington criteria

Useprophylactic fixation of impending pathologic fracture of the proximal femur (TO)
✗ Wrong options: scoliosis · Blount stapling · proximal humeral fracture

Criterion
>50% destruction of diaphyseal cortices
>50–75% destruction of metaphysis (>2.5 cm)
Permeative destruction of subtrochanteric femur
Persistent pain after irradiation

Limitation (TO)not validated · proximal femur only

5Management

Treatment algorithm

Impending pathologic fracture
  • No proven primary with biopsy-proven bone mets→BIOPSY FIRST
    • Primary bone sarcoma→CLOSE, REFER TO SARCOMA CENTRE (no nailing)
  • Mirels ≤8, painful→RADIATION
  • Mirels >8 → PROPHYLACTIC FIXATION (protect whole bone)→POST-OP RADIATION

Biopsymay need a separate incision from nail incision · nailing a sarcoma contaminates the whole bone → loses limb salvage

Goals of fixationimmediate mobilization + weight bearing · protect the entire bone · implant matched to prognosis

Prophylactic fixation by site

Site Fixation
Proximal humerus (Bickel I) Endoprosthesis (✗ Wrong options: IM nail (poor hold in metaphyseal flare) · plate (thin cortex))
Humeral diaphysis (Bickel II) IM nail · resection + intercalary spacer · plate + screws (less preferred)
Distal humerus (Bickel III) Flexible nails · elbow replacement
Femoral neck / head Cemented hemiarthroplasty (fails by dislocation, infection)
Peri-/subtrochanteric Cephalomedullary (IM) nail (fails by nonunion, AVN) (✗ Wrong options: proximal femoral replacement (dissection, weakness, infection, dislocation) · curettage alone (stress riser))

Post-operative radiation↓ pain, slows progression, treats residual tumour (✓ for myeloma lesion after fixation)

How it's asked

"Mirels criteria are used for?"quantifying impending fracture risk in a neoplastic lesion (TO)

"Breast mets, proximal humerus, moderate pain, blastic, 1/3–2/3 — Mirels + next step?"6 → radiation
✗ Wrong options: plate at 8 · nail at 7 · replacement

"RCC humeral lytic lesion >2/3, severe pain — Mirels?"10

"Myeloma subtrochanteric lytic lesion, functional pain?"prophylactic internal fixation + radiation (Mirels 12)
✗ Wrong options: RT alone · curettage · proximal femoral replacement

"Commonest site of pathologic fracture from lung mets?"proximal femur

"Primary with shortest survival after pathologic fracture?"lung

"Harrington criteria are used for?"prophylactic fixation of impending proximal femoral fracture (TO)

Intramuscular Myxomas

Pathology · 28 / 66

1Basics

Naturebenign soft-tissue tumour (mesenchymal) · no malignant potential · slow-growing, deep, confined within skeletal muscle

Demographics40–60 (40–70) · slight female predilection

Sitethigh, shoulder, buttock, upper arm, calf

Originprimitive mesenchymal cells → benign fibroblasts that lose the capacity to secrete collagen

Associations

Mazabraud syndromemultiple intramuscular myxomas + (monostotic or polyostotic) fibrous dysplasia

Myxoid liposarcomamain differential

3Presentation

Slowly growing mass · painful or painless · firm, mobile · deep to fascia

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

MRIhomogeneous · T1 dark, T2 bright (myxoid) · intramuscular location · well circumscribed, no oedema or NV encasement · rind of fat at ≥1 pole (bright T1)

Histology

Bland, hypocellular myxoid stroma · undifferentiated stellate cells with thin collagen fibres · no atypia, low N:C ratio, no atypical mitoses

Differential

Lesion Distinguishing
Intramuscular myxoma Intramuscular, homogeneous, fat rind, little enhancement
Myxoid liposarcoma Intermuscular · intense diffuse enhancement · "pulmonary oedema" pattern, lipoblasts, chicken-wire vessels
Neurofibroma Follows nerve — entering-and-exiting nerve sign · ovoid/spindle cells in myxoid stroma
Ganglion Dense fibrous wall around myxoid material, joint origin
Metastasis Soft-tissue mets unusual

5Management

Observationasymptomatic

Marginal excisionsymptomatic lesions · no neoadjuvant chemo/RT · recurrence + metastasis uncommon
✗ Wrong options: observation · PT when symptomatic

Excision margin terms

Margin Plane Example
Intralesional Within pseudocapsule — highest recurrence Select benign lesions
Marginal Through reactive zone / capsule ✓ Intramuscular myxoma
Wide Cuff of normal tissue Sarcomas (myxofibrosarcoma), solitary fibrous tumour
Radical Whole compartment Highly aggressive, no limb-sparing margin

Not marginal excisionABC (curettage + adjuvant) · desmoid (often non-operative, infiltrative)

How it's asked

"40-yr-old, hip/thigh pain, homogeneous intramuscular T2-bright mass, bland myxoid histology?"surgical (marginal) excision
✗ Wrong options: observation · neoadjuvant chemo · adjuvant RT

"56-yr-old, slowly enlarging medial thigh mass, history of thyroid cancer?"intramuscular myxoma
✗ Wrong options: myxoid liposarcoma · neurofibroma · ganglion · metastasis

"Benign tumour treated with marginal excision?"intramuscular myxoma
✗ Wrong options: desmoid · myxofibrosarcoma · solitary fibrous tumour

"Multiple myxomas + fibrous dysplasia?"Mazabraud syndrome

Leiomyosarcoma

Pathology · 29 / 66

1Basics

Natureaggressive sarcoma from smooth muscle cells lining small blood vessels ("leio-" = smooth muscle)

Demographics5th–6th decades

Bone<100 reported cases (extra-facial) · metaphysis of long bones (also diaphysis) · femur, tibia, ilium, humerus

Genes & lab markers

Immunostainssmooth muscle actin +, vimentin +

3Presentation

Symptomsbone pain, palpable firm mass · ~6 months before diagnosis · weight loss

Pelvic masseshard to see on inspection

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raypurely osteolytic, ill-defined, moth-eaten / permeative · intramedullary ± soft-tissue extension

MRIT1 dark (like muscle) · T2 heterogeneous bright · diffuse enhancement

Stagingchest X-ray + chest CT (lung mets) · bone scan uptake

Histology

Spindle cell neoplasm (osseous = soft tissue form) · cigar-shaped (blunt-ended) nuclei · interlacing fascicles with parallel myofibrils · high N:C, mitoses

Round cells?no: leiomyosarcoma is the spindle-cell exception among round-cell tumour lists

5Management

Leiomyosarcoma
  • Localized→EARLY WIDE RESECTION + RECONSTRUCTION
    • Wide margin not achieved / contaminated bed→+ ADJUVANT RADIATION
  • Unplanned excision, margins unknown/positive→RE-EXCISION OF TUMOUR BED (± RT)
  • Diffuse lung mets / unresectable pelvic mass on NV structures→CHEMOTHERAPY

Chemo/RTvariable response · chemo + surgery better than surgery alone · neoadjuvant/adjuvant role controversial

Unplanned excision of 3 cm superficial LMSrepeat wide excision of tumour bed · residual disease in up to 35% · local recurrence 22% vs 7%
✗ Wrong options: observation · RT alone · chemo

6Complications

Prognosis (bone LMS)25% recurrence, 25% metastasis · 75% survival at 3 yr

How it's asked

"35-yr-old, aggressive lytic pelvic lesion, smooth muscle actin +?"leiomyosarcoma histology — interlacing spindle cells
✗ Wrong options: GCT · myeloma · OS · hyaline cartilage

"Outside simple excision of 3 cm thigh mass = leiomyosarcoma, margins unknown?"repeat wide excision of the tumour bed

"Neoplasms with round cells EXCEPT?"leiomyosarcoma (spindle cells, cigar nuclei)

Leukemia

Pathology · 30 / 66

1Basics

Definitionmalignant proliferation of lymphocytes / precursors (or myeloid cells)

Commonest malignancy of childhoodleukaemia (ALL)

Forms

Type Cell Age Key features
ALL Lymphoblasts Peak 4 yr · 80% of (childhood) leukaemia TdT + · bone demineralization, periostitis, lytic lesions · T-ALL teens · B-ALL: t(12;21) children, t(9;22) adults
AML Myeloblasts Older adults (5th–6th decade) Auer rods · marrow failure · APL t(15;17) disrupts retinoic acid receptor → all-trans-retinoic acid · megakaryoblastic → Down syndrome, <5 yr · monocytic → gum infiltration
CML Mature granulocytes 5th–6th decade t(9;22) Philadelphia chromosome → BCR-ABL tyrosine kinase (↑ division, ↓ apoptosis) · smear: immature granulocytes, basophilia, eosinophilia · imatinib
CLL Naive B cells 5th–6th decade Insidious · smudge cells · lymphadenopathy

Genes & lab markers

Marker Leukaemia
TdT + ALL
t(12;21) B-ALL, children
t(9;22) BCR-ABL CML · B-ALL adults
t(15;17) RARα APL (AML)
Auer rods AML
Smudge cells CLL

How it's asked

"4-yr-old, fatigue, fevers, recurrent ear infections, limp, ill-defined medullary lesions, lymphoblasts on smear?"ALL
✗ Wrong options: AML · CML · chronic osteomyelitis · LCH

"Most common location of vertebral fracture in leukaemia?"thoracic (TO)

"Philadelphia chromosome?"t(9;22) BCR-ABL — CML (imatinib)

Lipomas

Pathology · 31 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Common benign tumour of mature fat · ~50% of soft-tissue neoplasms (OB)

Demographics40–60 · slightly male · sedentary individuals

Sitesubcutaneous (upper back, thighs, buttocks, shoulders, arms) · deep/intramuscular (thigh, shoulder, arm) · dorsum of foot · multiple in 5–10%

Variants

Variant Key features Treatment
Spindle cell lipoma Men 45–65, neck/back/shoulder · mature fat + bland spindle cells, collagen, mast cells, myxoid · CD34 +, S-100 − Marginal excision · rarely recurs
Pleomorphic lipoma Middle age · lipocytes, spindle cells, atypical giant cells → mistaken for liposarcoma Marginal excision
Angiolipoma Painful on palpation · small nodules, upper limb · fat + nests of small arborizing vessels Excision
Intramuscular lipoma Often symptomatic · lipoblasts + muscle infiltration · may have infiltrative margins (✗ Wrong options: not malignancy) · small risk → liposarcoma Marginal resection
Hibernoma Brown fat · younger (20–40) · reddish-brown (vascular, mitochondria) Excision

Genes & lab markers

Spindle cell lipomaCD34 +, S-100 −

3Presentation

Painless, mobile, soft mass, long-standing, size plateaus · symptoms from pressure (sitting, shoe wear, tool use) · angiolipoma painful

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

MRI (diagnostic)well-demarcated, homogeneous, signal = subcutaneous fat on ALL sequences · T1 bright, T2 bright, fully suppressed on STIR / fat-sat · no enhancement

X-rayradiolucent soft-tissue shadow · mineralization → think synovial sarcoma

CTwell-demarcated, like subcutaneous fat

Biopsyusually unnecessary · core/incisional prone to sampling error in fatty tumours

Ganglionfluid-bright on T2 (not fat signal) · commonest soft-tissue foot/ankle lesion

Histology

Mature adipocytes, eccentric nuclei, bland acellular stroma, no atypia · gross soft, lobular, yellow/white, ± encapsulated

Atypical lipomatous tumour

Deep (subfascial) or retroperitoneal lipomashigher chance of being atypical lipomatous tumour · retroperitoneum = well-differentiated liposarcoma · extremity = atypical lipoma

MRI iso-intense with fat on all sequences → lipoma / ALT / low-grade liposarcoma → no biopsy, marginal excision · recurrence 25–50% at 10 yr · dedifferentiation rare

Intraosseous (calcaneal) lipoma

"Cockade sign"well-defined lytic calcaneal lesion with central calcification → pathognomonic of intraosseous lipoma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: chondroblastoma · NOF · EG

Sitecalcaneus ~30% (commonest) · subtrochanteric femur · around knee · ~40 yr (TO)

MRIhigh T1 and T2, suppressed on fat-sat (TO)

DDSBC/UBC, ABC, GCT, osteoblastoma, calcaneal pseudotumour (TO)

Milgram stage Histology
I Viable mature lipocytes
II Necrotic foci, foamy macrophages, reactive bone
III Complete necrosis, calcification, cysts (infarct-like)

5Management

Fatty soft-tissue mass, MRI = fat on all sequences
  • Painless, determinate benign→OBSERVATION
  • Symptomatic, rapidly growing, deep/retroperitoneal→MARGINAL EXCISION (no biopsy needed)

Not indicatedcore / incisional biopsy · wide/radical resection · RT · chemo · amputation

6Complications

Local recurrence<5% (Orthobullets) · 15–60% (OB review, lipomatous masses) · 25–50% at 10 yr for ALT

X vs Y

Lipoma Well-diff. liposarcoma / ALT Myxoid liposarcoma
Depth Subcutaneous mostly Deep, retroperitoneal Deep, intermuscular
MRI = fat all sequences Fat + thick septa/nodules T2 bright, myxoid, enhances
Histology Mature fat, no atypia Atypical stromal cells, lipoblasts Lipoblasts, chicken-wire vessels
Treatment Observe / marginal Marginal excision (extremity) Wide resection + RT

How it's asked

"55-yr-old, posterior thigh fullness worse sitting, gluteal mass T1 bright, STIR dark, lipoblasts + muscle?"intramuscular lipoma
✗ Wrong options: dedifferentiated liposarcoma · fibrosarcoma

"Forearm mass, T1 = fat, STIR not bright — histology?"lipoma (mature fat)
✗ Wrong options: myxoid liposarcoma · chondrosarcoma · Ewing

"Symptomatic intramuscular arm lipoma, CT CAP negative?"surgical excision
✗ Wrong options: core biopsy · RT

"Painful dorsal foot fatty mass, no enhancement?"lipoma, marginal excision
✗ Wrong options: wide resection

"Enlarging painful forearm intramuscular lipoma?"marginal resection
✗ Wrong options: core/incisional biopsy

"Large symptomatic axillary/chest wall mass iso-intense to fat?"marginal excision
✗ Wrong options: biopsy · wide + RT

"Shoulder mass, CD34 + spindle cells in fat?"spindle cell lipoma → marginal excision

"Cockade sign?"calcaneal (intraosseous) lipoma (TO)

"Heel pain, lucent calcaneal lesion with central calcification, T1 + T2 bright?"intraosseous lipoma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: ABC · SBC · osteoblastoma

Liposarcoma

Pathology · 32 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rank2nd commonest soft-tissue sarcoma in adults (UPS 1st) · ~20% of adult STS

Demographics50–80 · myxoid in younger adults (30–60, peak 5th decade) · male > female

Sitedeep to fascia (almost never subcutaneous) · 75% extremities (lower > upper) · retroperitoneum

Lipomas do not predispose to liposarcoma

Hallmark cell

Lipoblastsignet-ring cell, indented nucleus, vacuolated cytoplasm · distinguishes from lipoma and UPS

Subtypes & grade

Subtype Grade Share Metastasis
Well-differentiated / ALT Low 40–45% (OB) <1% (ALT ~0%)
Myxoid Low–intermediate ~50% — commonest (Orthobullets/OB) 10–30%
Round cell High (spectrum with myxoid) >50%
Pleomorphic High <5%, most aggressive >50%
Dedifferentiated High <10% · "commonest" per one OB explanation ~50% (highest)

Genes & lab markers

Marker Subtype
MDM2 + CDK4 amplification (12q13–15, ring / giant marker chromosome 12) ALT / WDL and dedifferentiated · CDK4 sens 86%/spec 89%, MDM2 86%/74%
t(12;16) FUS(TLS)–DDIT3(CHOP) Myxoid (± round cell) · rarely t(12;22)
p53 mutation, complex karyotype Pleomorphic

Best test to separate ALT from lipomaFISH for MDM2 amplification
✗ Wrong options: MRI · S-100 (MPNST/melanoma) · CD138 (myeloma) · CD20 (lymphoma)

MDM2negative regulator of p53

Genetic finding Tumour (distractors)
MDM2/CDK4 ✓ ALT/WDL
t(12;16) ✓ Myxoid liposarcoma
t(2;13) Alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma
t(X;18) SYT–SSX Synovial sarcoma
ETV6–NTRK3 Infantile fibrosarcoma
t(12;22) Clear cell sarcoma
t(11;22) Ewing

3Presentation

Slow-growing, painless, deep mass · often 10–20 cm · incidental · mass effect (sciatic paraesthesia, retroperitoneal)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

MRI with contrastbest characterization (sens 100%, spec 71%, PPV 100%)

Subtype MRI
ALT / WDL Like lipoma — fat signal (Orthobullets: bright T1, dark T2) · thick (>2 mm), irregular septa / stranding, nodules, mild enhancement, oedema → dedifferentiation focus
Myxoid Heterogeneous, T2 bright, little fat (~10%) · intense diffuse enhancement (vs myxoma)
High grade Like other sarcomas — T1 dark, T2 bright (only ~10% fat)

X-raysoft-tissue mass · calcification/ossification in WDL

Biopsyareas not consistent with fat on MRI must be biopsied

Histology

Subtype Histology
ALT / WDL Mature fat with atypical stromal cells, scattered lipoblasts, minimally cellular · lipoma-like, sclerotic, inflammatory variants
Myxoid Proliferating lipoblasts (signet ring at lobule edges) in myxoid stroma + delicate plexiform "chicken-wire" capillaries · "pulmonary oedema" pattern
Round cell Small round blue cells
Pleomorphic Giant lipoblasts, bizarre nuclei, eosinophilic giant cells (UPS-like + lipoblasts)
Dedifferentiated ALT/WDL transformed to high-grade sarcoma

Staging

Myxoid liposarcoma — extrapulmonary spreadlungs + abdomen, retroperitoneum, spine, other extremities' soft tissue, bone marrow

Myxoid staging/surveillanceCT chest/abdomen/pelvis + whole-spine MRI + MRI of primary site
✗ Wrong options: chest-only · PET (misses spine) · brain MRI · sentinel node · marrow biopsy

Other STSchest CT

5Management

Liposarcoma
  • ALT (extremity), MDM2/CDK4 +→MARGINAL RESECTION, NO RADIATION
  • Myxoid (intermediate)→WIDE RESECTION + RT
    • >5% round cells (OB: ≥25%) or margins inadequate→+ CHEMO (ifosfamide-based)
  • Round cell / pleomorphic / dedifferentiated (high grade)→WIDE RESECTION + RT (pre-op 50 Gy)

ALT/WDLmarginal resection without RT (OB) · Orthobullets lists WDL for wide resection + RT (vs ALT marginal) — read the options ·
✗ Wrong options: amputation · chemo · RT alone · wide resection for extremity ALT

Myxoidmost chemo- and radiosensitive liposarcoma

Radiation50 Gy preop, 66 Gy postop standard for STS · ↓ local recurrence

Techniquetourniquet without exsanguination · frozen section margins · NV resection only if bypass possible and morbidity acceptable

Soft tissue sarcoma treatment (margins, radiation, staging)

Most important factor preventing local recurrenceadequacy of surgical margin
✗ Wrong options: chemo · RT alone

Large (>5 cm), deep, high-grade STSwide excision + radiation (pre- or post-op)

Pleomorphic liposarcoma, no metsneoadjuvant RT 50 Gy → wide resection
✗ Wrong options: RFA · resection alone · chemo-based · disarticulation

6Complications

Mass effectNV bundles, retroperitoneum, peritoneum

Subtype Local recurrence Dedifferentiation Survival
ALT 10–50% over 10 yr 3–7% (2% extremity) ~100%
WDL (central) High 20% ~80% 5-yr
Intermediate/high ~20% — 50–80% 5-yr

X vs Y

ALT Lipoma
MDM2/CDK4 + −
Septa Thick (>2 mm), nodular Thin
Depth Deep Usually subcutaneous
Treatment Marginal excision Observe / marginal
Myxoid liposarcoma Intramuscular myxoma
Location Intermuscular Intramuscular
Cellularity Cellular, lipoblasts, chicken-wire vessels Hypocellular, bland
Enhancement Intense Little

How it's asked

"Thigh fatty tumour, MDM2 + CDK4 +?"atypical lipomatous tumour
✗ Wrong options: lipoma · RMS · LMS · myxofibrosarcoma

"Genetic basis + treatment of thigh WDL?"MDM2 amplification; marginal resection without radiation

"Gluteal ALT, MDM2 +?"marginal resection

"Best test to separate ALT from lipoma?"FISH for MDM2
