✗ Wrong options: t(12;16) · t(2;13) · ETV6-NTRK3 · SYT-SSX

"Lipomatous tumour with highest metastatic rate?"dedifferentiated liposarcoma

"Risk of ALT dedifferentiating?"3–7%

"Thigh mass, signet-ring lipoblasts in myxoid stroma — translocation?"myxoid liposarcoma, t(12;16)
✗ Wrong options: alveolar RMS · synovial t(X;18)

"33-yr-old, thigh mass, 12;16 translocation?"myxoid liposarcoma
✗ Wrong options: WDL · round cell · fibrolipomatous hamartoma

"Posterior thigh mass with sciatic symptoms, chicken-wire vessels?"myxoid liposarcoma
✗ Wrong options: synovial sarcoma · MFH · fibrosarcoma

"Myxoid liposarcoma after resection — extra staging?"whole-spine MRI

"Myxoid liposarcoma surveillance?"MRI of thigh + CT chest/abdomen/pelvis

"Slide warranting marginal resection alone?"well-differentiated liposarcoma / ALT
✗ Wrong options: myxoid · dedifferentiated · UPS · pleomorphic

"Thigh mass following fat with stranding + enhancement?"liposarcoma
✗ Wrong options: synovial sarcoma (cystic, no fat)

"Most important factor against local recurrence in UPS?"adequate surgical margin

"Large, deep, high-grade dedifferentiated liposarcoma?"wide excision + radiation

"15 cm pleomorphic liposarcoma, p53 +, t(12;16) −?"neoadjuvant RT (50 Gy) → wide resection

Lymphoma

Pathology · 33 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Primary lymphoma of bone (PBL)very rare · 2–3% of primary bone malignancies (OB) · 3–7% (TO, osteosarcoma chapter)

Extranodal10–35% of NHL patients

Demographicsmales · all ages, 35–55 commonest (median 45–60)

Sitebones with persistent red marrow: pelvis, spine, ribs · knee (distal femur, proximal tibia), proximal femur, shoulder girdle · diaphysis of long bones

Risk factorsimmunodeficiency (HIV, hepatitis) · viral/bacterial infection

Forms

Form Definition
Primary lymphoma of bone Solitary site, no other disease for 6 months · mostly non-Hodgkin B-cell · better prognosis than secondary bone involvement
Multiple bony sites No visceral disease
Bone + soft tissue —

Subtypediffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL) = 70–80% of bone lymphomas

Genes & lab markers

Immunostains +CD20, CD45 (leukocyte common antigen), CD79a, BCL-6 (B cell) · CD3 −

NegativeCD99 −, no t(11;22) (vs Ewing) · reticulin + (vs Ewing −)

Stain Tumour
CD20 + CD45 ✓ Lymphoma
CD99 + FLI-1 (EWSR1) Ewing
CD138 Myeloma
CD31 + CD34 Vascular tumours
Desmin + MyoD1 (myogenin) Rhabdomyosarcoma

3Presentation

Pain unrelieved by rest · B symptoms — fever, night sweats, weight loss, fatigue

Pathologic fracture25%

Masswarm, swollen, large soft-tissue mass · spinal cord compression

Labsmay mimic infection (mild ↑ ESR/CRP, lymphocytosis)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raylarge ill-defined, diffuse, permeative/moth-eaten lytic lesion, subtle mottling · diaphysis · cortical thickening 25% · may be sclerotic · "ivory" vertebra · multiple sites · often subtle/unremarkable

MRIextensive marrow involvement + large soft-tissue mass out of proportion to bone destruction · T1 dark, T2 bright

Bone scanintensely positive · PET/CT — staging + follow-up

CT chest/abdomen/pelvisstaging

Bone marrow aspiration + biopsyrequired for staging · before any definitive bony stabilization when no known primary
✗ Wrong options: chemo first · resection · prophylactic nail first

Moth-eaten (type 2) margins (TO)myeloma, mets, infection, EG, OS, chondrosarcoma, lymphoma · 45-yr-old, proximal humeral path fracture, moth-eaten → lymphoma

Histology

Mixed small round blue cells of different sizes and shapes · diffuse infiltration of trabeculae (not nodular) · little stroma

Needle biopsyoften non-diagnostic (crush artefact)

Differential

Destructive, young Malignant, older Small cells Chemo alone
Lymphoma ✓ ✓ ✓ ✓
Leukaemia · Ewing ✓ — ✓ —
OS · EG · osteomyelitis · desmoplastic fibroma ✓ — — —
Metastasis — ✓ ✓ —
Myeloma — ✓ — ✓
Chondrosarcoma · MFH · secondary sarcoma — ✓ — —
RMS · neuroblastoma — — ✓ —

Ewing vs lymphomaEwing younger, CD99 +, t(11;22) · lymphoma older, CD20/CD45 + · TB → caseating granulomas · osteomyelitis → mixed inflammatory cells

5Management

Treatment — chemotherapy (R-CHOP) & fixation

Primary lymphoma of bone
  • First→BONE MARROW BIOPSY + PET/CT (staging)
  • All→MULTI-AGENT CHEMO (R-CHOP) ± LOCAL RT (persistent disease)
  • Impending / actual pathologic fracture→STABILISE (IM nail / hemiarthroplasty) then chemo ± RT

ChemoCHOP: cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin (hydroxydaunorubicin), vincristine (Oncovin), prednisone ± rituximab (R-CHOP) · chemo alone effective for most · 70% 5-yr survival in disseminated disease

Radiationadjunct for local control of persistent disease · RT alone insufficient · routine RT in R-CHOP era uncertain

Surgeryonly to stabilise pathologic / impending fractures (prophylactic IM nail) · surgery mustn't delay chemo

Femoral neck lesion found at hemiarthroplasty (frozen section = lymphoma)complete hemiarthroplasty, then staging + chemoradiotherapy
✗ Wrong options: close wound + chemo · radical resection + megaprosthesis · marrow transplant · disarticulation

Not indicatedwide resection / limb salvage · amputation · hemipelvectomy · marrow transplant (relapse only) · neoadjuvant chemo + resection (OS/Ewing protocol)

6Complications

Prognosisprimary bone lymphoma better than secondary bone involvement

X vs Y

Lymphoma of bone Ewing Myeloma
Age 35–55 5–25 >50
X-ray Permeative, mottled, ± sclerotic, big soft-tissue mass Permeative, onion skin Punched-out, cold bone scan
Marker CD20, CD45 CD99 CD138
Treatment R-CHOP ± RT VAC/IE + surgery ± RT Chemo ± RT

How it's asked

"45-yr-old, destructive lesion + soft-tissue mass, round blue cells, CD99 −?"lymphoma of bone
✗ Wrong options: Ewing · chondrosarcoma · adenocarcinoma · OS

"32-yr-old distal femur, sheets of blue cells, no t(11;22)?"lymphoma
✗ Wrong options: Ewing · bacterial/TB osteomyelitis · OS

✗ Wrong options: CD31/CD34 · CD99/FLI-1 · CD138 · desmin/MyoD1

"48-yr-old, fevers, weight loss, CD20/CD45 — histology?"small round blue cells

"76-yr-old proximal femur, PET shows single site, prophylactic fixation + chemo — supporting pathology?"large atypical lymphoid cells CD20 +, CD79a +, BCL-6 +, CD3 −

"Pathologic femoral neck fracture through lymphoma at hemiarthroplasty?"hemiarthroplasty, then staging + chemoradiotherapy

"Severe thigh pain, extensive femoral lymphoma?"IM nail + multi-agent chemo ± RT
✗ Wrong options: AKA · wide resection + prosthesis

"Knee pain, failed antibiotics, biopsy = lymphoma?"multi-agent chemo ± local RT

"Iliac DLBCL, PET no other lesions?"CHOP chemotherapy
✗ Wrong options: marrow transplant · CT CAP for primary · hemipelvectomy · RT alone

"40-yr-old, distal thigh lytic lesions, weight loss, no known primary — next?"bone marrow aspiration + biopsy
✗ Wrong options: chemo · resection · nail first

Malignant Peripheral Nerve Sheath Tumor

Pathology · 34 / 66

1Basics

Synonymsneurofibrosarcoma, malignant schwannoma · high-grade STS from a nerve or pre-existing neurofibroma

Agesolitary 30–55 · with NF1 20–40

Diagnosis & NF1 association

NF1most cases · 4% of NF1 patients develop MPNST · autosomal dominant (17q11.2)
✗ Wrong options: AR · X-linked

Genes & lab markers

S-100 + (diffuse) · keratin −

S-100 positive tumoursmelanoma, nerve sheath tumours, clear cell sarcoma (± synovial sarcoma) · chondroblastoma, chondroid lesions

3Presentation

Mass on a large nerve — sciatic, brachial plexus, femoral

Neurologicmotor + sensory deficit, radicular pain · pressure → dysaesthesia distally

NF1 red flaga neurofibroma that enlarges and becomes constantly painful

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raynormal / non-specific soft-tissue mass

MRIT1 low, T2 high (like other high-grade sarcomas) · traced to a major nerve · oedema, rim enhancement (necrosis) · serial MRI: enlargement of a benign nerve sheath lesion = malignant change

PETSUV >2.0 → malignant

Bone scanmildly positive

Histology

Atypical pleomorphic spindle cells with wavy nucleiresembles fibrosarcoma · hypocellular stroma areas

MPNST Schwannoma Neurofibroma
Cells Pleomorphic, atypical spindle cells Bland, Antoni A/B Bland, wavy nuclei, mast cells
MRI Necrosis, oedema, rim enhancement Target sign Entering-exiting nerve, target sign
S-100 + + +

5Management

Wide resection including the entire affected nerve + preoperative (neoadjuvant) radiation — treated as high-grade sarcoma

Chemotherapynot useful

6Complications

5-yr survivalsolitary 75% · NF1-associated 30% (Orthobullets) · one OB-cited series: NF1 not independently worse

Poor prognosissize, mitotic rate, need for amputation

How it's asked

"45-yr-old, radicular buttock pain, S-100 + pleomorphic spindle cells?"MPNST
✗ Wrong options: schwannoma (bland, target sign) · liposarcoma · GCT tendon sheath

"NF patient, enlarging painful posterior thigh mass on sciatic nerve, rim enhancement, PET SUV >2?"MPNST
✗ Wrong options: benign PNST · MFH · liposarcoma · synovial sarcoma

"S-100 + spindle-cell tumour of femoral nerve — associated condition's inheritance?"autosomal dominant (NF1)

"Treatment?"wide resection of tumour + entire nerve + preop RT
✗ Wrong options: chemo

Melanoma

Pathology · 35 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Natureaggressive skin malignancy of melanocytes (from neural crest, deepest epidermal layer)

Demographics50–70 · M:F 1.2:1

Hand/foot sitesthumb > great toe > index finger · sun-exposed areas

Subtypeacral lentiginous melanoma (palms, subungual) · subungual melanoma = ALM subtype

Risk factors

Sun exposure (UV suppresses immunity, DNA damage) · family history · fair skin, blue eyes, freckling · xeroderma pigmentosum · FAMM syndrome · multiple / dysplastic naevi (precursor) · immunosuppression

Preventionsunscreen, avoid sun

Genes & lab markers

S-100 + (melanoma)

LDH ↑predicts poor prognosis · AST/ALT — liver mets · CBC

3Presentation

Pigmented lesion with recent change in shape or size · itching, bleeding, ulceration

ABCDEAsymmetry · Border irregularity · Colour variation · Diameter (>6 mm suspicious; <6 mm benign) · Elevation / enlargement

Subungualdystrophic nail prone to trauma → often blamed on injury

Hutchinson signbrown-black pigment extends from nail bed/plate onto cuticle and nail folds ± nail lifting

Longitudinal melanonychia — malignant featuresband >6 mm, single nail, wider proximally, colour heterogeneity, blurry borders, pigment into nail fold

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

CXRlungs often first metastatic site · US — nodes · PET/CT — metastases

Biopsyfull-thickness biopsy of the nail matrix after nail plate removal
✗ Wrong options: trephination · antibiotics · no work-up

Histology

Melanocytes with marked atypia + dermal invasion · vacuolated cytoplasm · hyperchromatic nuclei, prominent nucleoli

Breslow thickness

Stage Thickness
1 ≤0.75 mm
2 0.76–1.5 mm
3 1.51–4 mm
4 >4 mm

Clark level

Level Invasion
I Epidermis only (in situ)
II Papillary dermis
III Papillary dermis to papillary–reticular interface
IV Reticular dermis
V Subcutaneous tissue

Differential

Generalnaevi · seborrhoeic keratosis · basal cell carcinoma

Subungualtrauma / subungual haematoma (contained under plate, needs trauma) · onychomycosis (thickening, yellow-brown) · psoriasis (pitting) · SCC (no pigment) · splinter haemorrhages (endocarditis → echo) · glomus tumour (painful, blue) · DFSP (trunk)

5Management

Margins by thickness

Lesion Treatment
<1 mm Local resection, 1 cm margin
1–2 mm Resection 1–2 cm + sentinel node biopsy → radical node dissection if positive
Thick >3 cm margin (OB)
Metastatic Resection + node dissection + chemo

Subungual melanoma

Amputationthumb: IP joint disarticulation / through proximal phalanx · finger: PIP level · + sentinel node biopsy

Distal = proximaldistal amputation with adequate margins has same recurrence and survival as MCP/ray/CMC amputation → preserves function

Not usedneoadjuvant chemo to downsize · 1–2 mm margin · Mohs (controversial; for SCC/BCC) · isolated limb perfusion routinely

6Complications

Recurrenceusually regional lymph nodes

Prognosisdepth = most important factor · <0.7 mm → 96% survival · >4 mm → 47%

Poor prognosisdeep lesion · male · neck/scalp · positive nodes/mets · ulceration · ↑ LDH

Subungual5-yr survival 40–60% (late diagnosis, thicker, ulcerated)

Bone metastasismelanoma + lung: worst survival after pathologic fracture (<6 months)

How it's asked

"62-yr-old, enlarging brown nail streak on ring finger — biopsy to exclude?"melanoma
✗ Wrong options: DFSP · endocarditis · glomus · epithelioid sarcoma

"Persistent thumbnail pigment after hammer blow, Hutchinson sign — next step?"full-thickness nail matrix biopsy
✗ Wrong options: trephination · antibiotics · IPJ disarticulation first

"65-yr-old, thumbnail discoloration onto folds, no trauma?"melanoma
✗ Wrong options: SCC · onychomycosis · haematoma · psoriasis

"Thin (0.6 mm) subungual thumb melanoma — best cure with function?"IP joint disarticulation + sentinel node biopsy
✗ Wrong options: MCP disarticulation · ray amputation + limb perfusion · Mohs · chemo + 1–2 mm margin

"Most important prognostic factor?"depth (thickness)

Melorheostosis

Pathology · 36 / 66

1Basics

Naturerare, benign, painful developmental dysplasia of cortical bone · formation of periosteal (or endosteal) new bone — "flowing hyperostosis"

Demographicsbefore age 40 · no sex predilection

Geneticsnon-hereditary

Sitelower extremities commoner · any bone (long bones, ulna, hand, foot/metatarsals)

Genes & lab markers

ALPoften elevated

3Presentation

Symptomspain, reduced ROM, joint contractures, swelling

Skinfibrosis with induration and erythema over the lesion

Incidentalmay be found on unrelated imaging (asymptomatic)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (diagnostic)"dripping candle wax" — dense cortical hyperostosis flowing along the cortex · may cross joints → mechanical block

CTnon-aggressive sclerosis, may obliterate the medullary canal

Bone scanhot (↑ osteoblastic activity) — not a sign of malignancy here

Histology

Normal Haversian systems, enlarged trabeculae · bland osteoid · no atypia, no mitoses

Differential

Lesion Distinguishing
Melorheostosis Flowing candle-wax cortical hyperostosis, bland bone
Metastatic prostate cancer Blastic lesions, carcinoma on histology (↑ PSA is a distractor)
Periosteal OS Surface sunburst, malignant osteoid
Spindle cell sarcoma Malignant spindle cells
Stress fracture Fracture line, callus

Associationmelorheostosis can underlie secondary osteosarcoma (rare)

5Management

Melorheostosis
  • Asymptomatic / incidental→OBSERVATION (X-ray diagnostic, no biopsy)
  • Mild symptoms, adequate motion→SYMPTOMATIC CARE + BISPHOSPHONATES (zoledronate)
  • Severe contracture, immobility, pain→HYPEROSTOTIC BONE RESECTION + CONTRACTURE RELEASE (± osteotomy)

Bisphosphonateshelp pain and swelling
✗ Wrong options: calcitonin · rituximab · tamoxifen (desmoid) · stereotactic radiation

How it's asked

"55-yr-old, foot pain, sclerotic 2nd metatarsal, hot scan, bland bone, ↑ PSA?"melorheostosis
✗ Wrong options: metastatic prostate · periosteal OS · spindle cell sarcoma · stress fracture

"21-yr-old, incidental flowing hyperostosis of proximal femur?"observation
✗ Wrong options: needle/incisional biopsy · curettage · resection

"Elbow pain, swelling, ROM 30–90°, candle-wax hyperostosis — beneficial drug?"zoledronate

Metastatic Disease of Extremity

Pathology · 37 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Commonest cause of a destructive bone lesion in adults · commonest tumour of bone · aggressive lytic lesion >40 yr: metastasis > myeloma > lymphoma > Paget sarcoma > post-radiation sarcoma

Solitary bone lesion >40 yr500× more likely metastatic carcinoma than primary bone tumour

Bone = 3rd commonest metastatic site (after lung, liver)

Age>50

Primaries (BLT + Kosher Pickle)Breast (commonest in women; overall), Lung, Thyroid, Kidney, Prostate (commonest in men) · overall breast > prostate > lung (TO) · men: prostate > lung · women: breast > lung · children: neuroblastoma

Breastbone is the first metastatic site in 40–75% (TO)

Sites

Commonest site of bone metastasisspine (thoracic) (TO) > pelvis > proximal femur > humerus · then ribs, skull · red marrow

Pathologic fractureproximal femur commonest (then proximal humerus) · femoral neck 50%, subtrochanteric 30%, peritrochanteric 20% (OB: 50% neck, 30% subtroch, 20% intertroch) · TO: femoral neck commonest, transverse lines

Acral (distal to elbow/knee)rare · lung commonest (then kidney) → arterial tree spread · tibia commonest bone (TO) · poor prognosis

Occult primarybiopsy shows carcinoma but CT CAP + bone scan negative → lung
✗ Wrong options: colon, kidney, prostate, breast

Batson vertebral plexusvalveless venous plexus → lung, kidney, breast, prostate, thyroid to vertebral bodies, pelvis, skull, proximal girdles · breast → azygos → Batson in thoracic spine

Spinevertebral body involved before pedicle, but pedicle destruction ("winking owl") is commonest first plain-film sign (TO)

Biology of metastasis (integrins, MMPs, RANKL, endothelin)

Step Molecule
Release from primary ↓ E-cadherin (cell adhesion lost) · PDGF → migration
Intravasation, avoid immune surveillance —
Homing to bone CXCL12 (marrow stromal chemokine)
Attachment to endothelium / bone Integrin (tumour cell CAM)
Extravasation, ECM degradation MMPs (collagenases, gelatinases, stromelysins)
Angiogenesis VEGF
Survival Genomic instability, ↓ apoptosis · thrombospondin inhibits tumour growth

Necessary stepsintravasation, immune evasion, target localization, angiogenesis
✗ Wrong options: direct osteoclast stimulation (indirect, via RANKL) · increased apoptosis

Lytic "vicious cycle"tumour PTHrP (+ IL-6) → osteoblasts release RANKL → RANK on osteoclast precursors → osteolysis → TGF-β, IGF-1, Ca²⁺ released → more PTHrP · blocked by bisphosphonates, denosumab, OPG

Blasticendothelin-1 → ET-A receptor on osteoblasts, ↓ DKK-1 → WNT ↑ · also TGF-β, BMPs (TO)

Lytic vs blastic

Pattern Primaries
Lytic Kidney, thyroid, lung (also GI, melanoma)
Blastic Prostate (90%), seminoma, carcinoid, medulloblastoma · breast 60% (OB)
Mixed Breast — lytic, blastic or mixed ("all of the above") (TO) · commoner lytic (TO)

Pulsatile metastasesfollicular thyroid + RCC (most pulsatile tumours are mets > ABC > GCT) (TO)

Bone-to-bone metastasisEwing > osteosarcoma (TO)

Genes & lab markers

Test Looks for
CBC, ESR, BMP, LFTs, PT/PTT, Ca, phosphate Baseline, hypercalcaemia
SPEP / UPEP Myeloma
PSA Prostate
LDH Lymphoma of bone
ALP Paget, bone turnover
Urinalysis Renal cell
Acid phosphatase · urine hydroxyproline Prostate · massive destruction (TO)

Histology IHCkeratin, CK7 (breast, lung), TTF-1 (lung) · RCC: clear cell change, pseudoalveolar pattern

Breast receptor statusER, PR, HER2/neu guide therapy (tamoxifen/letrozole, trastuzumab)
✗ Wrong options: RANKL · BRCA · CA-125 · t(11;22)

3Presentation

Paindull, gradual; night pain (tumoral) or weight-bearing (mechanical) · weight loss, mass

Pathologic fracture8–30% at presentation · 90% need surgery (rarely heal)

Atraumatic lesser trochanter avulsion in an adultmalignancy until proven otherwise

Metastatic hypercalcaemia (emergency)confusion, weakness, polyuria/polydipsia, N/V, dehydration → IV fluids, loop diuretic, bisphosphonate

Complications (Coleman)hypercalcaemia 19%, pathologic fracture 19%, cord compression 10%, marrow failure 9%

4Diagnosis & Classification

Workup of solitary lytic lesion — staging & biopsy before fixation

New painful/destructive bone lesion, >40 yr
  • 1. History + exam (breast, thyroid, prostate) + LABS (SPEP/UPEP, PSA…)
  • 2. X-ray whole bone + CXR→CT CHEST/ABDOMEN/PELVIS + WHOLE-BODY BONE SCAN (± MRI lesion)
  • 3. Primary found + multiple lesions→biopsy most accessible lesion if needed
  • 4. Solitary lesion (even with remote/known cancer)→BIOPSY (treating team, intramuscular tract)
    • Carcinoma confirmed → FIX (whole bone) + RT · Sarcoma→wide resection protocol

Never treat a bone lesion without a tissue diagnosis
✗ Wrong options: IM nail with reamings sent as biopsy (contaminates femur, abductors, skin) · empirical chemo/RT · ORIF

Remote cancer + solitary lesionbiopsy (could be primary sarcoma) — breast cancer 10–26 yr earlier, lung cancer resected, colon cancer, Paget patient

Nailed "metastasis" proves high-grade chondrosarcomawhole femur contaminated → wide resection incl. hip disarticulation (or total femoral resection)

Open biopsyintramuscular interval
✗ Wrong options: intermuscular

Lab + CT + MRI before biopsy for atraumatic lesser trochanter fracture
✗ Wrong options: suture anchor repair · IM nail first

Pathologic humeral shaft fracture, no cancer historycoaptation splint, then bone scan + CT CAP (then biopsy)
✗ Wrong options: immediate nail with reamings · ORIF + biopsy first

Painful TKA with new tibial osteolysis, normal infection labsCT CAP / PET-CT staging
✗ Wrong options: revision

Primary identified~85% by history + exam + X-ray + CT CAP (OB: CT CAP 90%, >90% sensitive) · bone scan + CT ~50%

Key radiology findings

Modality Fact
X-ray Two planes, joint above + below · lytic / mixed / blastic · sensitivity ~50% — needs 30–50% mineral loss, 1–2 cm · lung → cortical mets
CT CAP Find primary in all >40 yr with unknown lytic lesion · CT of lesion for cortical containment · sens 71–100%
MRI Most sensitive (95%) + specific (90%) · marrow change before blastic change · T1 low, T2 high · local staging
Bone scan Other skeletal lesions · cold in myeloma + thyroid → skeletal survey · investigation of choice for occult blastic mets (TO)
PET-CT Investigation of choice for occult lytic mets (TO) · PET alone 30% specificity
Indium scan Infection (✗ Wrong options: tumour)

Histology

Epithelial cells in clumps / glands in fibrous stroma · RCC: clear cells, pseudoalveolar · metastatic gastric adenocarcinoma: glands + bone

Fix-without-wide-resection histologymetastatic carcinoma (breast)
✗ Wrong options: OS lacy osteoid · dedifferentiated CS · fibrosarcoma herringbone · chordoma physaliferous cells

Mirels criteria

Score Site Size (of shaft diameter) Appearance Pain
1 Upper limb <1/3 Blastic Mild
2 Lower limb 1/3–2/3 Mixed Moderate
3 Peritrochanteric >2/3 Lytic Functional (weight-bearing)

Recommendation (Orthobullets)<8 RT + observe · 8 judgement · >8 prophylactic fixation · OB explanations vary: "≥8", "≥9", ">9" — read the options

Mirels Fracture risk (6 mo)
4–6 0%
7 4%
8 15%
9 33%
10 72%
11 96%
12 100%

Sensitivity 80–90%, specificity 30–35%

Harrington (prophylactic fixation)>50% diaphyseal cortex destruction · >50–75% metaphysis · permeative subtrochanteric destruction · persistent pain after RT · pain with weight bearing · (OB: proximal femur lesion >2.5 cm, avulsion of lesser trochanter)

Spinal metastasis prognostic scores (Tokuhashi, Tomita)

Tokuhashiprognosis after spinal metastasis to guide treatment (TO) · ✓ includes general condition · ≤8 → <6 months (conservative) · 9–11 individual · ≥12 → >1 yr (excisional)

Modified Tokuhashi parameter Scores
General condition (KPS) Poor 10–40% = 0 · moderate 50–70% = 1 · good 80–100% = 2
Extraspinal bone mets >2 = 0 · 1–2 = 1 · 0 = 2
Vertebral body mets (original score) 0–2
Visceral (major organ) mets Non-removable 0 · removable 1 · none 2
Primary Lung, OS, stomach, bladder, oesophagus, pancreas 0 · liver, gallbladder, unidentified 1 · other 2 · kidney, uterus 3 · rectum 4 · thyroid, breast, prostate, carcinoid 5
Palsy Frankel A–B 0 · C–D 1 · E 2

Tomita (2001)3 parameters: primary growth, visceral mets, bone mets
✗ Wrong options: general condition (that's Tokuhashi) (TO)

Tomita parameter Score
Primary: slow (breast, thyroid) / moderate (kidney, uterus) / rapid (lung, stomach) 1 / 2 / 4
Visceral mets: none / treatable / untreatable 0 / 2 / 4
Bone mets: solitary / multiple 1 / 2

Tomita action2–4 aggressive surgery · 5–7 palliative surgery · 8–10 non-operative / supportive

OthersBauer, Linden, Rades, Douglas, Katagiri scores (TO)

5Management

General principles

Goalspain control, independence, prevent fracture · implant should outlive the patient, allow immediate weight bearing, protect the whole bone, not rely on healing

Nonoperativeasymptomatic · non-displaced fractures of humeral shaft, pelvis, scapula · non-weight-bearing bones · short life expectancy · systemically sensitive tumours

Life expectancy for surgerylower-limb fixation if ≥6–12 weeks (OB) · arthroplasty if >3 months (OB) · >6 months = best predictor of fracture healing (Orthobullets) · RT alone for pain if <6 months (OB)

Bisphosphonateslytic, blastic, mixed · ↓ skeletal-related events + hypercalcaemia · oral clodronate, IV pamidronate/zoledronic acid · not for acute impending fracture · complication ONJ

Denosumabsolid-tumour mets + myeloma · superior to zoledronic acid for SRE prevention · SC · ONJ

Radiationpalliation + local control · pain ↓ in 70% at 2 wk, complete relief ~30% · RCC + thyroid radioresistant (✓ breast, prostate, lymphoma, myeloma radiosensitive) · post-RT bone weakens first → fracture risk ~1 yr · radiating a high-risk lesion first ↑ fracture risk

OtherRFA (periosteal nerves), cementoplasty (pain, not structure), cryoablation, HIFU · radionuclide therapy (TO)

Postoperative radiation

All patients after fixation (unless death imminent or previously irradiated) · start 2–3 weeks post-op · field covers entire implant (e.g. whole femur after nailing)
✗ Wrong options: tumour site only

Preoperative RT↑ wound complications

Femoral lesions — cephalomedullary nailing & radiation

Location Treatment
Femoral head / neck Arthroplasty (cemented) · hemiarthroplasty if acetabulum free · THA if acetabular lesions / OA (✗ Wrong options: screws · DHS · pinning (high failure))
Intertrochanteric Calcar-replacing hemi/THA or cephalomedullary nail
Peri-/subtrochanteric Long, statically locked cephalomedullary nail ± PMMA · or proximal femoral replacement (✗ Wrong options: DHS / sliding hip screw · plate · dynamic locking (shortens) · standard antegrade nail · Enders + cement)
Femoral diaphysis Statically locked cephalomedullary nail

Arthroplasty vs nail/ORIFarthroplasty lower failure · THA more dislocation than hemi · arthroplasty more infection than nails

Cemented stemsimmediate stability, RT inhibits ingrowth · intra-op metastatic surprise during THA → convert to cemented stem + cup

PMMAfills defects, immediate structural support
✗ Wrong options: autograft (unreliable)

Post-op rehabfull weight bearing with cane (lung mets, Mirels 12) · no axillary crutches with axillary nodes

Periarticular lesions — arthroplasty & endoprosthesis

Site Treatment
Proximal humerus Arthroplasty / endoprosthesis (ORIF option) · RCC → proximal humerus replacement (✗ Wrong options: locking plate · nail · standard reverse)
Humeral diaphysis IM nail
Distal humerus Flexible nails / elbow replacement
Tibia IM nail (pathologic fractures progress, don't heal in cast/brace)

Solitary renal cell metastasis — embolisation & wide resection

Preoperative embolizationRCC + thyroid (hypervascular, VEGF) → ↓ blood loss, no effect on healing · palliative pain control

Solitary RCC metastasiswide (en bloc) resection + endoprosthetic reconstruction (cemented long stem) → ↓ revision, ↑ survival (median 1.3 → 4.8 yr with complete resection), ↓ complications, ↓ local recurrence
✗ Wrong options: IM nail / plate (progression, hardware failure)

RCC = strongest predictor of local progression after nailing

Polymetastatic RCCen bloc resection only gives lower local recurrence
✗ Wrong options: survival benefit · cheaper · less blood loss, reoperation equivalent

RCC featureslytic, acral possible, radio- and chemo-resistant, hypervascular, high complication rate with intralesional treatment, survival variable (6 mo – 4–5 yr) · fracture healing 44% · IL-2 immunotherapy response 18–35%
✗ Wrong options: sensitive

Thyroid met (subtrochanteric)curettage + IM nailing · long survival → stabilise even tibial fracture

6Complications

Pathologic fracture healing65% nonunion overall · prostate 42%, RCC 44% (comparable across carcinomas)

Prostate metsblastic (90%), radiosensitive, fewer SREs, better survival, comparable fracture healing

1-yr survival after surgery for metastatic disease~30% (prostate series)

Prognosis

Primary Median survival with bone mets
Thyroid 48 months
Prostate 40 months (Coleman 53)
Breast 24 months (Coleman 20)
Kidney Variable: 6 months – 4–5 years
Myeloma 2–3 years
Lung 6–7 months (worst) · <5% 5-yr

✗ Wrong options: bisphosphonates acutely · megaprosthesis

Metastatic Disease of Spine

Pathology · 38 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Commonest destructive bone lesion in adultsmetastasis · bone = 3rd site (after lung, liver) · >40 yr

Spine = commonest site of bone metastasisthoracic commonest (symptomatic: thoracic 68–70%, lumbosacral 16–22%, cervical 8–15%) · spine > proximal femur > humerus

Spinal diseaseup to 90% of metastatic cancer patients, only 10–20% symptomatic · first sign of malignancy in 20% · >90% of spinal tumours are metastatic

Primary tumours metastasising to spine

Primary % of patients developing spine mets
Breast 16–37%
Lung 12–15%
Prostate 9–15%
Kidney 3–6%
Thyroid 4%

Order of spinal secondary spreadbreast, prostate, lung, kidney ≈ 80% · plus thyroid, myeloma (~75% of bone mets)

Rarely to spinecolon · hepatic, uterine, skin, "osseous" are distractors · haemangioma doesn't metastasize
✗ Wrong options: breast, lung, renal, thyroid

Risk cluesBRCA1 (breast) · smoking (lung) · von Hippel–Lindau / VHL (renal) · hypercalcaemia
✗ Wrong options: APC + positive FOBT (colon — least likely)

Mechanism of metastasis

Theoriesseed and soil (compatible end-organ) · circulation (blood flow from primary)

Steps↓ E-cadherin → release · immune evasion · integrin → endothelial attachment · extravasation · VEGF angiogenesis · genomic instability · ↓ apoptosis
✗ Wrong options: osteocalcin

Batson plexusvalveless venous plexus → vertebral bodies, pelvis, skull, proximal girdles

Osteoclast-mediated bone resorption

Lysistumour-induced activation of osteoclasts · tumour releases TNF-α, IL-6, IL-11, PTHrP, TGF-β → osteoblasts secrete RANKL → RANK on osteoclasts · OPG binds RANKL, blocks resorption
✗ Wrong options: direct resorption by tumour cells (only osteoclasts resorb bone) · macrophages · necrosis · angiogenesis

Sclerosisprostate (± breast) → endothelin-1 → ET-A receptor on osteoblasts, ↓ DKK-1 → WNT ↑
✗ Wrong options: ET-1 causes lysis

Genes & lab markers

Labs (unknown primary)CBC + diff, ESR, BMP, LFTs, Ca, phosphate, ALP, SPEP/UPEP, TFTs, PTH, PSA, urinalysis (± CEA)

Red flags → first testsCBC, ESR, urinalysis (+ PSA, CEA with cancer history)

IHCkeratin · CK7 (breast, lung) · TTF-1 (lung) · vimentin (renal) · CDX2 (colon)

Associatedhypercalcaemia 10–30% (lung, breast) → IV fluids, loop diuretic, bisphosphonate · anaemia · hypercoagulability / VTE

3Presentation

Painaxial night pain / rest pain · mechanical pain (instability) · pain worse supine, relieved standing

Red flags (tumour)age >50, cancer history (most significant), weight loss, night pain, pain lying down

Neurologiccord compression → UMN signs: hyperreflexia, clonus, gait ataxia, weakness, bowel/bladder · distinguish root vs cord

Chemo-relatedperipheral neuropathy

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral)"winking owl" sign = unilateral absent pedicle (pedicle destruction) · lytic or mixed · up to 40% invisible on X-ray · discs preserved (vs infection)

MRI with contrastnext step for winking owl / suspected mets · dedicated region + limited sagittal whole spine · soft-tissue mass, ↑ T2, cortical permeation, cord compression · 98% sensitive + specific

CTsensitivity 66%, specificity 99% · CT myelography if MRI contraindicated · CT CAP for unknown primary

Bone scanextent · cold in myeloma + thyroid → skeletal survey

Blastic pelvis/spine in older man → prostate (→ prostate biopsy confirms) · lung blastic only 25–30%

Workup & diagnosis

Known cancer, no prior mets / no cancer historyevaluate before treating · history, exam, X-ray, labs, CT CAP + bone scan → identifies primary in 85% · biopsy adds 8%

Staging before biopsy
✗ Wrong options: CT-guided biopsy first · excision · RFA · NSAIDs + PT

Winking owl in IV drug user, normal WBC/ESR/CRPCT CAP
✗ Wrong options: cervical MRI · antibiotics · kyphoplasty

Multiple spinal lesions + very high PSAstaging + diagnostic tests together (prostate biopsy, CT CAP, bone scan, CT-guided spine biopsy)
✗ Wrong options: bone marrow biopsy (prostate rarely to marrow)

Known metastatic diseasebiopsy not required

Biopsy techniqueposterior transpedicular, wide-gauge Jamshidi, coring needle, image guidance · posterior (cervical) → vertebral artery risk · occult adenocarcinoma → small lung primary

Histology

Epithelial cells in clumps/glands in fibrous stroma

Tokuhashi (revised) score

Six elementsgeneral condition · extraspinal bone mets · number of vertebral mets · visceral mets · primary tumour (0–5) · palsy · max 15

Total Survival / approach
0–8 <6 months — conservative / palliative
9–11 (9–12) >6 months — individualise
≥12 (12–15) >1 year — excisional surgery

Worked example (OB)good health 2 + no extraspinal bone mets 2 + 2 spinal lesions 1 + removable visceral 1 + rectal primary 4 + incomplete palsy 1 = 11 → surgery

Tomitaprimary growth + visceral mets + bone mets
✗ Wrong options: general condition (TO)

NOMS framework

NOMSNeurologic (ESCC grade) · Oncologic (radiosensitivity) · Mechanical instability (SINS) · Systemic illness (disease burden, comorbidity, ECOG) · most comprehensive current decision framework (FNB 2024 key not given — NOMS fits; ✗ Wrong options: Bigliani · DeWald; Tomita is a score) (TO)

ESCC (epidural cord compression)0–1 low grade, 2–3 high grade

SINS (Spinal Instability Neoplastic Score)

Element Scores
Location Junctional (occiput–C2, C7–T2, T11–L1, L5–S1) 3 · mobile (C3–C6, L2–L4) 2 · semi-rigid (T3–T10) 1 · rigid (S2–S5) 0
Pain (mechanical — relief lying down / pain on loading) Yes 3 · occasional non-mechanical 1 · none 0
Lesion Lytic 2 · mixed 1 · blastic 0
Alignment Subluxation/translation 4 · de novo deformity (kyphosis/scoliosis) 2 · normal 0
Body collapse >50% 3 · <50% 2 · none but >50% body involved 1 · none 0
Posterolateral involvement (facet, pedicle, CV joint) Bilateral 3 · unilateral 1 · none 0
SINS Meaning
0–6 Stable — no surgical consult
7–12 Potentially unstable → surgical referral
13–18 Unstable → surgical referral

Worked example (OB)T6 (1) + occasional non-mechanical pain (1) + mixed (1) + focal kyphosis (2) + 30% collapse (2) + no posterolateral (0) = 7 → surgical referral
✗ Wrong options: normal-alignment combinations scoring 4–6

5Management

Spinal metastasis (NOMS)
  • Stable (SINS ≤6), no deficit, life <6 mo→PAIN CONTROL ± BRACE, EBRT
  • No/stable fracture, no compression or deficit→RT / SRS ± KYPHOPLASTY / ABLATION
  • Neuro deficit or high-grade ESCC, life >6 mo → DECOMPRESSION + STABILISATION→POST-OP RT
    • Anterior (vertebral body) compression→CORPECTOMY + ANTERIOR COLUMN RECON + POSTERIOR FUSION
    • RCC / thyroid→PRE-OP EMBOLIZATION (same morning)
  • Radiosensitive tumour (myeloma, lymphoma)→RT may suffice

Nonoperative & radiation

Pain + bracestable fracture, no deficit, life <6 months (Tokuhashi)

External beam RTpainful lesions without deficit or cord compression
✗ Wrong options: embolization · laminectomy · corpectomy

SRS88% local control if no instability or compression

Kyphoplasty / vertebroplasty / RFAno or stable fracture, no compression, deficit, or instability · transpedicular, fluoroscopy · kyphoplasty = balloon cavity then PMMA (partial reduction) · not with posterior cortex disruption or neurologic impairment

Surgery — decompression, stabilisation & radiation

Patchell RCTdirect decompression + post-op RT > RT alone for metastatic cord compression → best for regaining/maintaining ambulation

Indicationsneurologic deficit + life >6 months · intractable pain · instability

Sequencesurgery first, then radiation · chemo too slow for acute deficit
✗ Wrong options: RT before surgery → 3× wound complications

Anterior compression (vertebral body)corpectomy + anterior column reconstruction + posterior instrumented fusion · posterior-only via costotransversectomy / transpedicular with expandable cage
✗ Wrong options: laminectomy alone → doesn't decompress, progressive kyphosis · posterior fusion alone → continued compression

Approachposterior for thoracic, lumbar, sacral · cervical anterior or posterior · cement augments screws

Separation surgerydecompress + stabilise to create space for SRS · no en bloc resection for metastases
✗ Wrong options: goal of total resection; margin usually intralesional

Outcomes95% local control with surgery + RT · 16% of ASIA E with high-grade ESCC decline even with surgery
✗ Wrong options: laminectomy + steroids alone

Breast mets, failed chemo, T9 ventral compression, hyperreflexiathoracic corpectomy + instrumented fusion + post-op RT
✗ Wrong options: CHOP · posterior decompression after kyphoplasty · RT alone

Preoperative embolisation (renal, thyroid)

Hypervascularrenal + thyroid → preop embolization (✗ Wrong options: breast, colon, prostate, lung, liver) · ideally morning of surgery (✗ Wrong options: post-op) · target the tumour capillary bed, not main feeder (collaterals) · agents: glue, alcohol, PVA particles, Gelfoam

RCC spine with deficitembolization → decompression + stabilization → RT · RCC radioresistant → RT alone won't relieve compression
✗ Wrong options: kyphoplasty · steroids · cryotherapy

Colon cancerno embolization needed

Bisphosphonates

Zoledronic acid↓ skeletal-related events 30–40% (fracture, need for RT/surgery, cord compression, hypercalcaemia) · pain ↓ in ≥50% · ONJ ~2.5% · renal toxicity → dose by creatinine clearance · zoledronic acid > pamidronate
✗ Wrong options: 15% · 25%

6Complications

Recurrence · hardware failure / instability (RT, chemo, frailty; >6 levels → instrumentation failure) · nonunion

Revision surgery — soft tissue reconstruction

Wound complicationsrisk: smoking, prior RT, instrumentation, revision · immediate soft-tissue reconstruction → better outcomes (delayed = 2× wound complications)
✗ Wrong options: preop RT · tobacco · posterior approach

Prognosis

Timingcancer → spinal met 32 months · spinal met → fracture 27 months

1-yr survival after spine-met surgery50% · epidural disease worsens

Survival factorshistology + prior treatment, function + neurology, age, extent, nutrition

Median survivalthyroid 48 mo · prostate 40 · breast 24 · kidney variable (≥6) · lung 6

How it's asked

✗ Wrong options: UPEP · thyroid biopsy · renal US

✗ Wrong options: CT biopsy first · excision · RFA

✗ Wrong options: haemangioma · infection

✗ Wrong options: myeloma · RCC · thyroid (lytic)

"RCC spinal biopsy, failed nonoperative care?"embolization the morning of corpectomy + stabilization

"Zoledronic acid in breast mets — true?"↓ fractures, RT, surgery, cord compression, hypercalcaemia (SRE ↓ 30–40%)
✗ Wrong options: 25% ONJ · no pain benefit · less effective than pamidronate · renal function irrelevant

"Tumours most likely causing spinal mets?"breast, renal, prostate, lung (+ thyroid)

"Rarely metastasizes to spine?"colon

"Lytic T12 lesion resorption mediated by?"tumour-induced osteoclast activation

"Painful lumbar prostate mets, no deficit?"external beam radiation

"Improves outcome in revision metastatic spine surgery?"immediate soft-tissue reconstruction

"Tokuhashi score is used for?"prognosis after spinal metastasis (TO)

"Not a Tomita component?"general condition (TO)

"Most comprehensive current decision framework for spinal mets (FNB 2024)?"key not given · NOMS fits (TO)

Multiple Myeloma

Pathology · 39 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology & eponym (Kahler disease)

Kahler disease = multiple myeloma (TO)
✗ Wrong options: OS · MFH · benign fibrous histiocytoma

Most common primary (malignant) bone tumour (TO) · 3–4 per 100,000 · 1% of all malignancies

Demographics>40 yr (median 60; 65 in OB) · male · 2× African Americans

Forms

Form Key features
Multiple myeloma Commonest · marrow + multiple lesions · CRAB
Solitary plasmacytoma Single skeletal site, negative marrow · radiosensitive · >50% progress to MM · MRI + FDG-PET find more lesions in 33% · best prognosis
Osteosclerotic myeloma (POEMS) Polyneuropathy (symmetric, distal → proximal, doesn't improve) · Organomegaly · Endocrinopathy · M protein · Skin changes (trunk) · sclerotic lesions
MGUS / asymptomatic myeloma Annual surveillance

Pathophysiology (RANKL, osteoblast suppression)

Osteolysismyeloma cells bind marrow stromal cells → ↑ RANKL, M-CSF, IL-6, IL-11, MIP-1α → osteoclast activation · OPG ↓ (decoy receptor for RANKL)

Osteoblast suppressionTNF and DKK-1 ↓ osteoblast function → no sclerotic rim, cold bone scan

Lytic lesions (mets + myeloma)RANK ligand acting on osteoclasts (RANKL secreted by osteoblasts → RANK receptor; OPG = decoy)
✗ Wrong options: RANKL on neoplastic cells · OPG on osteoclasts

Genes & lab markers

Lab Finding
SPEP M spike · IgG ~50%, IgA ~25% (heavy chains IgG 52%, IgA 21%, IgM 12%)
UPEP Bence Jones proteins = monoclonal light chains (κ, λ) · proteinuria
Blood Anaemia, ↑ creatinine, hypercalcaemia (30%), ↑ ESR
β2-microglobulin Tumour burden, prognosis (ISS)
IHC CD138 +

Poor prognosis geneticsdel 13, t(4;14), t(14;16)

3Presentation

Localized bone pain (spine, ribs) · pathologic fracture (low-energy vertebral compression) · fatigue (anaemia, renal failure, hypercalcaemia) · radiculopathy, fevers

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raymultiple "punched-out" lytic lesions, no sclerotic border · visible only after >50% destruction · diffuse osteopenia, moth-eaten · marrow-packing process

Skeletal surveypreferred screen · bone scan cold in ~30% (30–50%) — no osteoblastic activity
✗ Wrong options: bone scan ideal

MRImore sensitive than X-ray (spine, pelvis) · T1 dark, T2 bright · marrow replacement · focal lesion ≥5 mm = MDE

FDG-PET93% sensitive (> X-ray) · finds more lesions in "solitary" plasmacytoma

Diagnostic criteria (IMWG)

Marrowclonal plasma cells ≥10% (or biopsy-proven plasmacytoma) + ≥1 CRAB feature or MDE

CRAB Threshold
Calcium >0.25 mmol/L (>1 mg/dL) above ULN or >2.75 mmol/L (>11 mg/dL)
Renal CrCl <40 mL/min or creatinine >177 µmol/L (>2 mg/dL)
Anaemia Hb >20 g/L below LLN or <100 g/L
Bone ≥1 lytic lesion (X-ray, CT, PET-CT) · if marrow <10%, >1 lesion needed
Myeloma-defining event (MDE)
≥60% clonal marrow plasma cells
Involved:uninvolved free light chain ratio ≥100 (involved ≥100 mg/L)
≥1 focal MRI lesion ≥5 mm

Plasma cell % — conflictIMWG/Orthobullets/TO: ≥10% · OB: plasmacytoma 10–30% vs myeloma >30% (keyed 35% as minimum) — read the options · normal marrow <2%

Next test with anaemia + serum M proteinbone marrow aspiration + biopsy (TO)
✗ Wrong options: UPEP (already have serum M protein) · CT head · EMG · lumbar puncture · vertebral CT biopsy

International Staging System (ISS)

Stage Criteria Median survival
I β2-M <3.5 mg/L + albumin ≥3.5 g/dL 62 months
II β2-M 3.5–5.4 mg/L 44 months
III β2-M ≥5.5 mg/L 29 months

Histology

Plasma cellsround, eccentric nucleus, "clock-face" chromatin, prominent nucleolus, basophilic cytoplasm · perinuclear clear zone (Hof) = Golgi (immunoglobulin production)

Differential

Older patient Multiple lesions Bisphosphonates help Chemo + RT
Myeloma ✓ ✓ ✓ (chemo ± RT)
Metastasis ✓ ✓ ✓ —
Lymphoma ✓ ✓ — ✓
Paget — ✓ ✓ —
Hyperparathyroidism — ✓ — —

5Management

Plasma cell disorder
  • MGUS / asymptomatic→ANNUAL SURVEILLANCE
  • Solitary plasmacytoma→EBRT 45–50 Gy (fix if fracture/impending)
  • Osteosclerotic (POEMS)→CHEMO + RT + PLASMAPHERESIS
  • Multiple myeloma→MULTIAGENT CHEMO ± STEM-CELL TRANSPLANT + BISPHOSPHONATES
    • Impending / complete fracture, life >3 mo→STABILISE (whole bone, cement) + EBRT
    • Painful VCF / instability or compression resistant to RT→KYPHOPLASTY

Treatment (fixation, radiation, bisphosphonates)

Chemonon-transplant (>65, comorbid): melphalan + prednisone + thalidomide/bortezomib, lenalidomide/thalidomide + dexamethasone · transplant candidates: bortezomib/lenalidomide + dexamethasone (± thalidomide) → autologous SCT (not curative, +2–3 yr DFS)

Very radio- and chemosensitiveRT may be primary for impending lesions · surgery shouldn't delay induction / transplant

Bisphosphonates↓ skeletal events

Surgerycomplete or impending fracture, life expectancy >3 months · whole-bone protection, immediate stability · post-op RT

Lesion Fixation
Femoral neck fracture Cemented hemiarthroplasty or THA (✗ Wrong options: IM nail · percutaneous screws · DHS · radical resection)
Proximal femur (stem) Full-length cemented stem (✗ Wrong options: press-fit (RT/chemo impair ingrowth))
Peritrochanteric, Mirels 12 Stabilisation + RT + bisphosphonates (✗ Wrong options: wide resection (never for myeloma) · hormone therapy · curettage + graft)
Distal humerus path fracture Internal fixation without resection (IM rod) → RT (✗ Wrong options: megaprosthesis · chemo first)
Periacetabular, intact subchondral plate Curettage + PMMA + modified Harrington pins
Periacetabular, subchondral plate violated Complex THA + acetabular cage
Painful VCF Kyphoplasty/vertebroplasty — restores height, corrects sagittal deformity

Prophylactic fixationindicated before fracture so systemic therapy isn't delayed (bed rest, immunocompromise)

6Complications

Fracture healingmyeloma has among the best healing (67%) of metastatic-type lesions · MM vertebral fractures heal poorly, progressive height loss

Prognosismedian 3 yr · 5-yr 30%, 10-yr 11% · renal failure → shortest survival

Poor prognosisdel 13 / t(4;14) / t(14;16) · circulating plasma cells · ↑ β2-microglobulin · ↓ albumin · ↑ marrow microvessels

X vs Y

Myeloma Metastatic carcinoma
X-ray Punched-out, no sclerotic rim Lytic / blastic / mixed
Bone scan Cold ~30% → skeletal survey Hot (except thyroid, RCC lytic)
Labs M spike, Bence Jones, CRAB Primary-specific (PSA…)
Marker CD138 Keratin, CK7, TTF-1
Wide resection Never Solitary RCC

✗ Wrong options: urine electrophoresis · CT head · EMG

✗ Wrong options: FD · osteoporosis · infarcts

Myositis Ossificans

Pathology · 40 / 66

1Basics

Diagnosis & traumatic aetiology

Naturereactive, benign soft-tissue bone formation (heterotopic ossification) · aka hematoma ossificans

Causedirect trauma / contusion → intramuscular haematoma · near diaphysis of long bones · ⅓ idiopathic · also paraplegia, GBS, AIDS encephalopathy, head injury, burns, coma
✗ Wrong options: surgery · parathyroid tumour · bisphosphonates · steroids

Demographicsyoung active males 15–35

Musclesquadriceps, brachialis, glutei · elbow = commonest joint · massage / vigorous passive physio after elbow injury provokes it (TO)

Geneticspost-traumatic, not genetic
✗ Wrong options: COMP · type X collagen · t(9;22) · p53

Genes & lab markers

Early↑ CRP, ↑ CPK (inflammation, muscle)

Mature phase↑ ALP · falling ALP + mature bone on X-ray = maturity (TO)

FOPACVR1 mutation (see below)

3Presentation

Phases (TO)acute (days–weeks): pain, swelling, joint stiffness · pseudotumoral (2–3 wk): painless hard mass · resolving (3–6 mo)

Massgrows over months to 3–6 cm, then firm · palpable · ↓ ROM (e.g. TKA stiffness after thigh blow)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayinitially normal · mineralization at 2–3 weeks, matures 3–6 months · peripheral ossification with central lucency ("dotted veil") · ossified, smooth · next to but not stuck on / not sharing the cortex

CT"eggshell" peripheral rim

MRI with gadoliniumrim enhancement in first 3 weeks · intramuscular · surrounding oedema early

Zonation phenomenon (Ackerman)matures periphery → centre (✓ MO) vs osteosarcoma centre → periphery

Variants (TO)stalked (peduncle to bone) · sessile (broad periosteal base) · muscle-only

Histology — zonal pattern

Zone Histology
Periphery Mature lamellar + woven bone trabeculae (osteoblastic rimming) · mature fibroblasts → calcified on X-ray
Centre Immature, proliferating fibroblasts (mitoses, atypia may be seen), ± cartilage · not calcified

TO keymature fibroblasts at periphery, proliferating at centre (TO) · Orthobullets: no cellular atypia · biopsy may mimic sarcoma → clinical + radiographic correlation critical
✗ Wrong options: reverse

Differential

Lesion Distinguishing
Myositis ossificans Peripheral maturation, separate from cortex, trauma history
Extraskeletal OS Older, central ossification, no zonation
Parosteal OS Stuck on posterior distal femur, linear radiolucent gap, 30–40 F
Osteochondroma (sessile) Cortex + medulla continuous with bone
Synovial sarcoma Wispy irregular calcification within mass
Periosteal chondrosarcoma On the bone, cartilage mineral
Ossifying lipoma · ossifying mets · extraskeletal chondrosarcoma Different histology

5Management

Management — observation

Myositis ossificans
  • Acute→REST, ICE, NSAIDs, COMPRESSION, SPLINT, ACTIVE ROM (no passive stretching)
  • Asymptomatic / maturing→OBSERVE + SERIAL X-RAYS (no biopsy if imaging diagnostic)
  • Symptomatic after maturity (≥6 mo; ~1 yr)→SURGICAL EXCISION

Passive stretching / massagecontraindicated (worsens) · PT for active ROM

Excisiononly if still a problem after maturation · wait ≥6 months · excision at 6–12 months → recurrence (Orthobullets) · maturation ~1 yr (TO)

Asymptomatic 13-yr-old with peripheral mature ossification after injuryobservation
✗ Wrong options: NSAIDs · rheumatology · biopsy · resection

6Complications

Prognosisself-limiting · mass shrinks after ~1 year

Fibrodysplasia ossificans progressiva (ACVR1)

GeneACVR1 (activin A type I receptor = BMP type-1 receptor) · R206H gain-of-function → uninhibited SMAD signalling (Activin A, BMP 2/4/6/7) · sporadic or AD

Hallmarksprogressive HO of muscles, fascia, tendons, ligaments, capsules after minor trauma ("Stone Man") + congenital bilateral monophalangeal hallux valgus (pathognomonic) · cervical fusions, short broad femoral necks, osteochondromas · stooped posture, trismus, recurrent "bony prominences"

Treatmentconservative · surgery only for refractory pain, motion block, nerve compression (excision provokes more HO)

Gene / protein Condition (distractors)
ACVR1 ✓ FOP
LMX1B Nail-patella
GNAS (Gs-α) Fibrous dysplasia
DTDST (sulfate transporter) Diastrophic dysplasia
RUNX2 Cleidocranial dysplasia
EXT1/2/3 MHE
HLA-B27 Ankylosing spondylitis

How it's asked

"Groin mass a year after helmet blow, dense ossification adjacent to bone, peripheral enhancement?"myositis ossificans
✗ Wrong options: ossifying lipoma · synovial sarcoma · periosteal chondrosarcoma

"Stiff TKA 6 mo after baseball hit to thigh, prostate cancer history?"myositis ossificans
✗ Wrong options: ossifying mets · parosteal OS · sessile osteochondroma

"Most likely cause?"contusion

"Medial arm mass after arm wrestling, peripheral mineral on CT?"myositis ossificans (zonation of Ackerman)
✗ Wrong options: extraskeletal OS · parosteal OS

"Thigh mass after jogging?"myositis ossificans
✗ Wrong options: sarcoma · pseudoaneurysm · abscess

"Quadriceps ossified mass in footballer — linked to?"history of thigh trauma

"Asymptomatic ossified hip 'knot' 4 mo after injury?"observation

"Stone man, trismus, bony prominences, sister affected?"ACVR1 mutation
✗ Wrong options: EXT · HLA-B27

"Thigh deformity since youth, ACVR1 mutation?"FOP
✗ Wrong options: melorheostosis · Paget · FD · MHE

"Congenital bilateral hallux valgus + stiffness — mutated protein?"activin receptor type 1A

"True about myositis ossificans?"mature fibroblasts at periphery, proliferating at centre (TO)

Neurilemmoma

Pathology · 41 / 66

1Basics

Synonymschwannoma · benign, encapsulated nerve sheath tumour of Schwann cells on the surface (epineurium) of a peripheral nerve · motor or sensory nerves

Age / sex3rd–6th decades (tested ages 40, 45; 20–50) · M = F

Siteflexor surfaces of extremities, head and neck · large ones in pelvis · tibial nerve (posterior ankle, tarsal tunnel), median, superficial peroneal

Malignant transformationextremely rare (vs neurofibroma)

Genes & lab markers

NF2 gene mutations
✗ Wrong options: NF1 (neurofibroma)

S-100strong, uniform/diffuse staining

3Presentation

Usually asymptomatic · slow-growing mass · paraesthesia in nerve distribution · discomfort with shoe wear

Percussion (Tinel) signradiating pain distally on tapping = most reliable sign · sensory deficit possible, motor weakness rare · rest pain = worrying for MPNST

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

MRIT1 dark, T2 bright, diffuse gadolinium enhancement · fusiform, homogeneous, in continuity with a named nerve · eccentric to nerve

String signnerve tapering into tumour above and below · target sign — central low signal

Benign vs MPNST on MRIwell-defined capsule favours benign
✗ Wrong options: >2 cm peritumoral oedema · lack of enhancement · ≥2 deep lobulations · limb location irrelevant

Hard to separate from neurofibroma on MRI

Histology

Grossgrey, well encapsulated in nerve sheath

Feature Description
Antoni A Dense, cellular spindle cells in intersecting bundles ("spindle cell battle formation")
Antoni B Hypocellular, loose myxoid areas
Verocay bodies Two rows of palisading nuclei — pathognomonic

Differential

Schwannoma Neurofibroma MPNST
Relation to nerve Eccentric, on surface, encapsulated Within nerve, fusiform From nerve/neurofibroma
Gene NF2 NF1 NF1
Histology Antoni A/B, Verocay Wavy nuclei, mast cells Pleomorphic spindle cells
Excision Marginal, shell out, nerve preserved Requires nerve sacrifice Wide + nerve
Recurrence / malignancy Rare / extremely rare Higher / can transform —

Othersganglion (mucin-filled synovial-lined sac, near joint) · nodular fasciitis (deep fascia) · lipoma (bright T1 + T2) · haemangioma · synovial sarcoma (periarticular, heterogeneous)

5Management

Treatment — marginal excision

Observationasymptomatic

Marginal (intracapsular) excisionsymptomatic / affecting QoL · excise parallel to nerve fascicles → tumour extrudes, nerve preserved · recurrence rare (<1%) · small risk of sensory deficit

Not indicatedwide resection / nerve reconstruction · radiation · chemotherapy · RFA

Fibrolipomatous hamartoma

Intraneural lipomacongenital, benign · median nerve commonest · MRI: "coaxial cable" (axial) / "spaghetti" (longitudinal) — fat between thickened fascicles (T1 bright) · asymptomatic → observe · symptomatic → carpal tunnel decompression ± epineurolysis

How it's asked

"Posterior ankle mass, percussion radiates into foot — true?"Verocay bodies on histology
✗ Wrong options: NF1 · mucin sac (ganglion) · MPNST transformation · high recurrence

"Tender shoulder mass, Antoni A/B, uniform S-100?"schwannoma
✗ Wrong options: MFH · lymphoma · synovial sarcoma · neurofibroma

"String + target signs, palisading nuclei?"schwannoma
✗ Wrong options: nodular fasciitis · lipoma · haemangioma

"Medial ankle mass, Tinel over tarsal tunnel?"neurilemmoma
✗ Wrong options: neurofibroma · ganglion

"MRI feature favouring benign nerve sheath tumour?"well-defined capsule

"Symptomatic arm schwannoma — treatment?"marginal resection only
✗ Wrong options: + RT · wide + chemo · RFA

✗ Wrong options: Ewing · OS · liposarcoma · impending fracture

"Superficial peroneal schwannoma with paraesthesia?"marginal excision
✗ Wrong options: observation · wide resection

Neuroblastoma

Pathology · 42 / 66

1Basics

Naturemalignant tumour of sympathetic neural tissue (neural crest) · near adrenal gland or spinal cord (neck, chest, abdomen, pelvis)

Incidence~1/100,000 children · slight male predominance · majority <2 yr

Rankcommonest (malignant) solid tumour of childhood (Orthobullets, OB) · TO: commonest extracranial / abdominal / infancy tumour; commonest solid tumour overall = brain tumour — read the options · leukaemia = commonest childhood malignancy (not solid)

Small round blue cell tumour by age<5 yr → metastatic neuroblastoma (also leukaemia) · bone mets common in children

3Presentation

Systemicfever, malaise, weight loss, diarrhoea · tachycardia (adrenal)

Examabdominal mass, hepatosplenomegaly

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raybone mets: permeative, lytic, anywhere · may cause vertebra plana

CT chest/abdomen/pelvisinitial study, visceral extent · MRI — anatomy · bone scan — bony mets

Biopsyneedle or open incisional required

Histology

Small round blue cells forming rosettes (Homer-Wright) · neurofilament + (vs Ewing −)

Other small round blue cell tumours of childhoodrhabdomyosarcoma, non-Hodgkin lymphoma, Ewing/PNET, blastemic Wilms

5Management

Neuroblastoma
  • Very young infant, low risk→OBSERVE (spontaneous regression)
  • Low grade, resectable with wide margin→SURGICAL EXCISION
  • High grade / metastatic, unresectable→CHEMO (cisplatin, ifosfamide) + STEM-CELL TRANSPLANT
  • High grade near NV structures→CHEMO + SCT + EXCISION

6Complications

Prognosismajority cured · bone metastasis = poor prognosis · spontaneous regression in very young

How it's asked

"Most common malignant solid tumour of childhood?"neuroblastoma
✗ Wrong options: leukaemia (not solid) · lymphoma · Ewing · EG

"Most common tumour in children (FNB)?"brain tumour (neuroblastoma = commonest extracranial) (TO, OS chapter)

"Small round blue cell tumour in a child <5?"metastatic neuroblastoma

Neurofibroma

Pathology · 43 / 66

1Basics

Naturebenign peripheral nerve sheath tumour · mostly with neurofibromatosis (NF1)

Agesporadic 20–40 · NF1 <20

Sitesporadic: peripheral nerves of extremities · NF1: near spinal cord/brain, unusual sites (parotid, tongue)

Cellsfibroblasts predominate + non-myelinating Schwann cells (cell of origin) + perineural cells, mast cells (vs schwannoma = Schwann cells only)

Genes & lab markers

NF1autosomal dominant, 17q11.2 · biallelic inactivation of NF1 tumour-suppressor in non-myelinating Schwann cells → loss of neurofibromin → ↑ RAS (RAS-MAPK) activity

MPNST transformation+ loss of CDKN2A or TP53

S-100variable (vs uniform in schwannoma)

Schwann cell Axons Disease
Myelinating Large-diameter, myelin —
Non-myelinating Small-diameter, cytoplasmic processes Neurofibromatosis

Subtypes

Dermal Plexiform
Origin Single nerve Multiple nerve bundles
Look Fusiform swelling "Bag of worms"
Association 90% sporadic, 10% NF1 Pathognomonic of NF1
Onset Puberty Early childhood
Malignancy Never 10% → MPNST

3Presentation

Symptomsasymptomatic · stinging, itching, pain, disfigurement · exquisite tenderness (benign nerve sheath tumour hallmark) · reduced sensation

Cutaneous"button-holes" through skin defect with finger pressure

NF1 diagnostic criteria (NIH — 2 of 7)

Criterion
≥2 neurofibromas or 1 plexiform
Axillary/groin freckling (Crowe sign)
≥6 café-au-lait spots ("coast of California") — >5 mm prepubertal, >15 mm postpubertal
Sphenoid wing dysplasia or long-bone cortical thinning (anterolateral tibial bowing)
≥2 Lisch nodules (iris hamartomas, slit lamp)
Optic glioma
First-degree relative with NF1

NF1 associationsdystrophic kyphoscoliosis, rib penciling, intraspinal dumbbell neurofibromas, dural ectasia, meningiomas · cancer risk ~4×

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

MRI ± contrastT1 low, T2 very high (myxoid-type)

Sign Meaning
Nerve continuity (entering-exiting) Fusiform tumour in continuity with NV bundle
Split fat Thin rim of fat around mass in NV bundle
Target High-signal periphery, low centre (STIR)

Neurofibroma vs schwannomaneurofibroma central to nerve (fascicles run through it, no capsule) · schwannoma eccentric, encapsulated

PET-CTdistinguishes MPNST from benign neurofibroma (↑ SUV) in NF1 with multiple nodules
✗ Wrong options: UPS vs MPNST · infection vs tumour

Histology

Hypocellular, fibroblasts + Schwann cells, mast cells, lymphocytes · elongated, wavy nuclei · "wire-like"/rope-like wavy collagen · may be myxoid · corpuscle-like (Pacini/Meissner) structures

Histology Diagnosis
Wavy collagen, elongated cells, hypocellular ✓ Neurofibroma
Herringbone, atypia Fibrosarcoma
Stromal cells, haemosiderin macrophages, giant cells GCT of tendon sheath
Mono/biphasic epithelioid cells Synovial sarcoma

MPNST risk

MPNST in NF5% (10–25% lifetime) · usually from plexiform · painful, enlarging mass · poor prognosis

5Management

Observeasymptomatic

Surgical excisionsymptomatic · tumour within nerve → may need nerve sacrifice / grafting (vs schwannoma shelled out)

How it's asked

"Forearm mass 6 yr, episodic sharp pain, fusiform on median nerve?"nerve sheath tumour
✗ Wrong options: sarcoma · metastasis · haematoma · abscess

"Buttock pain sitting, sciatic paraesthesia, wavy collagen?"neurofibroma
✗ Wrong options: myxoid liposarcoma · myxoma · MFH · fibromatosis

"Exquisitely tender long-standing leg mass in tibial nerve, rope-like collagen?"neurofibroma
✗ Wrong options: fibrosarcoma · GCT tendon sheath · synovial sarcoma

"NF1 with multiple nodules — PET-CT distinguishes?"MPNST vs benign neurofibroma

Nodular Fasciitis

Pathology · 44 / 66

1Basics

Naturecommonest fibrous soft-tissue lesion · pseudosarcomatous, self-limiting reactive proliferation of fibroblasts + myofibroblasts

DemographicsM = F · adults 20–40 · young people

Sitehalf in upper limb · volar forearm, back, chest wall, head and neck

Triggeroften a recalled trauma

Evolutionshort duration → myxoid · longer → hyaline fibrosis

Marginal resection

Treated with marginal excision
