Non-Ossifying Fibroma

Pathology · 45 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Commonest benign bone lesion of childhood (with osteochondroma) · in 30–40% of skeletally immature children · multiple in 8%

Demographicsmales 2:1 · 5–15 yr

Sitemetaphysis of long bones · 80% lower limb: distal femur > proximal tibia > distal tibia · uncommon proximal femur/humerus

Synonymsmetaphyseal fibrous defect, non-osteogenic fibroma, fibrous cortical defect (<2 cm), fibroxanthoma

Pathophysiology

Benign fibrogenic lesion from dysfunctional ossification — abnormal subperiosteal osteoclastic resorption during metaphyseal remodelling

Geneticssomatic RAS-MAPK activating mutations → considered a neoplasm (RASopathy family) · mutation in a subset of cells; histiocytes + giant cells reactive

Associated conditions

Jaffe-Campanacci syndromemultiple NOFs + café-au-lait + mental retardation/cognitive delay + heart, eye, gonadal defects + giant cell lesions of the mandible
✗ Wrong options: Jaffe-Lichtenstein (FD + café-au-lait) · Maffucci · Mazabraud · McCune-Albright

Othersneurofibromatosis · familial multifocal NOF · ABC

Genes & lab markers

RAS-MAPK somatic mutations · no lab abnormality

3Presentation

Asymptomatic, incidentalfound after ankle/knee sprain or fall · pain from the injury, not the lesion

Pathologic fracture± stress fractures with cortical involvement

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (diagnostic)eccentric, metaphyseal, cortically based "bubbly"/scalloped lytic lesion with thin sclerotic rim · narrow zone of transition · cortex may be thinned/expanded · length > width · no periosteal reaction, no soft-tissue mass

With growthmigrates to diaphysis, enlarges (1–7 cm) · near maturity → sclerotic, reossifies

Quantitative CTpredicts fracture risk

MRIT1 low · T2 varies (higher when developing) · thin enhancing rim · rarely needed

Mimicadductor magnus avulsion / cortical desmoid at posteromedial distal femur

No biopsy, MRI, bone scan, staging for classic lesions

Histology

Fibroblastic spindle cells in whorled / storiform pattern ("helicopters in a wheat field") · fibroblastic background · lipophages (foamy histiocytes), giant cells · haemosiderin · occasional ABC component

Differential

Bubbly lytic X-ray Haemosiderin Observation alone
NOF ✓ ✓ ✓
ABC · UBC ✓ — —
PVNS — ✓ —
FD · enchondroma · osteochondroma · EG · Paget — — ✓
Lesion Distinguishing
NOF Eccentric, scalloped, metaphyseal, sclerotic rim
UBC Central, expands to physis width, fallen leaf / fallen fragment sign
FD Central, ground glass
GCT Painful, skeletally mature, subchondral, no mineral, destructive
Osteofibrous dysplasia Diaphyseal, anterior tibial cortex
Enchondroma Central, mineralized, not sclerotic rim
Osteosarcoma Painful, permeative, periosteal reaction, mineralized mass
Chondroblastoma Epiphyseal

Cortical desmoid (TO)

Posteromedial distal femoral metaphysis (adductor magnus traction), boys 10–15 · cortical erosion with sclerotic base, no lytic lesion · no biopsy, observe · "posterolateral" is wrong (TO)

5Management

Non-ossifying fibroma
  • Incidental, asymptomatic→OBSERVE (X-ray 6 + 12 mo, then yearly; or 2–3/3–6 mo check)
  • Pathologic fracture, non-displaced→CAST / SLING, let fracture heal, then observe
  • Symptomatic + large (>50–75% cortex / >50% diameter) or high QCT risk→CURETTAGE + GRAFT ± PROPHYLACTIC FIXATION

Observationfirst line · reossifies spontaneously in 2nd–3rd decade · non-urgent referral
✗ Wrong options: biopsy · MRI · bone scan · CT staging · cast · CPS

Pathologic fracturetreat the fracture (casting · humerus → sling + analgesia) · curettage only after healing unless ORIF needed
✗ Wrong options: IM nail without diagnosis · aspiration + injection (UBC)

Curettage + bone graftingsymptomatic + large (>50–75% cortical involvement / just over half diameter in weight-bearing bone) · persistent activity pain → + prophylactic internal fixation
✗ Wrong options: RFA · doxycycline · steroid injection · sclerotherapy · wide excision

6Complications

Pathologic fracture

90% lower limb · 50% distal tibia

Risk factors>50% of transverse diameter · >33 mm length in weight-bearing bones

Prognosisspontaneous resolution · no malignant or metastatic potential
✗ Wrong options: 5–10% (that's MHE)

How it's asked

"20-yr-old skier, incidental lateral distal femoral sclerotic-rimmed lesion?"reassurance + WBAT
✗ Wrong options: biopsy · resection

"Distal fibula lesion after sprain, asymptomatic 16-yr-old?"activities as tolerated, X-ray in 3–6 mo
✗ Wrong options: staging · cast · curettage

"6-yr-old, knee abrasion, tibial lesion?"repeat X-ray in 3 months

"14-yr-old tibial lesion after ankle injury?"routine follow-up
✗ Wrong options: endocrine (McCune-Albright) · GI cancer (Gardner) · open biopsy

"18-yr-old soccer player — diagnosis + treatment?"NOF, observation
✗ Wrong options: enchondroma · ABC curettage

"Associated with NOF EXCEPT?"fallen leaf sign (UBC) (✓ stress fractures, ✓ commonest childhood lesion, ✓ Jaffe-Campanacci, ✓ haemosiderin)

"9-yr-old, mild ankle pain, family doctor calls?"symptom management, non-urgent referral
✗ Wrong options: urgent CT/labs · RFA · curettage

"Incidental distal tibial / proximal tibial lesion?"observation
✗ Wrong options: MRI · bone scan · biopsy

"15-yr-old 'shadow on the bone', eccentric sclerotic metaphyseal?"NOF
✗ Wrong options: GCT · infection · OFD (diaphyseal) · enchondroma

"NOF best characterized as?"eccentric scalloped metaphyseal radiolucency
✗ Wrong options: fallen leaf · central fusiform to physis width (UBC) · ground glass (FD)

"True of NOF?"typically ossifies spontaneously
✗ Wrong options: trauma-related · cast first-line · high fracture risk · 5–10% malignancy

"Trampoline ankle injury, bubbly lesion — histology?"storiform fibroblasts

"Pathologic fracture through which lesion (lobulated, eccentric)?"NOF

"Humeral pathologic fracture through eccentric soap-bubble lesion?"sling + pain medication

"Outside core biopsy, no treatment needed — histology?"whorled/storiform fibroblastic spindle cells

"11-yr-old gymnast, limp, NOF >50% diameter, daily pain?"curettage + bone grafting + prophylactic internal fixation

"Persistent pain, 4 cm NOF over half the diameter?"curettage + bone grafting
✗ Wrong options: observation · cast · sclerotherapy

"Multiple NOFs, café-au-lait, cognitive delay, eye problems, mandibular lesion?"Jaffe-Campanacci syndrome

"Cortical desmoid — all true EXCEPT?""posterolateral distal femur" (it is posteromedial) (TO)

Osteoblastoma

Pathology · 46 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Rare1% of primary bone tumours, 1–5% (3%) of benign · 10% of osseous spinal neoplasms · less common than osteoid osteoma

DemographicsM:F 2:1 · 10–30 yr

Siteposterior elements of spine + sacrum (30–40%; >35–40%) · long bones (lower > upper), diaphysis/metadiaphysis (femur commonest), never epiphyseal · mandible ("cementoblastoma")

Spinal level (TO)cervical > lumbar > thoracic > sacrum (17%) — no firm consensus; keyed answer cervical

Posterior element tumours (TO)osteoid osteoma > osteoblastoma > ABC · GCT → vertebral body

Naturebenign, locally aggressive, progressive
✗ Wrong options: self-limiting

Genes & lab markers

FOS (chr 14) / FOSB (chr 19) rearrangements

Nuclear β-catenin staining → osteoblastoma · cytoplasmic/membranous → osteosarcoma

Associationssecondary ABC 10–40% · oncogenic osteomalacia

3Presentation

Painslowly progressive dull ache · night pain not typical · partial NSAID response (TO FNB stem: night pain improving with NSAIDs, posterior element, 28 yr → keyed osteoblastoma)

Spineneurologic symptoms (root compression, weakness, rarely paraplegia), scoliosis

Signsswelling, muscle atrophy, limp · may be incidental

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raylytic or mixed lytic-blastic, radiolucent nidus 2–10 cm (>2 cm), intralesional densities · reactive sclerosis · expansile · 66% cortical, 33% medullary · 25% look aggressive (mistaken for malignancy)

CTfull characterization: size, location, cortical breach, soft-tissue component

MRIreactive soft-tissue/marrow oedema · T1 low–intermediate, T2 intermediate–high

Bone scanhot, intense focal uptake

Histology

Like osteoid osteoma, more giant cells · sharp demarcation nidus–reactive bone · nidus of immature osteoid + plump osteoblasts (normal nuclei) · irregular osteoid/woven spicules rimmed by osteoblasts in loose fibrovascular stroma · mitoses but no atypical ones · rich vascularity, extravasated RBCs · no permeation of host lamellar bone

Osteoid osteoma vs osteoblastoma

Osteoid osteoma Osteoblastoma
Incidence 10% of benign 3% of benign
Size <2 cm (<1.5) >2 cm (3–3.5)
Site >50% long-bone diaphysis (proximal femur > tibia > phalanges > spine) >35% posterior spine
Natural history Self-limited Progressive
Symptoms Night pain, relieved by NSAIDs · painful scoliosis, lesion on concave side Dull ache, partial NSAID response, neuro symptoms
Spine management Nonoperative first → RFA/resection Always surgery

Differential

Lesion Distinguishing
Osteosarcoma Periostitis, soft-tissue mass, permeates lamellar bone, malignant cells, cytoplasmic β-catenin
ABC Fluid levels, septations, blood-filled sinusoids
Osteoid osteoma <2 cm, thick sclerosis around small nidus
GCT Epiphysis–metaphysis, giant cells with stromal nuclei
Chondroblastoma Epiphyseal, chondroid
NOF Eccentric metaphyseal, storiform
FD Ground glass, Chinese letters
Osteomyelitis · stress fracture Sinus/skin changes · no large lucent lesion

5Management

Osteoblastoma (doesn't respond to nonoperative care)
  • Most→CURETTAGE / MARGINAL EXCISION + BONE GRAFT (± phenol, cryo)
  • Large, locally aggressive, recurrent, some spinal→EN BLOC RESECTION (lower recurrence)
  • Selected small lesions→ABLATION (RFA / MRgFUS)

Observationrarely (keeps growing)

Treatment — ablation (MRgFUS vs RFA) (TO)

MRgFUSless invasive (needleless, no ionizing radiation), real-time thermal map · does not penetrate deeper (deep lesions → RFA needle reaches better)

RFACT-guided, ~17G needle, single-day stay

Bothequally safe/effective · equal resolution of bone oedema (oedema loss = inactive lesion) · post-ablation "halo/ring" sign (necrotic centre + sublethal rim) · not "RFA less chondral damage" (claim not supported)

6Complications

Local recurrence15–25%, mostly within 2 years

Prognosisexcellent, most disease-free after first surgery

How it's asked

"Most common site of osteoblastoma (spinal levels)?"cervical spine (keyed) (TO)

"28-yr-old, posterior element tumour, night pain improves with NSAIDs, no deficit?"osteoblastoma (keyed; ✗ Wrong options: ABC · GCT · lymphoma) (TO)

"17-yr-old, distal humeral bone-forming lesion extending beyond cortex, large osteoblasts, no atypia?"osteoblastoma
✗ Wrong options: osteoid osteoma (smaller) · OS · chondroblastoma · GCT

"18-yr-old knee, well-marginated lytic/blastic, benign osteoblasts?"osteoblastoma
✗ Wrong options: FD · OS · NOF · osteomyelitis

"23-yr-old, back pain radiating down leg relieved by NSAIDs, mixed lesion >2 cm?"osteoblastoma
✗ Wrong options: OS · GCT · ABC · stress fracture

"True about osteoblastoma ablation?"MRgFUS less invasive than RFA (TO)

"Touch-me-not lesions EXCEPT?"osteoblastoma (painful, needs intervention) (TO, FD chapter)

Osteochondroma & Multiple Hereditary Exostosis

Pathology · 47 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Commonest benign bone tumour (20–50%) · commonest benign cartilage tumour · tested ages 9–24 (adolescents, young adults)

Sitebone surface, metaphysis, often at tendon insertions · knee (distal femur > proximal tibia) · proximal femur, proximal humerus · femur 30%, tibia + humerus 10–20% each · 2nd commonest site: pelvis (iliac crest) (TO) · spine rare (posterior elements of cervical spine) · never facial bones (intramembranous) · subungual exostosis (hallux)

Polyostotic conditions (TO)FD (McCune-Albright), MHE ("diaphyseal aclasis"), enchondroma (Ollier/Maffucci)
✗ Wrong options: haemangioma (non-polyostotic)

Pathophysiology

Hamartomatous bone + cartilage · physeal cartilage escapes through a perichondral ring (node/zone of Ranvier) defect and grows by endochondral ossification under the periosteum → grows away from the joint

Secondary causesSalter-Harris fracture · surgery · radiation (commonest benign radiation-induced bone tumour, 6–24%, latency 3–17 yr)

MHE genetics (EXT1/EXT2, heparan sulfate, AD)

Inheritanceautosomal dominant · penetrance 96% females, 100% males (>95%) · child of affected parent ≥50% risk · ~15% of osteochondroma patients

GenesEXT1 (8q24), EXT2 (11p13), EXT3 (19) — tumour suppressors (exostosins) → ↓ heparan sulfate in physeal chondrocytes · affect prehypertrophic chondrocytes · dysregulated Indian hedgehog signalling

EXT1 vs EXT2EXT1 more severe: more exostoses, more malalignment, shorter limbs/height, less forearm + knee ROM, more pelvic/flat-bone lesions, higher chondrosarcoma rate
✗ Wrong options: lower

Gene Condition (distractors)
EXT1/2/3 ✓ MHE
PHEX X-linked hypophosphataemic rickets
PMP-22 Charcot-Marie-Tooth
SYT-SSX Synovial sarcoma
GNAS FD / McCune-Albright
IDH1 Ollier / Maffucci
DTDST Diastrophic dysplasia (cauliflower ear, hitchhiker thumb) — AR
FGFR2 · FGFR3 Apert · achondroplasia
RUNX2/CBFA1 Cleidocranial dysplasia

Other inheritance contrastsOllier sporadic · fibular hemimelia none · sickle (infarcts) AR

3Presentation

Solitarypainless mass · mechanical symptoms, irritation (bursitis), NV compression · grows until skeletal maturity

MHEfamily history · multiple masses (knees, wrists, shoulders)

Forearm & ankle deformity in MHE

Deformitiesshort stature, LLD, femoral shortening · coxa valga (lesser trochanter lesions) · knee valgus + patellar dislocation and ankle valgus (short fibula) · ulnar shortening + radial bowing, ulnar deviation of wrist, radial head (radiocapitellar) dislocation · pectoral/pelvic asymmetry · premature OA · no absent thumb

Masada Description Frequency
I Distal ulnar exostosis → short ulna + radial bowing, radial head NOT dislocated Commonest (55–61%) (TO)
II Short ulna + radial head dislocated · IIa proximal radial exostosis · IIb no proximal radial exostosis 22–33%
III Distal radial exostosis → relative radial shortening —

Masada treatment (TO)I: conservative / excise painful lesion · II: wait to ~13 yr, then levelling ± radial head resection or ring-fixator reduction · III: excise painful lesion, ulnar shortening for positive variance

Forearm rotation lossosteochondroma excision most effective · deformity → excision + ulnar lengthening + radial closing-wedge osteotomy
✗ Wrong options: osteotomies · radial head resection

Ankle valgus in a 7-yr-oldmedial (distal tibial) hemiepiphysiodesis
✗ Wrong options: full epiphysiodesis · cast · curettage · resection alone

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (diagnostic)sessile (broad) or pedunculated (stalk) surface lesion · pedunculated points away from the joint (in both solitary and MHE) · cortex continuous with host · medullary cavity continuous with host (pathognomonic) · cartilage cap radiolucent, involutes at maturity

Cartilage capMRI best (T1 low, T2 high) · US and CT also measure it

Cap thickness Meaning
Child (immature) 1–3 cm normal — thick cap = growth, not malignancy
Adult (mature) 2–3 mm normal
Adult >2 cm (≥2 cm) Secondary chondrosarcoma concern (Orthobullets, TO) · OB variants: >1.5 cm, ">1–2 cm", ">2–3 cm" · thickening in adulthood (✗ Wrong options: thinning)

Biopsynot recommended for diagnosis
✗ Wrong options: needle biopsy · bone scan · PET

Histology

Like a normal physishyaline cartilage cap + well-defined perichondrium + linear columns of active chondrocytes + normal primary trabeculae · stalk = cortical + cancellous bone

Differential (bony bumps)

Lesion Key feature
Osteochondroma Cortical + medullary continuity
Subungual exostosis (Dupuytren) Dorsal distal phalanx (hallux), no medullary continuity
Trevor disease (dysplasia epiphysealis hemimelica) Epiphyseal osteochondromas, lower limb, M:F 3:1, no malignancy
Turret exostosis Dome on dorsal proximal/middle phalanx, post-traumatic, no continuity
BPOP (Nora lesion) Hand phalanges/metacarpals, bone + cartilage + fibrous, high recurrence
Parosteal OS Posterior distal femur, dense lobulated, no continuity
Juxtacortical chondroma Saucerized cortex, 20–40
Subperiosteal haematoma Smooth elliptical, trauma
Myositis ossificans Doesn't share cortex, zonation

"Benign bone tumour presenting as a bump"osteochondroma (turret exostosis + Nora lesion = non-tumours) (TO)

Cartilage tumours (TO)chondroblastoma, osteochondroma, CMF all cartilage → "EXCEPT" = none

5Management

Treatment — observation vs marginal excision

Osteochondroma
  • Asymptomatic / minimal (incl. MHE child)→OBSERVATION
  • Symptomatic, cosmetic, mechanical→MARGINAL RESECTION at stalk base + whole cartilage cap
    (delay to skeletal maturity if possible → ↓ recurrence)
  • MHE deformity→excision, hemiepiphysiodesis, osteotomy
  • Growth/pain after maturity, cap >2 cm → MRI/biopsy → secondary chondrosarcoma→WIDE RESECTION

Marginal resectionresect at base of stalk including entire cartilage cap (↓ regrowth) · graft/fix large defect
✗ Wrong options: chemo · RT · curettage/liquid N₂ · radical resection

No further tests before excising a classic symptomatic lesion (MRI already done)

Incidental humeral / femoral sessile lesion, asymptomaticobservation
✗ Wrong options: MRI, CT, bone scan, biopsy, long-leg films if alignment normal

MHE childobserve · excision for radial head dislocation, loss of rotation, symptoms
✗ Wrong options: biopsy largest · PET · remove all · bisphosphonates

Subungual exostosis

Hallux, adolescents · reactive metaplasia (microtrauma) · painful, may ulcerate · mistaken for wart · histology: mature trabecular bone capped by fibrocartilage

Treatmentnail removal + excision / osteotomy through base, local wound care (protect nail matrix) · recurrence possible
✗ Wrong options: observation if painful · amputation · chemo/RT

6Complications

Malignant transformation to chondrosarcoma

Setting Risk
Solitary osteochondroma <1%
MHE 5–10% (Orthobullets) · 3–10% · 0.57–8.3% · ~10% · "1 in 10,000" (one OB) — read options

Risk factorspelvic/central (proximal) location (highest), sessile, MHE, EXT1, older age, cap >2 cm in adult

Secondary chondrosarcomalow grade (~90%; 67–85%) · 3rd–4th decade (tested 50) · pelvis commonest · <1% in children · presents as new pain / growth after maturity · pelvic mass → urinary retention, perineal numbness

Treatmentwide resection (same as chondrosarcoma), curative 70–90% · pelvic → multidisciplinary (general + plastic)
✗ Wrong options: chemo · RT · local excision

Other complications

Vascularpopliteal artery pseudoaneurysm (rare), compression, true aneurysm, arterial/venous thrombosis

Nerve compression (up to 25%)sciatic, common peroneal (anterior + lateral compartment atrophy), radial

Tendonshoulder: cuff impingement, subscapularis tear, bicipital tendinitis

Bursa formation (± metaplastic cartilage nodules) · recurrence 2–5% after resection

How it's asked

"HME — EXCEPT?""exostoses grow towards the physis" (they grow away) (✓ AD, ✓ prehypertrophic chondrocytes, ✓ EXT1-3, ✓ medullary continuity)

"MHE — EXCEPT?""grow towards joint in MHE but away in solitary" (both grow away)

"NOT associated with MHE?"absence of the thumb

"EXT1 vs EXT2 — all EXCEPT?"lower sarcomatous transformation (EXT1 is higher)

"AD gene defect with ↑ chondrosarcoma risk?"EXT-1
✗ Wrong options: PHEX, PMP-22, SYT-SSX, GNAS-1

"EXT-1 defect — diagnosis + tumour risk?"MHE, chondrosarcoma

"X-ray for EXT1/2/3 mutation?"multiple osteochondromas
✗ Wrong options: rickets · cleidocranial · NF bowing · Madelung

"Which X-ray shows an AD disorder?"MHE
✗ Wrong options: infarct/sickle AR · diastrophic AR · fibular hemimelia · Ollier sporadic

"MHE with EXT — true?"↓ heparan sulfate in physis
✗ Wrong options: cap thinning = malignancy · EXT2 worse · 25% malignancy · distal lesions riskier

"Father with exostoses + hemiepiphysiodesis, child with bumps — gene?"EXT1

"Incidental lesion — true in children?"secondary transformation usually low grade, cured by surgery alone
✗ Wrong options: cap <1 mm · 20%

"62-yr-old, enlarging lifelong medial thigh bump, multiple bumps?"EXT; secondary low-grade chondrosarcoma

"MHE, pelvic grade 2 chondrosarcoma with urinary retention?"wide resection with multidisciplinary team

"Highest risk of malignant transformation?"pelvic location
✗ Wrong options: sessile · isolated · young age · 2 mm cap

"Non-polyostotic tumour?"haemangioma (TO)

"2nd commonest site of osteochondroma?"pelvis (TO)

"Benign tumour presenting as bump on long bones?"osteochondroma (TO)

"Sessile medial distal femoral lesion sharing cortex + medulla?"osteochondroma
✗ Wrong options: myositis ossificans · parosteal OS

"Thoracic transverse-process lesion on trauma CT — aetiology?"aberrant cartilaginous growth

"Symptomatic pedunculated tibial osteochondroma?"marginal resection
✗ Wrong options: chemo · radical · curettage + liquid N₂

"14-yr-old, sessile anterior femoral exostosis, painless?"observation

"Painful, cosmetic distal femoral osteochondroma, skeletally mature?"marginal resection with complete cap removal

"Tests before resecting classic symptomatic lesion?"none

"7-yr-old with multiple exostoses?"observation

"Most common forearm deformity in HME?"ulnar exostosis with radial bowing (Masada I) (TO)

"Limited pronation/supination in MHE?"osteochondroma excision

"7-yr-old, ankle valgus from fibular exostosis?"medial hemiepiphysiodesis

"Painful hallux lesion misdiagnosed as wart?"nail removal + excision / osteotomy through base

Osteofibrous Dysplasia

Pathology · 48 / 66

1Basics

Naturerare, benign fibro-osseous tumour-like lesion of the tibial (± fibular) cortex · aka ossifying fibroma

Age<10 yr (peak 1–5) · M > F

Siteanterior tibial cortex, diaphysis (± metaphysis)

Genes & lab markers

Trisomies 7, 8, 12, 22 reported

No Gs-α (GNAS) mutation (vs fibrous dysplasia)

Keratin − (vs adamantinoma: keratin + epithelial nests)

3Presentation

Painless swelling of the shin · may be tender when bumped

Deformityanterior / anterolateral tibial bowing (procurvatum), limp

Pseudarthrosis10–30%

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral)eccentric, intracortical, multiloculated lytic lesion of the anterior tibial cortex · well-marginated lucencies, sclerotic borders · expands/thins anterior cortex · no periosteal reaction · confined to anterior cortex

CTcortical expansion, no periosteal reaction or matrix · bone scan — focally hot

Biopsyneeded when young adult (15–35) or red flags (growth, change, aggressive features) → exclude adamantinoma

Histology

Like fibrous dysplasia EXCEPT osteoblastic rimmingfibroblast proliferation (bland spindle stroma) around woven bone islands lined by osteoblasts (lamellar rim) · mitoses common · ± giant cells · no epithelial nests

Differential

Tibial diaphysis Observation alone
OFD Yes Yes
Adamantinoma Yes No (wide resection)
FD · EG · enchondroma · osteochondroma · NOF · Paget — Yes
OFD Adamantinoma Fibrous dysplasia
Age <10 20–40 <30
Location Anterior tibial cortex Anterior mid-tibial cortex Medullary, femur/ribs/skull
Histology Woven bone with osteoblastic rimming Epithelial nests, keratin + Woven bone without rimming
Gene Trisomies — GNAS
Treatment Observe Wide resection Observe / fix

OthersNOF (doesn't erode cortex, storiform) · UBC/ABC (metaphyseal, femur/humerus) · Ewing (onion skin, round cells) · EG

5Management

Osteofibrous dysplasia
  • Most→OBSERVATION (annual follow-up) — regresses by maturity
  • Significant deformity interfering with walking→BRACE
  • Impending pathologic fracture / pain→CURETTAGE + GRAFT
  • Persistent deformity after skeletal maturity→CORRECTIVE OSTEOTOMY

Avoid surgery before ~15 yrvery high local recurrence

No curettage + grafting for routine OFD · (✗ Wrong options: casting · wide resection · chemo)
✗ Wrong options: "accepted for all EXCEPT OFD" question (see ABC chapter)

6Complications

Prognosislesions regress, no problems in adulthood · link to adamantinoma debated (continuum?)

How it's asked

"3-yr-old, bowing + limp, lucent anterior proximal tibial diaphyseal lesion?"osteofibrous dysplasia
✗ Wrong options: FD (ground glass) · UBC/ABC (metaphyseal) · NOF

"12-yr-old, tender bump below knee, anterior cortical lucencies, osteoblastic rimming?"OFD
✗ Wrong options: adamantinoma · NOF · ABC · low-grade CS

"9-yr-old shin mass, trabeculae lined by osteoblasts?"OFD
✗ Wrong options: NOF · osteoblastoma · chondroblastoma · Ewing

"6-yr-old, tender tibial shaft lucency, hot bone scan?"OFD
✗ Wrong options: GCT · NOF · FD · EG

"10-yr-old painless lower-leg deformity, OFD histology — next?"observation
✗ Wrong options: brace · cast · curettage · osteotomy

"7-yr-old, incidental anterior tibial lesion, keratin-negative biopsy?"observation with annual follow-up
✗ Wrong options: wide resection (adamantinoma)

Osteoid Osteoma

Pathology · 49 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence10–14% of benign bone tumours · 2–3% of primary bone tumours · most common true benign bone tumour (TO)

DemographicsM:F 2.5:1 (Orthobullets) · 2–4:1 (TO, spine) · 5–25 yr, 70% before 20 · peak 2nd decade

Site (Orthobullets)lower limb >50%: proximal femur > tibial diaphysis, cortical · spine 10–15% (thoracolumbar > cervical/sacral, posterior elements, concave side of scoliosis) · hand 5–10% (scaphoid, proximal phalanx) · foot <5% (talar neck)

Site (TO)femur > tibia, cortical, diaphysis (keyed "femur, cortical diaphysis") · vertebrae 7–20% · usually cortical, can be intramedullary, subperiosteal, intra-articular

Spinal OO (TO)lumbar · posterior elements 75% · pars interarticularis commonest · CT: lesion at pedicle–lamina junction

Pathophysiology (COX, PGE2)

Niduscentral nodule of woven bone + osteoid with osteoblastic rimming · reactive zone — thickened bone + fibrovascular tissue

Pain↑ prostaglandin E2 (+ PGI2) and COX-1 / COX-2 expression · ↑ number and size of unmyelinated nerve fibres in nidus

NSAIDscyclooxygenase inhibition → ↓ PGE2 · ibuprofen/naproxen competitive · aspirin irreversible acetylation
✗ Wrong options: mu-opioid agonist · 30S · transpeptidase · ACh agonist

Most important pain mediatorPGE2 · first shown by Makley and Dunn
✗ Wrong options: PGI2 (endothelial COX-2, anti-platelet) · IGF-1 · glutamate · ATP

Associationsscoliosis, lesion on concave side (paravertebral spasm) · LLD (epiphyseal → overgrowth) · flexion contractures

3Presentation

Painconstant, progressive, worse at night and with alcohol, not activity-related, relieved by NSAIDs/aspirin · near joint → mimics arthritis

Spinenight pain 80–100% · painful scoliosis 63–70% (TO) · 75% (Orthobullets) · backache + paravertebral spasm + scoliosis in adolescent → suspect OO

Handpainless swelling possible

Examswelling, tenderness, effusion, contracture, limp, muscle atrophy

Delaymean ~14 months to diagnosis · often misdiagnosed as stress fracture in runners

LabsESR, CRP, WBC normal
✗ Wrong options: osteomyelitis

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (orthogonal views)intense reactive sclerosis around a radiolucent nidus · may be normal early · spine: subtle pedicle sclerosis, typical only in half · obliques help (pedicle, facet, pars) (TO)

CT (modality of choice)nidus <1.5 cm (>1.5 cm → osteoblastoma) · vascular groove sign in 80% (1 mm serpentine cortical lucencies) · bull's-eye calcified centre (50%) · sclerosis may exceed nidus · fine-cut CT separates OO from stress fracture

Bone scanmost sensitive for localization (TO) · double-density sign — intense nidus uptake + lower surrounding uptake

MRInot recommended — marrow oedema masks features · useful for intra-articular nidus · not needed for diagnosis

Diagnosisclinical history + NSAID response + X-ray/CT → no biopsy, no staging, no labs needed

Histology

Distinct demarcation nidus vs reactive bone · uniform immature osteoid trabeculae (woven bone) with osteoblastic rimming · plump, regular osteoblasts · vascular stroma · no infiltration of host bone · no neutrophils/dead bone
✗ Wrong options: Brodie

Enneking staging of benign lesions

Stage Grade Examples
1 Latent Enchondroma, NOF
2 Active Osteoid osteoma, UBC, ABC, chondroblastoma, CMF, GCT
3 Aggressive GCT, ABC

Differential

Lesion Distinguishing
Brodie's abscess Neutrophils, dead bone · similar cortical X-ray
Stress fracture Activity-related · fine-cut CT separates
Osteoblastoma >2 cm, intramedullary, aggressive, dull ache
ABC Posterior elements, fluid levels
Ewing Permeative, onion skin
Low-grade intramedullary OS Intramedullary sclerosis, ~1% of OS
Enchondroma Intramedullary, painless, cartilage
Osteoma Dense juxtacortical bony mass
Chondroblastoma Epiphyseal — OO is diaphyseal (TO)

5Management

Osteoid osteoma
  • First line (most extremity; painful spine without scoliosis)→NSAIDs + OBSERVATION
    (50% burn out over several years)
  • Failed NSAIDs / GI side effects→RFA (standard of care) or MR-HIFU
  • Near skin / nerve / cord, digit, recurrence after RFA, fixed spinal deformity→SURGICAL EXCISION

NSAIDs & observation

First lineNSAIDs + observation
✗ Wrong options: RFA/HIFU/resection before trial · MRI

Incidental OOunrelated acute symptoms (e.g. contralateral radiculopathy after injury) → continued observation

Radiofrequency ablation & MR-HIFU

RFACT-guided electrode into nidus · 90°C for 4–6 min → 1 cm necrosis zone · 90% success in 1–2 sessions (95% one procedure — OB) · 10–15% recurrence · least invasive, fastest resolution, least morbidity

RFA distances>1 cm from skin · >1 cm from articular surface · 1–1.5 cm from neurovascular structures · spine safe with motor response testing

RFA after failed NSAIDship, tibia, femur → RFA
✗ Wrong options: biopsy · CT chest · en bloc · curettage + cement

MR-HIFUnon-invasive, non-ionizing · thermal ablation of periosteal nerves + nidus · comparable to RFA · for failed medical management (e.g. NSAID GI side effects)

Surgical excision (spine, hand)

Indicationsnot amenable to RFA (close to skin, nerve, cord/dura) · digital lesions (RFA → thermal skin necrosis, digital NV injury) · recurrence after RFA · spine with fixed deformity

Spine near neural elementssurgical resection / en bloc excision of nidus
✗ Wrong options: RFA · vertebrectomy/corpectomy · fusion

Digitcurettage with power burr (<10% local recurrence)
✗ Wrong options: RFA · wide resection

Principlecomplete marginal removal of nidus (sclerotic bone can stay) · 94% success

Spinal deformityresolves if excised within 15 months of onset · no TLSO for mild curve

6Complications

RFArecurrence 10–15% · cellulitis, thrombophlebitis, skin necrosis (<1 cm from skin) · NV damage (<1–1.5 cm)

LLDepiphyseal lesion → limb overgrowth

Open curettage/en bloc near hiplate joint morbidity

Prognosispain resolves ~3 yr (2–4 yr, hand) · lesion resolves 5–7 yr · malignant transformation extremely rare

X vs Y

Osteoid osteoma Osteoblastoma
Incidence 10% of benign 3% of benign
Size <2 cm (typically <1.5) >2 cm (3–3.5)
Site >50% long-bone diaphysis · proximal femur > tibia > phalanges > spine >35% posterior spine
Natural history Self-limited Progressive
Symptoms Night pain, relieved by NSAIDs · painful scoliosis (concave side) Dull ache, partial NSAID response, neuro symptoms
Spine treatment Nonoperative first → RFA/resection Surgery always

Paget's Disease

Pathology · 50 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Natureidiopathic abnormal bone remodelling — excessive resorption + abnormal new bone formation (osteitis deformans) · 2nd commonest chronic bone-remodelling disorder in the US after osteoporosis · 3–4% of US population >40

Demographics>40 yr, peak 5th decade · M = F · Caucasians (northern European / Anglo-Saxon)

Distributionmonostotic or polyostotic

Sitesfemur > pelvis > tibia > skull > spine (Orthobullets) · pelvis > tibia (TO) · also clavicle · sacrum typically involved

Pathophysiology — osteoclast abnormality

Primary abnormality↑ osteoclastic bone resorption (✗ Wrong options: osteoblast · osteocyte · chondroblast · synoviocyte) → focally increased remodelling (✗ Wrong options: ↓ vascularity · sclerotic bone replacing marrow)

Causeslow virus (intranuclear nucleocapsid-like inclusions) — paramyxovirus, RSV

Osteoclast diseasesPaget · osteopetrosis (defective osteoclastic resorption) · pycnodysostosis — share nuclear inclusion bodies
✗ Wrong options: OI (osteoblast) · osteochondroma (chondrocyte) · myeloma · osteoporosis (uncoupling, not inherited)

Osteopetrosis genescarbonic anhydrase II · vacuolar proton pump · chloride channel 7

Genes & lab markers

Inheritancemostly spontaneous · familial clusters, ~40% autosomal dominant · family history in 15% (OB)

SQSTM1 (p62/sequestosome 1), 5q35-QTERmost important · severe disease

TNFRSF11Ainsertion mutation in the gene encoding RANK

Labs↑ serum ALP (most useful test; tracks extent + activity) · ↑ urinary collagen cross-links · ↑ urinary hydroxyproline · ↑ urinary N-telopeptide, α-C-telopeptide, deoxypyridinoline · calcium + phosphate normal · PTH normal (↑ in small subset with very active disease)

α-CTXrises more than β-CTX → ↑ α/β CTX ratio (TO) · urinary NTX — sensitive, monitors treatment response (TO)

Hypercalcaemiaonly with immobilization (↑ osteocyte sclerostin), fracture, predominant lytic phase, or sarcoma (TO) · ambulatory → normal calcium

vs myelomaALP ↑ in Paget, normal in myeloma (TO)

Phases

Phase Process X-ray
Lytic Intense osteoclastic resorption, hypervascular Lucent, expanded, thin intact cortex · blade of grass / flame advancing edge · skull osteoporosis circumscripta
Mixed Resorption + compensatory formation (weak brittle bone) Lysis + sclerosis, coarse trabeculae
Sclerotic (burnt-out) Osteoblastic formation dominates · dense avascular mosaic bone Bone enlargement, cortical thickening — commonest presentation

All three phases can coexist in one bone

3Presentation

Asymptomaticcommonest · found incidentally (↑ ALP, X-ray for another complaint)

Paincommonest presenting symptom · dull ache · stress fractures, hypervascularity, warmth · cyclic recurring leg pain

Deformitylong-bone bowing (femur, tibia varus) · kyphosis · short neck (flattened skull base, basilar invagination) · skull enlargement

Fracturesbrittle bone · transverse — banana / incremental fracture (TO) · subtrochanteric

Arthritiship + knee OA (83% have a rheumatic syndrome — OB)

Neurodeafness (CN VIII compression, otosclerosis, cochlear BMD loss) · impaired vision, facial palsy, trigeminal neuralgia · spinal stenosis · vascular steal → cerebral ischaemia, spinal claudication (TO)

Cardiachigh-output heart failure — large/multiple lesions + poor cardiac reserve

Red flagnew intense pain + swelling → Paget sarcoma

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raycoarsened trabeculae, blastic appearance · remodelled cortices — loss of cortex–medulla distinction · bone enlargement + cortical thickening · bowing · fractures · OA

Sign (TO) Finding
Picture-frame vertebra Peripheral cortical thickening (✗ Wrong options: VDRR · fluorosis · MED)
Ivory vertebra Diffusely sclerotic vertebra
Cotton-wool spots Fluffy sclerotic skull patches
Osteoporosis circumscripta Focal lytic skull lesion, lytic phase
Brim sign Sclerotic iliopectineal line
Blade of grass V-shaped diaphyseal lucency
Banana fracture Transverse pathological fracture of bowed bone
Skull sign (TO) Disease
Salt and pepper Hyperparathyroidism
Bevelled edge, double contour Eosinophilic granuloma (LCH)
Osteoporosis circumscripta · cotton wool Paget (lytic · sclerotic)
Rain-drop skull (punched-out) Myeloma
Spine sign (TO) Disease
Picture frame · ivory Paget
Rugger jersey Hyperparathyroidism / renal osteodystrophy · osteopetrosis
Cod-fish vertebra Osteoporosis (biconcave)

Bone scanmarks disease sites accurately · intensely hot in lytic + mixed, less in sclerotic · pagetoid bone hot even without sarcoma

MRIthick trabeculae with normal marrow signal · lumbar stenosis

CTcortical thickening, coarse trabeculae

Sarcomacortical destruction + soft-tissue mass

Histology

Mosaic patternwoven + lamellar bone, broad irregular trabeculae, disorganized cement lines · large osteoclasts, many nuclei (up to 100), virus-like inclusion bodies · fibrovascular tissue between trabeculae · biopsy rarely needed (TO)

Differential

Benign, older (40–80) Multiple lesions, older Observation only Bisphosphonate helps
Paget's disease ✓ ✓ ✓ ✓
Enchondroma (older adult) ✓ — ✓ —
Bone island ✓ — — —
Bone infarct ✓ ✓ — —
Hyperparathyroidism (brown tumour) ✓ ✓ — —
Metastatic bone disease · myeloma — ✓ — ✓
Lymphoma of bone — ✓ — —
NOF · osteochondroma · EG — — ✓ —
FD — — — ✓

Distractorsosteoporosis (compression fractures, neck/IT) · renal osteomalacia (erosions, amyloid) · lymphoma (blastic but no enlargement/coarse trabeculae) · melorheostosis (periosteal new bone) · infarct

5Management

Paget's disease
  • Asymptomatic→OBSERVATION + SUPPORTIVE (physio, orthoses, NSAIDs, analgesics)
    (treat if active disease at sites where complications likely)
  • Symptomatic → BISPHOSPHONATES (1st line)→CALCITONIN (2nd line)
  • Hip/knee OA→THA / TKA after medical pre-treatment
  • Bowing with fracture / impending fracture / failed brace→METAPHYSEAL OSTEOTOMY + PLATE

Medical treatment — bisphosphonates

Indicationsbone pain · pre-op (lower ALP to 50% of pretreatment → less bleeding) · hearing loss, spinal stenosis, HOHF · prevention of fracture/deformity

Mechanismosteoclast inhibition
✗ Wrong options: TNF-α · IL-1R · ER blockade · COX

Drug Notes
Alendronate, risedronate Oral
Etidronate Old, inhibits osteoclasts and osteoblasts · ≤6 months at a time
Pamidronate, zoledronic acid IV, osteoclasts only · single 5 mg IV zoledronic acid — most effective, normalizes ALP up to 6.5 yr
Calcitonin 2nd line (bisphosphonate intolerance) · osteoclasts shrink within minutes · SC/IM
Calcium + vitamin D Prevent hypocalcaemia on bisphosphonate/calcitonin · not as treatment
Teriparatide Contraindicated — secondary OS risk

Teriparatide contraindicationsPaget · hypercalcaemic disorders · OS · bone mets · pregnancy · skeletal radiation

NSAIDspain relief, no change in course
✗ Wrong options: methotrexate · monoclonal antibody · chemo · radiation · wide resection

Benign biopsy, lytic sacral lesion in old melanoma patientPaget → bisphosphonate

Arthroplasty & osteotomy in Paget disease

Procedure Commonest complication
THA Bleeding (rich blood supply) · loosening ~15%
TKA Malalignment

Before arthroplastypharmacological pre-treatment to cut bleeding · activity by ALP + urine hydroxyproline

Bowing + persistent pain after 6 mo bracecorrective osteotomy + plate fixation
✗ Wrong options: IMN without osteotomy · amputation · knee arthrodesis

6Complications

Paget sarcoma

Incidence<1% · 5–10% in advanced polyostotic disease (TO)

Typeosteosarcoma > fibrosarcoma, chondrosarcoma (spindle-cell sarcomas) · pleomorphic spindle cells, giant cells, atypia

Patientolder (55–80, 7th decade) · polyostotic · pelvis, femur, humerus, proximal skeleton

Treatmentchemotherapy + wide resection + reconstruction · palliative RT if too infirm
✗ Wrong options: surgery alone

Prognosisworst of bone sarcomas · metastatic 5-yr survival <10% (Orthobullets/OB) · <5% (OB2004) · non-metastatic >50% · secondary OS in >60 yr 7.5% vs primary 37% (Huvos)

Premalignant for secondary OS (TO)FD · Paget · irradiation · multiple osteochondromatosis · bone infarct

Othersfractures · OA · HOHF · deafness · stenosis

How it's asked

✗ Wrong options: VDRR · fluorosis · MED

✗ Wrong options: osteoporosis · renal osteomalacia

✗ Wrong options: infarct · myeloma · melorheostosis

✗ Wrong options: osteopetrosis · OI

✗ Wrong options: prophylactic fixation · RT

"Bowed tibia, brace failed?"corrective osteotomy + plate

"Paget THA — commonest complication?"increased blood loss
✗ Wrong options: malalignment (TKA)

"Paget TKA — commonest complication?"malalignment

Parosteal Osteosarcoma

Pathology · 51 / 66

1Basics

Naturelow-grade, well-differentiated surface osteosarcoma · <5% (OB7521) / ~5% (OB8282) of osteosarcomas

Demographicsfemales > males · 30–40 yr (3rd–4th decades) · range in stems 20–42

Sitesurface of metaphysis · posterior distal femur > proximal tibia > proximal humerus · 80% femur (Orthobullets) · posterior distal femur ~75% (OB7488) / 80% (OB4407)

Marrow invasion25% · does not affect long-term survival

Genes & lab markers

MDM2 amplification + ring chromosome — high rate

3Presentation

Painless mass · slow-growing, firm, mild/no pain · long history (>3 yr)

↓ Knee flexion / tightnesslarge posterior distal femoral lesion · tenderness on deep posterior palpation · effusion

LabsESR, CBC normal

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayheavily ossified, lobulated mass arising from cortex, "stuck-on" the metaphyseal surface over a broad area · cloud-like ossification · may be multifocal

MRImandatory, whole bone · soft-tissue/marrow involvement, skip lesions · very dark signal (heavy mineralization)

CT chestmandatory staging (lung mets)

Bone scanmandatory · always hot

Angiographyfinds hypervascular dedifferentiated areas → target biopsy

Next stepbiopsy after staging
✗ Wrong options: arthroscopy · bisphosphonate · chemo/RT first

Histology

Low graderegularly arranged normal osseous trabeculae · atypical spindle cells between trabeculae = most prominent feature · bland fibroblastic stroma · some atypia needed for diagnosis

Pitfallmistaken for fibrous dysplasia histologically (X-ray differs)

Differential

Bone surface lesion Similar histology Surgery alone
Parosteal OS ✓ — ✓
Periosteal OS ✓ — —
Periosteal chondroma ✓ — ✓
Osteochondroma / MHE ✓ — ✓
Developmental defect (adductor magnus insertion) ✓ — —
Myositis ossificans ✓ — —
Chondrosarcoma · adamantinoma — — ✓
Chordoma — — ✓ (controversial)
FD — ✓ —
Lesion Key distinction
Osteochondroma Medullary continuity with host bone on cross-section
Myositis ossificans Thin egg-shell peripheral calcification, often not touching bone
Surface chondrosarcoma More cortical destruction, rings and arcs
Dedifferentiated parosteal Hypervascular areas on angiogram

5Management

Parosteal osteosarcoma
  • Low grade→WIDE RESECTION ALONE
    (geometric osteotomy to keep native joint; no chemo or RT)
  • Dedifferentiated (high-grade component)→NEOADJUVANT CHEMO + WIDE RESECTION

Treatment — wide resection alone

Only OS subtype treated with wide resection alone
✗ Wrong options: conventional · periosteal · telangiectatic · secondary (all chemo + wide + chemo)

Low-grade intramedullary (central) OSalso surgery with wide margins only · "lacey" osteoid, minimal atypia, long premorbid history

✗ Wrong optionscurettage (high recurrence) · marginal resection · observation

Conventional OS for contrastpre-op chemo 8–12 weeks, maintenance 6–12 months after surgery

6Complications

Prognosis95% long-term survival with local control · better than other OS

Dedifferentiationpoor prognostic factor → chemo

How it's asked

"21-yr-old, dense bone 'stuck-on' proximal humerus, mature bone in fibroblastic stroma?"parosteal OS
✗ Wrong options: periosteal OS · osteochondroma · MO

"33-yr-old knee, surface lesion, hypervascular on angiogram, high-grade areas?"dedifferentiated parosteal OS — neoadjuvant chemo + wide resection

"34-yr-old woman, ↓ knee flexion, lobulated posterior distal femoral surface mineralization?"parosteal OS
✗ Wrong options: chondrosarcoma (rings and arcs) · lymphoma

"Field hockey player, ↓ knee flexion, posterior femoral surface lesion — next?"surgical biopsy
✗ Wrong options: chemo · RT · arthroscopy

"Low-grade intramedullary OS, no mets — treatment?"surgery only

"Parosteal OS (biopsy) — definitive management?"wide surgical resection
✗ Wrong options: neoadjuvant chemo (conventional OS)

"24-yr-old, tight knee, surface OS — plan?"surgical resection only
✗ Wrong options: chemo · RT · observation

"42-yr-old slowly enlarging thigh mass, surface OS?"wide resection
✗ Wrong options: curettage (recurrence)

"Which OS subtype gets wide resection alone?"parosteal

Periosteal Chondromas

Pathology · 52 / 66

1Basics

Naturerare, benign chondrogenic surface tumour (under periosteum)

Age10–20 yr

Siteproximal humerus (59%) · distal femur, proximal femur · hand (metacarpals, phalanges)

3Presentation

Painmany painful from irritation of overlying tendons · night pain possible · palpable, mildly tender mass

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raywell-demarcated, shallow cortical defect · saucerization of underlying cortex · punctate mineralization in 1/3 · cortically based, eroding cortex
✗ Wrong options: medullary epicentre (enchondroma)

X-ray is the keyhistology can look like chondrosarcoma

Histology

Like enchondroma, but more cellular with more malignant-looking cells · bland hyaline cartilage · small chondroid cells in lacunae

Differential

Surface lesion Similar chondroid histology Marginal excision
Periosteal chondroma ✓ ✓ ✓
Osteochondroma / MHE ✓ ✓ —
Parosteal OS · periosteal OS ✓ — —
Enchondroma / Ollier / Maffucci — ✓ —
Chondrosarcoma — ✓ —
Neurilemmoma · nodular fasciitis (soft tissue) — — ✓
Epidermal inclusion cyst · glomus tumour — — ✓
Lesion Key distinction
Enchondroma Same histology, central medullary epicentre
Surface chondrosarcoma Malignant histology
CMF Fibrous + myxoid elements
Chondroblastoma Polyhedral cells, chicken-wire calcification
Periosteal OS / CS Similar location — malignant

5Management

Periosteal chondroma
  • Mild symptoms→OBSERVATION
  • Severe / progressive pain, function impaired→MARGINAL EXCISION INCLUDING UNDERLYING CORTEX
    • Large defect→BONE GRAFT

Recurs if cartilage left behind
✗ Wrong options: wide excision · RT · chemo

How it's asked

"19-yr-old shoulder night pain, surface cartilage lesion on CT + biopsy — treatment?"marginal excision including underlying cortex
✗ Wrong options: wide excision · chemo · RT 60 Gy

"10-yr-old wrist, cortical saucer-shaped defect, benign cartilage?"periosteal chondroma
✗ Wrong options: enchondroma (medullary) · surface CS · CMF · chondroblastoma

Periosteal Osteosarcoma

Pathology · 53 / 66

1Basics

Natureextremely rare intermediate-grade surface osteosarcoma

Demographics15–25 yr · F > M

Sitediaphysis of long bones · femur, tibia

Genetics (p53, RB, Li-Fraumeni)

p53 germ-line mutation15–20% of periosteal OS

Li-FraumeniAD, germ-line p53 inactivation · breast, colon cancers, sarcomas — 50% are osteosarcoma → OS = commonest bone tumour with p53
✗ Wrong options: chondrosarcoma (p53 = aggressive behaviour) · Ewing (EWSR1–ETS) · myeloma

Sporadic OSp53 mutation in 15–30%

Retinoblastoma (RB1)hereditary (bilateral) retinoblastoma → OS · RB1 linkage analysis identifies at-risk relatives

Genes & lab markers

Finding Tumour
RB + p53 alterations Conventional high-grade OS
Supernumerary ring chromosomes Parosteal OS
t(11;22) Ewing
t(X;18) Synovial sarcoma
Telomeric associations GCT of bone

3Presentation

Paincommonest symptom · pathologic fracture 25%

Examregional swelling, tenderness · may present after a minor twist

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray"sunburst" / "hair-on-end" periosteal reaction, often in a saucerized cortical depression · no medullary involvement typically

StagingCT chest (lung mets) + bone scan (very hot) required

Workup — MRI of entire bone

Bone-forming lesion, indistinct margins → MRI of the entire bone ASAP before biopsy · biopsy by the treating surgeon
✗ Wrong options: biopsy first · amputation · observation · NSAIDs

Histology

Grossly lobular, cartilaginous · osteoid + chondroblastic matrix · no osteoid → chondrosarcoma

Surface OS compared

Parosteal OS Periosteal OS
Grade Low Intermediate
Age · sex 30–40 · F 15–25 · F
Site Posterior distal femoral metaphysis Femur/tibia diaphysis
X-ray Stuck-on, lobulated, ossified Sunburst in saucerized cortex
Histology Spindle cells between regular trabeculae Chondroblastic + osteoid
Genes MDM2, ring chromosome p53 (15–20%)
Treatment Wide resection alone Chemo + wide resection + chemo

Other distractorsosteochondroma (medullary continuity, not aggressive) · dedifferentiated CS (from prior cartilage tumour) · synovial sarcoma (soft tissue, spindle cells, may calcify)

Conventional OS distractors (same module)

Lesion Histology
Osteosarcoma Osteoid from pleomorphic / malignant spindle cells · Codman triangle, soft-tissue mass
Ewing Uniform small blue round cells (reactive osteoid not prominent)
Osteomyelitis Inflammatory cells, dead bone, no mass
EG Histiocytes + eosinophils, rarely mass
MFH of bone Lytic, storiform, no osteoid
Osteoblastoma Benign stromal cells
Stress fracture T1 MRI fracture line, no mass

5Management

Treatment — chemotherapy & resection

Same as intramedullary OSneoadjuvant multi-agent chemo (8–12 wk) → wide resection (limb salvage) → maintenance chemo (6–12 mo)

Limb salvageallograft / megaprosthesis · rotationplasty · amputation if needed

✗ Wrong optionsradiation (OS is not radiosensitive) · chemo alone · surgery alone · resection before chemo

6Complications

Pulmonary mets20–35%

Prognosisintermediate between parosteal and intramedullary OS

Good sign90–98% necrosis after neoadjuvant chemo

Bad signMDR gene (pumps chemo out) · in 25% of primaries, 50% of mets

How it's asked

"47-yr-old knee pain/swelling, surface lesion, osteoid + cartilage?"periosteal OS
✗ Wrong options: chondrosarcoma · Ewing · GCT · FD

"24-yr-old softball twist, sunburst surface ossification?"periosteal OS
✗ Wrong options: parosteal (stuck-on) · osteochondroma · dedifferentiated CS · synovial sarcoma

"16-yr-old distal femoral bone-forming lesion, pleomorphic osteoid?"osteosarcoma
✗ Wrong options: Ewing · rhabdomyosarcoma · MFH

"10-yr-old distal femur, destructive bone-forming, malignant spindle cells?"osteosarcoma
✗ Wrong options: osteoblastoma · Ewing

"10-yr-old distal tibia, Codman triangle, soft-tissue mass?"osteosarcoma
✗ Wrong options: EG · osteomyelitis · trauma

"Li-Fraumeni (p53) — commonest primary bone tumour?"osteosarcoma

"9-yr-old, retinoblastoma, mother bilateral RB, humeral OS — treatment?"chemotherapy + wide resection
✗ Wrong options: RT

"16-yr-old conventional OS — genetic feature?"RB + p53 alterations
✗ Wrong options: ring chromosomes · t(11;22) · t(X;18) · telomeric

"13-yr-old high-grade OS — treatment?"neoadjuvant chemo → resection + reconstruction
✗ Wrong options: resection then chemo · RT

"10-yr-old proximal tibial OS?"surgery + chemotherapy

"10-yr-old humeral OS?"chemo + limb-sparing surgery with reconstruction
✗ Wrong options: amputation alone · RT

"15-yr-old, indistinct bone-forming knee lesion — next?"MRI of the entire bone
✗ Wrong options: biopsy first

Pigmented Villonodular Synovitis

Pathology · 54 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Namenow tenosynovial giant cell tumour (TGCT) · WHO 2013: diffuse TGCT = PVNS · localized TGCT = GCT of tendon sheath (commonest in fingers) (TO)

Naturelocally aggressive neoplastic synovial disease ("not a true neoplasm" — Orthobullets) · neoplastic: aneuploidy, trisomy 7, ~20 malignant/metastatic cases (TO) · "non-neoplastic inflammatory" (OB7584) · rarely metastasizes

Incidence9.2 per million/yr (Orthobullets) · 1.8 per million/yr, 1 in 250 arthroscopies (TO)

Demographics30–40 yr (any age; stems 14–58) · M = F (Orthobullets) · slight F predominance (OB9003)

Sitemonoarticular · knee > hip > ankle > shoulder > elbow · localized: anterior knee (80%), patellofemoral, infrapatellar fat pad

History of trauma50%

CSF-1 pathogenesis & pexidartinib

Driveroverexpression of CSF1 → CSF1R-expressing cells cluster → synovial hyperplasia · chromosome 1p13 · 5q33 rearrangement

Gene distractor Lesion
CSF1 ↑ PVNS / TGCT
USP6 ABC, nodular fasciitis
MDM2 Parosteal / low-grade OS, ALT
APC Desmoid (FAP)
c-myc Aggressive GCT of bone
EXT MHE
RB OS (up to 35%)
p53 Li-Fraumeni sarcomas, breast
FGFR3 · RUNX2 Achondroplasia · cleidocranial dysplasia
Ki-67 · NF1 · NTRK Ewing · neurofibromatosis · various solid tumours

Localized vs diffuse

Localized Diffuse
Location Knee > hip > ankle Knee (75%)
Age 30–50 <40
Sex M = F F ≥ M
Presentation Painless, swollen, long-standing · locking/catching (meniscal mimic) (TO) Painful, swollen, tender, ↓ motion
X-ray Erosion from local pressure Degenerative change both sides of joint
MRI Well-circumscribed mass Ill-defined mass
Recurrence after synovectomy 8% 30%
Treatment Nodule excision + partial synovectomy Total synovectomy (anterior arthroscopic + posterior open)

3Presentation

Hallmarkrecurrent atraumatic haemarthrosis · insidious pain, stiffness, swelling · boggy effusion, warmth, erythema, joint-line tenderness, ↓ ROM

Painoften not the main complaint (TO) · vague for years

LabsESR, CRP normal

Aspirategrossly bloody / brown · e.g. >50,000 RBC, 1,100 WBC

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral)soft-tissue swelling · cystic erosions with sclerotic margins on both sides of the joint · knee often shows advanced OA; erosions/cysts commoner in hip + ankle (tight joints) (TO) · no mineralization
✗ Wrong options: synovial chondromatosis

CTextent of cystic bone loss

MRI (most sensitive)low signal on T1 and T2 (haemosiderin) · blooming artefact on gradient echo (iron) · fat signal on T1 · effusion, synovial expansion, erosion · posterior extra-articular extension · modest enhancement · brighter foci = less haemosiderin (TO) · "somewhat bright on T2" (OB7420)

Arthroscopy (gold standard + biopsy)brownish/reddish inflamed synovium, frond-like papillary projections

Histology

Haemosiderin-stained multinucleated giant cells · mononuclear stromal cells · vascular villi lined by plump hyperplastic synoviocytes · pigmented foam cells (lipid-laden histiocytes) · mitoses common (few in OB stems) · gross: yellow-brown mass

Histology distractor Lesion
Biphasic spindle + epithelial Synovial sarcoma
Lipoblasts, myxoid, signet-ring Myxoid liposarcoma
Antoni A/B Neurilemmoma
Plasma cells, lymphoid follicles RA
Caseating granulomas TB
Mature chondrocyte nodules Synovial chondromatosis
Osteoclast-like cells + stroma + osteoid OS / GCT of bone

Differential

Lesion Key distinction
Synovial chondromatosis Nodules low T1, high T2, mineralized loose bodies · no haemorrhagic effusion
Synovial sarcoma Periarticular, not intra-articular · T2 bright · spindle cells
Haemophilia Recurrent effusions, but MRI not nodular mass
RA Pannus, uniform signal · no haemorrhage
Gout Punched-out erosions, overhanging edges, tophi
Tumoral calcinosis Extracapsular periarticular calcification
GCT of bone Eccentric epiphyseal/metaphyseal lesion in bone
Baker's cyst Fluid outpouching, semimembranosus–medial gastrocnemius
ABC · chondroblastoma Intra-osseous (PVNS is extra-osseous)

Behaviour descriptorlocally aggressive
✗ Wrong options: malignant · inflammatory · normal variant

5Management

PVNS / TGCT
  • Asymptomatic→OBSERVATION
  • Localized→NODULE EXCISION + PARTIAL SYNOVECTOMY (arthroscopic if anterior)
  • Diffuse→TOTAL SYNOVECTOMY (arthroscopic anterior + open posterior) ± EBRT
  • End-stage joint → SYNOVECTOMY + ARTHROPLASTY (ankle→ARTHRODESIS)
  • Recurrent / unresectable, surgery morbid→PEXIDARTINIB

Surgical treatment & recurrence

Mainstaysynovectomy (open or arthroscopic) · arthroscopic → less morbidity, better ROM · open → pain, stiffness (TO)

Marginsynovectomy = intralesional (OB417 — only lesion where intralesional is standard) · = marginal excision (Orthobullets, OB7568)

Localizedresection of nodule only · arthroscopic anterior excision + partial synovectomy
✗ Wrong options: complete synovectomy · RT · FNA (excisional biopsy)

Diffuse kneelowest recurrence: open anterior + posterior synovectomy (morbid) · arthroscopic anterior + open posterior (between gastrocnemius heads — popliteal NV bundle at risk) · posteromedial portal for posterior compartment (TO)

Foot/anklemarginal excision · radioisotopes poor (multi-joint spread, skin necrosis if extravasated)
✗ Wrong options: amputation · wide excision

EBRT30–35 Gy / 15 fractions or 50 Gy / 25 · with total synovectomy → recurrence 10–20% · for recurrent/destructive disease · not RT alone

Intra-articular Y-90 / radiocolloidcombined with surgery historically (TO) · now removed/abandoned — haematomyelopoietic disease, high complications (OB)

Not usedchemotherapy (TO) · intra-articular steroids · plica excision · TKA in young without end-stage cartilage

Pexidartinib

Mechanismoral CSF-1R antagonist (TKI) · also KIT + FLT3-ITD · first and only FDA-approved systemic therapy (2019) (TO)
✗ Wrong options: imatinib · emactuzumab · nilotinib (used, not approved)

Indicationdiffuse TGCT not amenable to resection / severe morbidity from surgery / recurrent recalcitrant
✗ Wrong options: synovial chondromatosis · GCT of bone · synovial sarcoma (TO)

Efficacyonce daily 24 wk → ~40% improve (ENLIVEN) · partial responses → surgical downstaging

Side effectscholestatic hepatotoxicity (REMS, LFT monitoring) · hair depigmentation · dose taper
✗ Wrong options: alopecia

6Complications

Recurrencemost common complication · 30–50% despite complete synovectomy (Orthobullets) · ~50% (OB219276) · 8% localized / 30% diffuse

Joint destructionearly OA → arthroplasty (↑ stiffness, infection) · arthrodesis or amputation

Radiationskin necrosis, radiation-induced sarcoma

Uncommon: arthrofibrosis, chondrolysis, metastasis

✗ Wrong options: GCT of bone · Baker's cyst

"Indication for pexidartinib?"PVNS (TO)

✗ Wrong options: USP6 · MDM2

"Knee synovectomy planned — mutated gene?"CSF-1
✗ Wrong options: APC · c-myc · EXT · RB

✗ Wrong options: Y-90 · steroid · TKA

Plantar Fibromatosis (Ledderhose Disease)

Pathology · 55 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Naturebenign myofibroblast + collagen proliferation of the plantar fascia · commonest soft-tissue neoplasm of the foot

Demographicsmales · middle-aged / elderly (any age) · Caucasian

Bilateral25–50%

Risk factorsmale · Caucasian · epilepsy · repeated trauma · diabetes · long-term alcohol · chronic liver disease · other fibrosing conditions

AssociationsDupuytren's (plantar fibromatosis in 25% of Dupuytren's) · Peyronie's

Geneticsbasis unclear (unlike Dupuytren's)

Anatomy

Plantar fascia3 bands (central, medial, lateral) · medial band most affected · origin medial + anterior calcaneus → 5 digital slips at MTPJs → base of proximal phalanges

Phases

Phase Feature
Proliferative ↑ Fibroblast activity, cell proliferation
Involutional / active Nodule formation
Residual / resting ↓ Fibroblast activity, collagen maturation, scar/contracture

3Presentation

Subcutaneous thickening / nodules, medial plantar foot · usually asymptomatic, may become painful · digital contractures rare

Examlook for Dupuytren's, Peyronie's · ankle/hindfoot motion · Achilles/gastrocnemius contracture

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral)usually normal

MRI (study of choice)nodular thickening on medial plantar aponeurosis · low T1 (acellular, high collagen) · low/medium T2 (high-signal areas if aggressive)

USless reflective fibroma within bright fascia · comb sign — alternating hypo- and iso-echoic linear bands

Histology

Dense fibrocellular tissuemature collagen, fibrocytes at varying maturity · no atypia, no abnormal mitoses · gross: lobulated firm irregular mass

Differentialpost-traumatic neuroma · fibrosarcoma · clear-cell sarcoma

5Management

Plantar fibromatosis
  • Small / minimally painful→OBSERVATION + SUPPORTIVE
    (NSAIDs, orthotics, PT, steroid injection, radiotherapy)
  • Large, painful, activity-limiting→TOTAL FASCIECTOMY ± RADIATION

Treatment & recurrence

Excision Recurrence
Local excision 57–100%
Wide excision (2–3 cm margins) 8–80%
Total fasciectomy (preferred) 0–50%

Techniqueavoid incisions directly over nodules · partial fasciectomy → excise nodule + overlying skin (± skin graft)

Surgical complicationsskin necrosis · nerve entrapment (medial plantar nerve) · loss of arch height / windlass · wound breakdown

Radiotherapybest evidence: randomized double-blind sham-controlled trial (2023), durable efficacy at ≥1 yr · post-op RT for high-grade lesions to cut recurrence

Evidence gradescollagenase: insufficient (case reports) · orthotics: expert opinion (Level V) · surgery: no Level I superiority data

6Complications

Recurrence60% of excised lesions · recurs more aggressive · risk: bilateral, multiple nodules, family history · prevent with adjuvant RT

Prognosismalignant transformation rare

How it's asked

"Plantar fascial lesion — excisional biopsy's likely complication?"recurrence
✗ Wrong options: metastasis · wound breakdown, nerve injury, windlass loss = wide excision problems

"Bilateral plantar nodules, failed orthotics/NSAIDs — highest-grade evidence?"radiotherapy (RCT, double-blind sham-controlled)
✗ Wrong options: collagenase RCTs · surgery RCTs

Pleomorphic Sarcoma of Bone (Malignant Fibrous Histiocytoma)

Pathology · 56 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence<2% of primary malignant bone tumours · 2nd commonest bone sarcoma in adults after chondrosarcoma

Demographics20–80 yr (peak 2nd–4th decades) · M:F 3:2 · Caucasian

Site75% appendicular · metaphysis of long bones: distal femur, proximal tibia, proximal humerus

Secondary (25%)from bone infarct, Paget's, prior radiation

Genes & lab markers

p53 mutations — secondary MFH of bone

Labs↑ WBC · eosinophilia · abnormal LFTs · hypoglycaemia

3Presentation

New, painful mass · pathologic fracture · constitutional (fever, chills, night sweats, weight loss) · swelling, limp, ↓ ROM

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral)purely lytic, destructive metaphyseal lesion · no matrix mineralization · variable periosteal reaction · cortical destruction + soft-tissue extension · non-specific

MRIdeep heterogeneous mass · internal haemorrhage · T1 low, T2 high

CTchest CT staging (lung mets) · bone destruction

Bone scanstaging · ↑ uptake in adjacent cortex

Histology

Pleomorphic spindle + histiocytic cells in storiform pattern · malignant multinucleated giant cells with grooved/indented nuclei · chronic inflammatory cells · variable collagen · fascicles radiating from hypocellular foci · haemorrhage/necrosis → high grade · no osteoid (vs OS)

Differential

Malignant destructive, older Sacral, older Wide resection + chemo

Chondrosarcoma (conventional) ✓ ✓ —
Dedifferentiated chondrosarcoma ✓ ✓ ✓

Lymphoma of bone ✓ ✓ —
Secondary sarcoma ✓ ✓ ✓

Chordoma (sacral) — ✓ —

Enneking staging (OB)

Stage Meaning
I Low grade
II High grade
III Distant metastasis
A / B Intracompartmental / extracompartmental

Stage IIB spindle-cell sarcoma → pre-op chemo + wide excision + chemo

5Management

Diagnosis & treatment

Treat like osteosarcomaneoadjuvant chemo (8–12 wk) → wide resection → adjuvant chemo (6–12 mo) ± RT
✗ Wrong options: wide resection alone · chemo alone · RT alone · observation

Marginwide excision or amputation → better 5-yr survival than intralesional/marginal

RTincomplete/questionable margins · adjunct

Soft-tissue UPS for contrastradiation + wide resection (painless, slow, T1 dark/T2 bright)

Reconstruction after resection

Limb salvageendoprosthesis · resection arthrodesis · allograft · rotationplasty

Proximal tibia Megaprosthesis (MP) Allograft-prosthetic composite (APC)
10-yr implant survival 79% 94%
Failure rate No significant difference No significant difference
Function Similar Similar · better extensor mechanism in high-demand patient
Leg length Both restore Both restore

6Complications

Survival50–60% at 5 yr

Lung metsup to 30%

Prognosissecondary worse than primary · >90% necrosis after chemo → better · age <40 → better DFS

X vs Y

Distractor (soft-tissue thigh mass) Key feature
UPS Painless, slow, deep, T1 dark / T2 bright
Liposarcoma Lipoblasts, signet-ring cells
Desmoid Upper torso, dark T1 + T2, fibroblasts + collagen
Synovial sarcoma (periarticular) Biphasic, t(X;18), SYT-SSX

PET↓ glucose uptake = early response predictor (OS, Ewing, GIST)

How it's asked

"75-yr-old, slow painless 7 cm thigh mass, T1 dark/T2 bright, pleomorphic?"undifferentiated pleomorphic sarcoma
✗ Wrong options: liposarcoma · desmoid · synovial sarcoma · schwannoma

"60-yr-old, spindle-cell sarcoma of bone — optimal treatment?"neoadjuvant chemo + wide resection + adjuvant chemo
✗ Wrong options: wide resection alone

"12-yr-old, purely lytic distal femoral metaphyseal lesion, storiform malignant spindle cells?"chemotherapy + surgery (MFH of bone)

"Proximal tibial resection — APC vs megaprosthesis?"APC better extensor mechanism in high-demand patient
✗ Wrong options: MP longer survival · APC higher failure

Radiation Therapy

Pathology · 57 / 66

1Basics

Mechanismsfree radical production · direct genetic (DNA) damage

Radiosensitive tumours

Use Tumours
Definitive control (primary bone) Ewing / PNET · primary lymphoma of bone · haemangioendothelioma · solitary plasmacytoma
First-line RT alone (spinal mets — OB) Leukaemia, lymphoma, myeloma/plasmacytoma, haemangioendothelioma, Ewing
Adjuvant to surgery Soft-tissue sarcomas (pre- or post-op)
Palliation / after fixation Metastatic bone disease — after fixing impending/pathologic fractures
Metastasis sensitivity
Prostate Very radiosensitive
Breast 70% sensitive, 30% resistant
GI, renal Not radiosensitive

Radioresistant (OB)fibrosarcoma, chondrosarcoma, melanoma, angiosarcoma · osteosarcoma (not radiosensitive — OS chapters)

5Management

Technique

1 rad = 1 cGy · typical 180–200 cGy/day in fractions · RT is cumulative (total = sum of fractions)

Total dose Tissue healing
<45 Gy Usually uncomplicated
45–55 Gy Heals, with problems
>60 Gy Unlikely to heal

Preoperative vs postoperative radiation

Pre-op (neoadjuvant) Post-op (adjuvant)
Wound complications More (up to 40%) Less (<20%)
Oedema, fibrosis, stiffness Less (<15%) More (>20%, often temporary)
Lymphoedema · fracture Lower Higher
Field · dose Smaller field, lower dose Larger field, higher dose
Pseudocapsule Forms → close-margin resection No
Tumour shrinkage Yes No
Local recurrence Lower (Orthobullets) · no difference (OB6336) Higher (Orthobullets)
Overall survival No difference by timing No difference

6Complications

Radiation complications (fracture, secondary sarcoma)

Earlydelayed wound healing, infection, desquamation

Latefibrosis, joint stiffness, secondary sarcoma, fractures

Post-radiation fracture~25% after STS resection + EBRT

Fracture risk factor Yes / No
Dose ≥59 Gy Yes
Weight-bearing bone, esp. femur Yes
Female Yes
Volume of bone irradiated Yes
Anterior femoral compartment resection Yes
Age Yes
Periosteal stripping (→ consider prophylactic fixation) Yes
Osteoporosis Yes
Method of RT administration No

Post-radiation sarcomasarcoma in a previously irradiated field · ~13% · ↑ with prior chemo · very poor prognosis

Criteria≥5 yr after RT (range 3–50 yr) · in the RT field · different histology from the original disease

How it's asked

"Pre-op vs post-op RT — higher with pre-op?"wound necrosis
✗ Wrong options: fibrosis · lymphoedema (post-op) · local recurrence (no difference)

"Synovial sarcoma (SS18-SSX), pre-op RT (A) vs post-op RT (B)?"A: more wound complications, less fibrosis

"NOT a risk factor for post-RT fracture?"method of RT administration (✓ female, ✓ age, ✓ dose, ✓ periosteal resection)

"47-yr-old, clavicle lytic lesion, mantle RT 22 yr ago, atypia + bone formation?"radiation-associated sarcoma
✗ Wrong options: osteonecrosis · osteomyelitis

"L4–5 lytic metastases — RT first-line for?"lymphoma
✗ Wrong options: fibrosarcoma · chondrosarcoma · melanoma · angiosarcoma

Rhabdomyosarcoma

Pathology · 58 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology & subtypes

Naturemalignant tumour of primitive mesenchyme (mesoderm, skeletal-muscle precursors)

Commonest soft-tissue sarcoma in children
✗ Wrong options: foot (synovial sarcoma) · calcifies (synovial sarcoma) · Maffucci (haemangiomas) · from myxomas

Sitehead/neck, genitourinary, retroperitoneum · GU > extremities (TO, OO chapter)

Subtype Age Histology 5-yr survival
Embryonal Infants, young children Small round blue cells, ± cross-striations 80%
Alveolar Adolescents, young adults (15–40) Poorly differentiated round cells + multinucleated giant cells, aggregates in dense fibrous septae 60%
Botryoid ("bunch of grapes") Infants, young children · vagina — <30% ("uniformly fatal")
Pleomorphic 40–70 yr Multiple cell types, like other pleomorphic sarcomas 25%

Genetics — t(2;13) PAX3-FKHR

Alveolart(2;13) → PAX3-FKHR · t(1;13) → PAX7-FKHR (as named in OB8296; paired the other way round in some sources) · high metastatic risk

MRInon-diagnostic but needed for planning · dark T1, bright T2 (like other STS)

Nodessentinel node biopsy (esp. if nodes enlarged)

Lymph-node-metastasizing sarcomas

5Management

Rhabdomyosarcoma
  • Paediatric→WIDE EXCISION + CHEMO (+ RT if no radical margin)
    (vincristine, dactinomycin, cyclophosphamide)
  • Adult pleomorphic→WIDE EXCISION + RT (chemo not effective in adults)
  • Unresectable / close or positive margins→RT
  • Widespread metastases (selected)→CHEMO ALONE

Teenager, isolated limb RMSwide resection + chemo + RT · chemo has a substantial survival benefit · amputation rarely (only if better function)
✗ Wrong options: observation · marginal excision

How it's asked

"True of rhabdomyosarcoma?"commonest soft-tissue sarcoma in a child

Soft Tissue Sarcoma

Pathology · 59 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence12,000/yr in US · 4,700 deaths/yr · M > F · 85% >15 yr

Site60% extremities · 44% thigh

Originmesoderm (connective tissue, muscle, fat, nerve, vessels) · grow centripetally with a pseudocapsule, compress neighbours

Types>50 histologic types (~100 — OB) · same presentation, imaging, differential and treatment · commonest: UPS/MFH, liposarcoma, leiomyosarcoma, synovial sarcoma

Commonest by group (TO)adults: MFH > liposarcoma · young adults: synovial · children: rhabdomyosarcoma · hand/UL: epithelioid · foot: synovial

Genes & lab markers

Translocation / association Sarcoma
t(X;18), SYT-SSX Synovial sarcoma
t(2;13) Alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma
t(12;16)(q13;p11) Myxoid liposarcoma
NF1 MPNST
Stewart-Treves / chronic lymphoedema Angiosarcoma
S-100 + HMB45 Clear-cell sarcoma (melanoma of soft parts)

Sarcomas with lymph node spread

"RACES"Rhabdomyosarcoma · Angiosarcoma · Clear-cell sarcoma · Epithelioid sarcoma · Synovial sarcoma → consider sentinel node biopsy

Overallonly 5% of STS go to nodes
✗ Wrong options: liposarcoma · desmoid · GCT · Ewing · OS · chondrosarcoma · MFH · MPNST

Leiomyosarcomacan spread via lymphatics but mainly haematogenous → lungs

3Presentation

Slow-growing, painless mass · trauma draws attention · rapid growth → higher grade

Red flags>5 cm, deep to fascia, immobile, rapid growth, pain in a previously painless mass, recurrent swelling

Mimicslipoma, haematoma (no trauma, no ecchymosis → suspect sarcoma; mean 7-month delay when misdiagnosed), desmoid, nerve sheath tumours, abscess, biceps tear

"Sarcoma until proven otherwise"large (>5 cm), deep, heterogeneous mass

4Diagnosis & Classification

Workup of soft tissue mass (MRI, biopsy)

Soft-tissue mass
  • X-rays (2 planes)→bone involvement, mineralization (synovial sarcoma)
  • Contrast MRI → diagnostic & benign & symptomatic→EXCISE WITHOUT BIOPSY
    (lipoma, ganglion, schwannoma, intramuscular myxoma)
  • MRI indeterminate / sarcoma-like → CORE NEEDLE or OPEN BIOPSY→STAGING CT

MRI (mandatory)T1 low (iso to muscle), T2 high, gadolinium: peripheral enhancement + non-enhancing necrotic centre · ~90% accurate benign vs malignant (ill-defined, large, heterogeneous)

CT cheststaging (lungs) · myxoid liposarcoma → CT chest/abdomen/pelvis (± bone scan)

PETevolving · useful for node-spreading sarcomas · ↓ uptake predicts response

Next step orderX-ray → MRI (✗ Wrong options: biopsy before MRI · FNA · excisional biopsy) → biopsy (✗ Wrong options: RT · chemo · resection · observation before tissue diagnosis) → staging

Biopsy

Core needlemodality of choice · correct in 68–93% (Orthobullets) · 84% (Skrzynski) · cheaper ($1106 vs $7234)

Open biopsygold standard · after non-diagnostic core (e.g. "only blood" in a presumed haematoma)

Excisional biopsyonly small (<3 cm) clearly benign lesions

Whoat the treating centre, by the definitive surgeon · 2–12× more complications otherwise

Techniquelongitudinal incision · avoid NV structures/compartments · meticulous haemostasis · drain distal + in line with incision · expert pathologist
✗ Wrong options: transverse

Histology distractors

Lesion Histology
Leiomyosarcoma Spindle cells, smooth muscle
Myxoma Paucicellular myxoid stroma, bland spindle cells, macrophages
Haematoma Cystic cavity, necrotic debris, fibrin, clot
Neurofibroma (dermal) Discoid proliferations of peripheral nerves
Fibroma Poorly marginated hypocellular collagen
Fibrosarcoma Herringbone spindle cells (TO)
MFH / UPS Storiform pleomorphic
Clear-cell sarcoma Nests of clear cells in fibrocollagenous septa, giant cells

AJCC 8th staging (extremity / trunk)

Stage Size Nodes Mets Grade
IA <5 cm None None Low
IB >5 cm None None Low
II <5 cm None None Intermediate/high
IIIA 5–10 cm None None Intermediate/high
IIIB >10 cm None None Intermediate/high
IVA Any Any N+ None Any
IVB Any Any M+ Any

8th edition changesseparates appendicular/truncal from other sites (head/neck, visceral, retroperitoneum) — widely accepted · depth removed (not independent) · new T3 (10–15 cm) + T4 (>15 cm) · node = stage IV (controversial, equated with lung mets)
✗ Wrong options: margins · histologic subtype · hospital type

Vanderbilt stagingincludes depth · Enneking — no size, not for marrow/RES tumours, limited for spine

5Management

Soft tissue sarcoma (localized)
  • Most→WIDE RESECTION + RT (pre- or post-op)
  • Abutting critical structure→PLANNED CLOSE / MICRO-POSITIVE MARGIN + RT
    (remove adventitia / perineurium / periosteum)
  • Encasing critical structure→EN BLOC with the structure (bypass graft if possible)
  • Can't get negative margin / irreversible nerve loss / comorbid→AMPUTATION (often foot)
  • Resectable lung mets→WEDGE RESECTION / LOBECTOMY

Surgical margins & resection

Most important treatment factor (subcutaneous sarcoma DFS) — size of operative margin
✗ Wrong options: chemo dose · RT · RFA

High gradeat least wide · not intralesional or marginal · dorsum of foot → wide resection + fasciocutaneous flap
✗ Wrong options: STSG over tendons

Abutting SFA + sciatic nerve, tumour within 1 mm, ink negativeequivalent to wide resection removing the structures (with RT)

Periosteal resection >10 cm → fracture risk → prophylactic nailing

Large midfoot synovial sarcomatranstibial amputation (best local control, fastest return)

Resection techniqueelliptical incision incorporating biopsy + drain sites · tourniquet without exsanguination · frozen section margins

Lung metsfirst-line surgical resection if complete resection possible (wedge/lobectomy) · curative in up to 25%
✗ Wrong options: RT · chemo first

Chemotherapycontroversial (no survival benefit in RCTs for most) · consider high grade · standard for rhabdomyosarcoma + Ewing · synovial sarcoma more chemo-sensitive

Radiation therapy

Effect↓ local recurrence regardless of margin status · no overall survival benefit (Yang) · transient strength/ROM loss · does not hamper limb salvage

Dose50 Gy pre-op, 66 Gy post-op (Orthobullets, OB7588) · 60 Gy typical adjuvant (OB1117), range 45–65 Gy · palliative bone mets ~30 Gy · HO prophylaxis ~6 Gy

Pre-op Post-op
Wound complications ↑ — 20–30% (Orthobullets) · 30% (O'Sullivan) · 35% vs 17% (OB7588) ↓
Dose · volume Lower, smaller Higher, larger
Fibrosis, oedema, stiffness, fracture, lymphoedema ↓ ↑ · ↑ radiation-induced sarcoma
Function Trend better —
Local recurrence · survival No difference No difference

RT not usedchondrosarcoma, osteosarcoma (as adjuvant) · WD liposarcoma / ALT → excision alone (recurrence 2%)

Unplanned excision — tumour bed re-excision

Commonest mistake in STS treatment · 74% residual disease

Unplanned ("whoops") excision
  • First→MRI limb + CT chest/abdomen/pelvis (no RT or surgery before imaging)
  • Positive margins→TUMOUR BED RE-EXCISION (wide, ≥3 cm along scar) ± RT
  • Negative margins→OBSERVATION vs ADJUVANT RT

Deep, positive margins (e.g. sartorius)wide tumour bed excision + RT
✗ Wrong options: RT alone · amputation · chemo

High-grade superficial forearmwide resection of tumour bed + coverage
✗ Wrong options: marginal to spare NV

After wide re-excision vs planned↑ plastic reconstruction · less blood loss · same function · 5-yr LR-free >90% · no extra chemo

Specific subtypes (clear cell, alveolar soft part)

Clear-cell sarcomamalignant melanoma of soft parts · adolescents/young adults · foot/ankle, bound to tendons · S-100, HMB45 · lymph node mets · chemo + RT resistant → wide excision / amputation

Alveolar soft part sarcoma<1% of STS · very vascular · lung, bone, CNS mets at presentation · LR 11–50% · wide resection + metastasectomy

Fibrosarcoma (TO)diagnosis of exclusion · herringbone spindle cells · 5–10 cm slow painless deep lower-limb mass, 30–50 yr

Synovial sarcoma — histology & prognosis (TO)

Not synovial in originarises near bursae, tendon sheaths, capsules from multipotent stem cell rests → biphasic = epithelial + mesenchymal (spindle) · lacks synovial differentiation

Subtypesbiphasic (epithelial + spindle) · monophasic (spindle only) · poorly differentiated

Geneticst(X;18)(p11;q11), SS18 (SYT) on 18 + SSX1/2/4 on X → SYT-SSX · in >90% / 95% · SYT-SSX1 → biphasic · markers (poorly differentiated): CD56, CD99, HMW keratin · BCL-2 under study

Epidemiology7% (6–8%) of STS · commonest foot STS · commonest non-RMS paediatric STS · median 35 yr, 15–40 · slight M · knee commonest

ImagingX-ray: heterogeneous well-defined septate cystic mass, calcification in 1/3 (CT best) · MRI: cystic, sharp, multiloculated → misread as benign · PET for mets/recurrence

Gradeall high grade · FNCLCC grading used

Treatmentwide excision (>4 cm margin) + RT (all >5 cm: EBRT, brachytherapy, IMRT) ± (neo)adjuvant chemo · amputation for fungating mass, difficult fracture, infection · metastatic → doxorubicin + ifosfamide

Advanced-disease drug Mechanism
Pazopanib Only approved targeted drug for STS · TKI vs VEGFR, PDGFR, KIT
Trabectedin Blocks DNA binding of the fusion oncoprotein
Tazemetostat EZH2 inhibitor (INI1/SMARCB1-negative)
NY-ESO-1 T cells Antigen in 80%
Isolated limb perfusion TNF-α + melphalan/doxorubicin, hyperthermic · limb preservation 72–96%
Poor prognosis (TO) Yes / No
Age >50 (younger better) ✓
Size ≥5 cm ✓
Deep ✓
Axial site (distal extremity best) ✓
Higher stage · FNCLCC grade II–III · mitoses ✓
Monophasic histology ✓
CXCR4, IGF-1R nuclear expression ✓
Inadequate margins ✓
Biphasic histology No (favourable)

Metastasismost important factor · in ~50% · lung 80%, nodes up to 20%, bone 9.9%, liver 4.5% · 5-yr OS ~60% (61–73%)

6Complications

Metastasislungs commonest (40–60%) · then lymph nodes (OB6341) · or bone/soft tissue (OB7545) · liver, brain rare

Local recurrence<10% with surgery + RT · commonest site for low-grade recurrence = local · most within 5 yr, but follow indefinitely

Radiationpre-op wound problems · post-op fibrosis, fracture, secondary sarcoma

Prognosis

Most important factortumour stage (Orthobullets) · metastatic disease at presentation (OB6333, bone sarcoma) · histological grade (TO one-liner)

Poorhigh grade · mets · >5 cm · deep to fascia · delayed diagnosis · unplanned excision

Stage 5-yr survival
Low grade 90%
II–III 50–75%
IV 15% · median 12–18 months
