Squamous Cell Carcinoma

Pathology · 60 / 66

1Basics

Naturemalignant tumour of keratinocytes (epidermis) · elderly

Handcommonest malignancy of the hand (skin cancers ≫ primary MSK malignancy) · commonest subungual malignancy · dorsum of hand + forearm

Skin overall2nd commonest skin cancer after BCC (OB212007) · in the hand, BCC is 2nd (OB220030) · face/neck > hand

Risk factorsUV exposure (main) · actinic keratosis · Bowen's disease, keratin horns, leukoplakia · solid-organ transplant (immunosuppression) · chronic osteomyelitis

Marjolin ulcer

DefinitionSCC in chronic draining sinus tracts (osteomyelitis), burn scars, pressure sores

Rate~1% malignant change · latency ~30 yr · many low grade

DistractorsMooren (corneal) · chancroid (genital) · Curling (peptic, burns) · Cushing (gastric, ↑ ICP)

High suspicionbiopsy chronic draining sites

3Presentation

Indurated, scaly, erythematous plaque/lesion · pain · subungual mass mistaken for haematoma

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral)lytic lesion when bone involved (e.g. distal phalanx tuft destruction)

Biopsypunch, shave or incisional

Histology

Keratin nodules / squamous pearls · tumour nests penetrating deep fascia

Skin lesion Feature
SCC Keratin pearls
BCC Pink/flesh papule, central ulcer · nests of basal cells
Melanoma From naevus · ABCD changes
Seborrhoeic keratosis "Stuck-on" waxy lesion · histology like SCC
Lentigo Small pigmented macule
M. marinum Culture 30–32°C
Sarcoidosis Non-caseating granulomas

5Management

Hand SCC — diagnosis & treatment

Squamous cell carcinoma
  • Most→WIDE LOCAL RESECTION (6 mm margin to subcutaneous fat) ± SKIN GRAFT
  • Small lesions→MOHS MICROSURGERY (highest cure rate)
  • Bone involvement (digit)→AMPUTATION
  • Marjolin ulcer→WIDE RESECTION / AMPUTATION + soft-tissue reconstruction

Adjuvant RT (Orthobullets)>2 cm wide · 4 mm deep · perineural invasion · nodal mets · OB: RT not needed for primary SCC, not typical for SCC ("radiation" is wrong in Marjolin and hand stems)

Lymph node biopsymay be needed

Marjolin✗ Wrong options: IV antibiotics · flap or STSG alone

Treated with wide resection alone
SCC · adamantinoma · chordoma · chondrosarcoma · parosteal OS ✓

6Complications

Prognosisnodal metastases + high histologic grade = poor · more metastatic than BCC · lymphatic → nodes, then lungs, liver, brain, bone, skin

✗ Wrong options: Curling · Cushing · Mooren · chancroid

✗ Wrong options: antibiotics · flap · STSG · RT

"Most common malignancy of the hand?"SCC
✗ Wrong options: epithelioid sarcoma · lung mets · chondrosarcoma

"El Paso construction worker, scaly indurated hand lesion, SCC?"wide local resection
✗ Wrong options: transradial amputation · RT

"Subungual 'haematoma', bone involved, digit amputated?"SCC
✗ Wrong options: BCC · epithelioid sarcoma

"Kidney transplant, UV exposure, dorsal hand plaque, keratin nodules?"SCC
✗ Wrong options: BCC · melanoma · seborrhoeic keratosis

Synovial Chondromatosis

Pathology · 61 / 66

1Basics

Diagnosis — benign metaplasia

Naturebenign metaplastic proliferation of synovium → cartilage metaplasia → multiple intra-articular loose bodies (soft synovial tissue → firm cartilage nodules → calcify → ossify)
✗ Wrong options: neoplasia · reactive arthritis · OA · vascular

Metaplasiaone non-cancerous phenotype → another (cf. Barrett's oesophagus — but Barrett's has high malignant risk)

Demographics30–50 yr (3rd–5th decades) · M:F 2:1

Siteknee commonest: 70% (OB219396) · 50–65% (TO) · then hip (20%), shoulder, elbow, ankle, wrist · also tendon sheaths, bursae (e.g. over an osteochondroma) · not purely intra-articular

Geneticsoccasional chromosome 6 abnormalities

Primary vs secondary (TO)

Primary (Reichel syndrome) Secondary
Frequency Less common More common
Joint One large joint, no underlying pathology Knee/hip/shoulder with OA, trauma, inflammation, osteonecrosis, neuropathy
Age · sex 3rd–5th decade · M >2:1 Older
Bodies Many, uniform · "ring and arc" calcification Fewer, larger, variable size · concentric rings
Origin Synovial metaplasia Shed hyaline cartilage implants in synovium

Phases (TO)

Phase Pathology
1 Active synovial cartilage formation, no loose bodies
2 Synovial proliferation + shedding of loose bodies (commonest MRI pattern)
3 Many loose bodies, no active synovial disease

3Presentation

Slow pain + swelling (worse with activity) · mechanical symptoms — catching, locking, stiffness · ↓ ROM (e.g. 0–40°), crepitus, effusion, quadriceps atrophy · ± warmth, erythema

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral)multiple stippled / spheroidal calcified loose bodies · ring-and-arc chondroid calcification (pathognomonic) · extrinsic bone erosion possible · 20% radiographically occult (mineralization is time-dependent)

MRIearly disease: cartilage nodules only on MRI · lobular · nodules low T1, high T2 · later signal drop-out (calcification)

Arthroscopyfinds far more nodules than the X-ray shows (unmineralized)

Histology

Discrete hyaline cartilage nodules at various stages of calcification/ossification · clustered chondrocytes with mild atypia, binucleate cells, occasional mitoses

Differential

Lesion Key distinction
PVNS No mineralization, bloody effusions, low T1+T2, blooming · CSF1
Synovial sarcoma (periarticular) Rarely intra-articular, diffuse mineralization, no loose bodies
Synovial haemangioma Diffuse mineralization (phleboliths), no loose bodies
Lipoma arborescens No calcification
OA loose bodies Fewer, smaller, mobile
Gout Urate crystals, erosions, no calcified bodies
Septic arthritis Acute, no cartilage on biopsy
Chondroblastoma Osseous, cobblestone chondroblasts

5Management

Treatment & recurrence

Synovial chondromatosis
  • Mild symptoms, ROM preserved→OBSERVATION
  • Pain, ↓ ROM, ↓ function→LOOSE BODY REMOVAL + SYNOVECTOMY
    • Arthroscopic (preferred — TO) or open (similar outcomes)

Benign metaplasia → symptomatic treatment only
✗ Wrong options: wide excision · chemo · disarticulation

Hip, young adult, joint space preservedarthroscopic loose body removal (+ synovectomy, chondroplasty, labral debridement)
✗ Wrong options: THA · resurfacing · 600 cGy RT

Knee"open, complete synovectomy" (OB7782) · complete synovectomy role debated (OB219396) · may prevent degenerative change

Recurrenceup to 30% (OB219396) · 3–23% overall, arthroscopic hip 7.1–25% (OB220967) · due to incomplete synovial removal · not joint-specific or predicted by ring-and-arc

6Complications

Malignant transformation (synovial chondrosarcoma)

Incidence1% to 6.4% (TO) · ~5% (OB) · long-standing disease with multiple recurrences

Grademostly low (grade 1: 20%, grade 2: 70%)

Feature Recurrence Malignancy
Extensive calcified mass in/around joint Yes Yes
Soft-tissue / bursal extension Yes Yes
Deep extrinsic bone erosion Yes Yes
Cortical destruction + marrow invasion / permeation No ✓ (most reliable)
Rapid growth, rapid clinical decline — Suspicious → biopsy
Lung mets No Definitive

Histology of malignancymarked myxoid matrix · sheet-like / "lining up" chondrocytes (loss of clustering) · spindling · necrosis · marrow permeation · moderate–marked atypia

Untreatedarticular destruction, chronic pain

✗ Wrong options: wide excision · neoplastic

✗ Wrong options: PVNS · lipoma arborescens · haemangioma · synovial sarcoma

✗ Wrong options: OA loose bodies · chondroblastoma

"Stiff hip, arthroscopy or open debridement — underlying process?"metaplasia

"Knee ROM 0–40°, calcified bodies — true?"benign metaplastic process
✗ Wrong options: hip commonest · purely intra-articular · CSF1 · urate

✗ Wrong options: soft-tissue extension · extrinsic erosion · calcified mass

"Painful hip chondromatosis, young adult?"arthroscopic loose body removal

Synovial Sarcoma

Pathology · 62 / 66

1Basics

Epidemiology

Naturemalignant, usually high-grade STS · unknown cell of origin · spreads along fascial planes
✗ Wrong options: type II synoviocyte (misnomer from microscopic resemblance)

Frequency5–10% of STS (Orthobullets, OB5861) · 6% (OB7586) · 7% / 6–8% (TO) · 4th commonest STS (OB8312)

Age15–40 yr · commonest STS in young adults/adolescents (16% of STS at that age; RMS 15%) · 30% of childhood STS · mean <30

SexM ≈ F (1.2:1; 1.1:1)

Siteperiarticular, rarely intra-articular (only 10% in a major joint) · lower limb 60% · knee / popliteal fossa commonest → ankle, foot · shoulder, elbow, axilla · commonest sarcoma of the foot

Genetics — t(X;18) SYT-SSX

t(X;18)(p11;q11) in >90% · SYT (SS18) on 18 + SSX1 / SSX2 / SSX4 (rare) on X → SYT-SSX fusion · detect by RT-PCR

Mechanismfusion binds BAF complex, displaces tumour suppressor BAF47 → activates Sox2

SYT-SSX1 SYT-SSX2
Frequency More common (60%) 40%
Histology Biphasic Monophasic
Sex M:F 1:1 M:F 1:2
Presentation Larger, with mets Smaller, without mets
Survival Worse (Orthobullets, OB213030 answer) Better

ConflictTO: monophasic histology = adverse, biphasic favourable (and OB213030's explanation says biphasic survives better) · Orthobullets: SSX1 (biphasic) worse · fusion type = most important prognostic factor (Orthobullets)

Fusion / translocation Tumour
SYT-SSX, t(X;18) Synovial sarcoma
EWS-FLI1, t(11;22) Ewing
PAX3-FKHR, t(2;13) Alveolar RMS
EWS-ATF1, t(12;22) Clear-cell sarcoma
FUS-CHOP / FUS-DDIT3, t(12;16) Myxoid liposarcoma
t(9;22) Myxoid chondrosarcoma · BCR-ABL CML
COL1A1-PDGFB DFSP
Ring chromosome 12 WD liposarcoma, MFH (ring chromosomes also parosteal OS)
Monosomy 14, 22 GIST

Genes & lab markers

IHCvimentin +, epithelial membrane antigen (EMA) +, keratin / cytokeratin + (nearly all biphasic, many monophasic), sporadic S-100 · only synovial sarcoma (vs MFH) stains vimentin + EMA · TO: CD56, CD99, HMW keratin (poorly differentiated)

3Presentation

Slow-growing mass near a joint · painless or painful · mistaken for ganglion/bursitis · large deep >5 cm

Nerve signse.g. popliteal mass → plantar tingling, weak push-off, ↓ knee flexion

Check regional lymph nodes

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnosis & features (calcification, foot, histology)

X-raysoft-tissue calcification — 15–20% (OB6293) · 1/3 (TO) · irregular (vs round phleboliths of haemangioma) · may mimic HO · commonest STS with calcification
✗ Wrong options: Ewing · clear-cell · MFH

USnon-specific hypoechoic mass

CTwell-circumscribed, calcification · CT chest staging

MRI (gold standard)heterogeneous, dark T1 / bright T2 · T2 "triple sign" (high: necrosis/cyst · intermediate · low: calcification/fibrosis) · cysts in up to 20% · well-defined → misread as benign

Biopsycore needle or incisional · not FNA (no architecture)

WorkupMRI of the limb + CT chest · nodes examined · no bone scan · not US only

Histology

Biphasic (commonest) — epithelial cells (gland, nest, cyst-like; pale columnar) + spindle cells · monophasic — spindle only · spindle cells small, uniform, sheets/fascicles, scant cytoplasm, dark atypical nuclei

Histology distractor Lesion
Round cells in fibrous septae, giant cells Alveolar RMS
Nests of clear cells, fibrous septa, giant cells Clear-cell sarcoma
Signet-ring lipoblasts, chicken-wire capillaries Myxoid liposarcoma
Small round blue cells, rosettes Ewing
Granulomatous look Epithelioid sarcoma
Storiform pleomorphic MFH
Haemosiderin macrophages, villi PVNS
Osteoid · "blue balls of cartilage" OS · chondrosarcoma

5Management

Standardwide resection + perioperative RT (neoadjuvant or adjuvant — no consensus) · RT ↓ local recurrence and improves OS + DSS (Orthobullets)

Chemotherapyone of the more chemosensitive STS · may improve local control + OS · reserved for high-risk, metastatic, younger patients · metastatic → doxorubicin + ifosfamide (TO)

Large midfoot tumourtranstibial amputation (STS chapter)

6Complications

Lymph node spread & staging

Metastasis30–60% · lung commonest · lymph nodes (with angiosarcoma, clear-cell, RMS, epithelioid) → sentinel node biopsy · nodes worse than none but better than lung mets · ↑ with large, deep, high grade

Only RACES member with SYT-SSX1 → synovial sarcoma

vs myxoid liposarcoma t(12;16)synovial has fewer bony mets · but more nodal mets, more local recurrence, commoner in foot

Liposarcoma mets by gradeWD <1% · myxoid 10–30% · high grade >50%

Prognosispoor · 5-yr 50–60% · 10-yr ~25% · TO 5-yr ~60% (61–73%) · factors: size, grade, location, age, margins, fusion type

"NOT a characteristic of synovial sarcoma?"usually intra-articular

"29-yr-old forefoot pain, heterogeneous T2-bright mass?"synovial sarcoma
✗ Wrong options: plantar fibromatosis · Morton's

"SYT-SSX1 tumour that spreads to nodes?"synovial sarcoma

"Thigh SYT-SSX sarcoma — further workup?"MRI femur + CT chest
✗ Wrong options: bone scan · US

Telangiectatic Intramedullary Osteosarcoma

Pathology · 63 / 66

1Basics

Naturerare (4% of OS), malignant, high-grade OS

Demographics5–25 yr · M > F

Sitesame as ABC — proximal humerus, proximal femur, distal femur, proximal tibia · knee commonest in stems

Risk factorprior radiation

Genes & lab markers

Tumour suppressorsRb-1, p53 · oncogenes — HER2/neu, c-myc, c-fos

Prognostic labs↑ LDH, ↑ ALP → poor

3Presentation

Pain (ABC-like), night pain, poor NSAID relief, limp · swelling, tenderness

Pathologic fracture25%

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raylytic, destructive, expansile, eccentric, septae · cortex may be disrupted · periosteal reaction possible

MRI (all cases)fluid-fluid levels (less defined than ABC) · extensive surrounding oedema · soft-tissue component

Bone scan↑ uptake

Histology

Lakes of blood mixed with malignant cells (not in ABC) · high-grade cells with mitoses in intervening cellular septa · less osteoid than conventional OS · gross: "bag of blood" · experienced MSK pathologist essential

ABC vs telangiectatic OS

ABC Telangiectatic OS
Age · site 75% <20 · same four sites 5–25 · same four sites
X-ray Expansile, eccentric, lytic, septae, cortical thinning Same, possible cortical disruption
MRI Clear fluid levels, may extend to soft tissue Fluid levels less defined, surrounding oedema
Histology Blood spaces, spindle cells, benign giant cells, no malignant cells Blood lakes with malignant cells + mitoses
Treatment Curettage + graft Chemo + wide resection + chemo

Other distractorsGCT (females, 3rd–5th decade, curettage) · chondroblastoma (epiphyseal, cobblestone chondroblasts) · UBC (aspiration + steroid)

5Management

Neoadjuvant chemo (8–12 wk) → wide resection (limb salvage) → adjuvant chemo (6–12 mo) · as conventional OS

Agentsdoxorubicin (adriamycin), cisplatin, methotrexate, ifosfamide

Limb salvagepossible in ~90%

✗ Wrong optionswide resection alone · curettage + graft (ABC) · RT · steroid injection · observation · hip disarticulation first

6Complications

Local recurrence~5% · poor prognosis · ↑ with displaced pathologic fracture

Good≥98% necrosis after chemo · good response + wide margins ↓ recurrence

Pooradvanced stage (most important) · ↑ LDH, ↑ ALP · MDR gene (25% primaries, 50% mets)

Survivalmore chemosensitive, same survival as intramedullary OS · localized ~70% · metastatic ~20%

How it's asked

"21-yr-old proximal tibial mass, blood lakes with malignant cells — first step after staging?"neoadjuvant chemotherapy
✗ Wrong options: wide excision first · curettage · RT

"14-yr-old knee night pain, expansile lesion with blood lakes + atypia?"osteosarcoma — neoadjuvant chemo + wide resection
✗ Wrong options: ABC (wide + RT) · GCT · wide resection alone

"18-yr-old femur, poorly defined fluid levels, blood with mitoses + osteoid?"neoadjuvant chemo → wide resection → adjuvant chemo
✗ Wrong options: curettage · steroid injection

Tumoral Calcinosis

Pathology · 64 / 66

1Basics

Diagnosis & phosphate metabolism (FGF23)

Naturerare, hereditary dysfunction of phosphate regulation → massive periarticular, extracapsular calcinosis
✗ Wrong options: calcium · oxalate · chloride metabolism · renal tubular insufficiency

Demographicsfemales · African-Americans · onset childhood / early adolescence (stems ~30 yr)

Sitehip (trochanteric bursa commonest) > shoulder (Orthobullets) · extensor surfaces: hip, elbow (olecranon), shoulder, foot, wrist (OB7499)

Genes & lab markers

FGF-23 defect → familial hyperphosphataemic tumoral calcinosis
✗ Wrong options: IDH1 (Maffucci) · dystrophin (DMD) · ACVR1 (FOP) · 1α-hydroxylase (rickets)

Labshyperphosphataemia (OB) · calcium normal (OB7499) · Orthobullets: Ca, PO₄, uric acid, ALP normal or slightly ↑; renal and parathyroid function normal/slightly ↑ · PTH may ↑

Associationsmetabolic defects, collagen vascular disorders, trauma

3Presentation

Palpable mass / swelling around a joint · slow-growing, rarely painful · pain from compression of neighbours · ROM usually preserved

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayamorphous, cystic, lobular (round/oval), well-demarcated periarticular calcification · bones/joints rarely involved directly

CTfluid-fluid levels — calcium layering ("sedimentation sign") · homogeneous → less active, less growth

MRIdiffuse low signal · or bright nodular pattern alternating with signal voids · fluid levels

Bone scanintense uptake in the masses

Histology

Lobulated masses with defined capsules + thick septae · filled with calcareous material (calcium phosphate, carbonate, hydroxyapatite) + fluid · loculated calcific debris in fibrous stroma

Differential

Lesion Key distinction
Calcinosis of renal failure (secondary) Same X-ray + histology · CRF / dialysis, GFR, vit D, ↑ PO₄ · ~1% prevalence · any race · medical treatment (diet, binders, dialysate)
Calcific tendinitis Within tendon · shoulder > hip > elbow > wrist > knee · no sedimentation
Synovial osteochondromatosis Intra-articular / tendon sheath · rings and arcs
Myositis ossificans / HO Not lobular · faint calcification → bone in 6 weeks · mature cortex + medulla
Gout Hyperuricaemia · focal bone erosions
Osteochondroma · chondrosarcoma · BPOP (Nora) Bone-attached lesions

5Management

Tumoral calcinosis
  • Asymptomatic→OBSERVATION
  • Symptomatic, ↓ function→COMPLETE SURGICAL EXCISION (+ normalize phosphate)

Recurrence riskincomplete excision · poorly circumscribed lesion · excision while actively growing

How it's asked

"59-yr-old dialysis-dependent diabetic, painless calcified hip bump?"calcinosis of renal failure
✗ Wrong options: synovial chondromatosis · MO · parosteal OS

"30-yr-old African-American woman, hip swelling 3 yr, lobulated calcific masses?"tumoral calcinosis
✗ Wrong options: osteochondroma · chondrosarcoma · Nora's

"Tumoral calcinosis is a hereditary disorder of?"phosphate metabolism
✗ Wrong options: calcium · oxalate

"Olecranon calcified nodule — lab + gene?"hyperphosphataemia; FGF-23
✗ Wrong options: IDH1 · ACVR1 · dystrophin

Undifferentiated Pleomorphic Sarcoma

Pathology · 65 / 66

1Basics

Nameformerly malignant fibrous histiocytoma (MFH) · MFH removed from WHO 2013 · high-grade, aggressive, malignant fibrogenic tumour · cell of origin unknown

Commonest STSin adults 55–80 (Orthobullets) · commonest STS overall / of the extremity in mature adults (OB) · commonest radiation-associated STS · also after Paget's

Incidence1–9 / 100,000

Demographics55–80 yr (Orthobullets) · mean 59, range 32–80 (OB) · M > F (1.2:1) · Caucasian > African-American

Sitelower limb (~50%) > upper limb (~20%) > retroperitoneum · from bone in 1–5% (see Pleomorphic Sarcoma of Bone chapter)

Genes & lab markers

PD-1 expression in 30%

Labshypoglycaemia, ↑ WBC · ± fever

IHC — diagnosis of exclusionno lineage markers: negative for desmin, myogenin, SMA, S-100, CD34, MDM2, EMA · FISH negative for t(X;18), t(12;16)

Positive marker Excludes UPS →
Desmin, myogenin Rhabdomyosarcoma
Desmin, SMA Leiomyosarcoma
S-100 MPNST
CD34 DFSP
MDM2 amplification WD liposarcoma
EMA, t(X;18) Synovial sarcoma
t(12;16) Myxoid liposarcoma

3Presentation

Slow-growing, painless mass (can grow rapidly, fungate) · trauma draws attention · no ecchymosis (vs haematoma) · mass effect → NV symptoms · tight trousers, ↓ hip flexion

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnosis (MRI, immunohistochemistry)

X-rayorthogonal views first

MRI (mandatory)T1 isointense to muscle (low) · T2 heterogeneous high · heterogeneous enhancement, non-enhancing necrotic centre (poor prognosis)

MRI distractor Lesion
Isointense to fat on all sequences Lipoma / low-grade liposarcoma
Well-circumscribed, homogeneous T2 high Myxoid lesion
Intramuscular, T2 high, peri-lesional oedema at poles, fat rind, thin septal enhancement Intramuscular myxoma (40–70, F)

Histology

Spindle + histiocytic + multinucleated eosinophilic (bizarre) giant cells · storiform (cartwheel) pattern around small vessels · atypia, high mitotic index · gross: nodular grey-white

Subtype Share
Pleomorphic 80–85%
Giant cell 10%
Inflammatory <10%

AJCC staging (extremity / trunk)

Stage Size Nodes Mets Grade
IA <5 cm None None Low
IB >5 cm None None Low
II <5 cm None None Intermediate/high
IIIA 5–10 cm None None Intermediate/high
IIIB >10 cm None None Intermediate/high
IVA Any Regional None Any
IVB Any Any Present Any

Surgical margins (Enneking)

Margin Plane
Intralesional Through tumour — pseudocapsule violated
Marginal Through pseudocapsule / reactive zone (may leave satellite cells)
Wide Tumour + cuff of normal tissue
Radical Whole compartment / amputation

R classification (AJCC)R0 microscopically negative (goal of curative surgery) · R1 microscopic positive · R2 gross · UICC R+1 variant

Myxofibrosarcomainfiltrative → plan ≥3 cm margin, frozen sections

5Management

Treatment — wide resection & radiation

Standardwide resection + RT (pre- or post-op)
✗ Wrong options: wide resection alone · RT alone · marginal ± RT · wide + chemo · debridement · intralesional debulking

RT dose50 Gy pre-op · 66 Gy post-op (Orthobullets, OB217430) · 50–60 Gy (OB213031)

Pre-op RTlower dose, ~30% wound complications · post-op → more late toxicity, radiation-induced sarcoma

Chemosome cases · neoadjuvant chemo improves 5-yr RFS + OS in tumours >10 cm

Amputation / disarticulationonly if negative margins impossible, major nerve loss, or comorbidity · limb salvage whenever reconstruction possible

Biopsyat the treating centre · longitudinal · drain distal + in line · excised en bloc with tumour (elliptical incision)

Follow-upclose surveillance 5–10 yr

6Complications

Prognosis5-yr survival 50–60% · aggressive

How it's asked

"64-yr-old forearm mass, FISH negative for t(X;18) + t(12;16), desmin/myogenin/CD34 negative?"high-grade pleomorphic undifferentiated sarcoma
✗ Wrong options: alveolar RMS · myxoid liposarcoma · DFSP · synovial

"UPS MRI?"T1 isointense to muscle, T2 heterogeneous high
✗ Wrong options: fat-isointense (lipoma) · homogeneous T2 (myxoid) · polar oedema (myxoma)

"65-yr-old deep thigh mass, pleomorphic, SMA/desmin/S100/MDM2 negative?"UPS
✗ Wrong options: leiomyosarcoma · WD liposarcoma · MPNST · synovial

"Fungating posteromedial leg mass, necrotic centre, pleomorphic spindle cells?"wide resection + RT
✗ Wrong options: wide resection alone · debridement

"63-yr-old enlarging thigh mass, UPS?"wide resection + radiation
✗ Wrong options: wide + chemo · marginal + RT

"Truck driver, large anteromedial thigh UPS, no mets?"RT + wide surgical excision
✗ Wrong options: hip disarticulation · debulking · RT alone

"Neoadjuvant RT then resection, normal tissue cuff at closest margin?"wide
✗ Wrong options: marginal · radical

Unicameral Bone Cyst

Pathology · 66 / 66

1Basics

Diagnosis, location & natural history

Naturenon-neoplastic, serous (yellow) fluid-filled cyst · aka simple bone cyst (SBC) · 3% of biopsied bone tumours / of primary tumours in first 2 decades

Age<20 yr (85%) · peak 3–14, mean ~9 · M:F 2:1

Sitelong bones 95%, central metaphysis next to physis → migrates to diaphysis with growth

Site Share
Proximal humerus 50–60% (Orthobullets) · 52% (OB219296)
Proximal femur 30% (Orthobullets) · 25% (OB219296) · 50% of femoral cysts in patients >17
Humerus + femur >80% (TO) · 90% (OB5435)
Calcaneus 6% · commonest tarsal
Others Distal tibia, ilium, spine (posterior elements), metacarpals, phalanges, distal radius · iliac wing, ribs, talus, radius → adults

Pathophysiologyvenous stasis in cancellous bone → resorption · ↑ pressure, inflammatory proteins, ↑ lysosomal enzymes in fluid · "temporary failure of medullary bone formation near the physis" · resolves after losing contact with physis

Genes / labsnone · no labs needed
✗ Wrong options: ALP (OS) · ESR · IgG (myeloma) · LDH (Ewing/OS)

Active vs latent

Active Latent
Distance to physis Adjacent, ~1 cm (Orthobullets) · <5 mm (TO) Normal bone between cyst and physis · >5 mm (TO)
Steroid injection Indicated, but lower success —
Curettage / fixation Avoid → growth arrest Symptomatic, failed injections, proximal femur

Natural historyshrinks near skeletal maturity, may heal after growth · Donaldson: none fully healed though 87% of physes closed
✗ Wrong options: grows · malignant

3Presentation

~80% asymptomatic · found incidentally or after fracture

Pathologic fracturepresents with fracture in 50–75% · 63–87% eventually fracture (TO) · commonest cause of pathologic fracture in children · minor trauma (throwing, sport)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (diagnostic)central, lytic, well-demarcated metaphyseal lesion, thin sclerotic rim · unilocular, symmetric cortical thinning, smooth endosteal scalloping · no periosteal reaction · not wider than the physis · oblong along long axis · 2–3% cross physis · multiloculated/trabeculated after fractures

Sign Meaning
Fallen leaf / fallen fragment Cortical fragment in dependent part after fracture — pathognomonic (✗ Wrong options: before fracture) (TO)
Trapdoor Fragment hinged on periosteum, folds inward (TO)
Rising bubble Gas in non-dependent part — pathognomonic
Doughnut (bone scan) Cold centre, warm rim

CTspine/pelvis · wall thickness, occult fracture, fracture risk · thin wall, pseudo-septa

MRIT1 dark (better fracture-risk predictor than X-ray) · T2 bright · rim enhancement · few septae/fluid levels · use when malignancy not excluded
✗ Wrong options: double-density fluid levels (ABC)

Bone scan / PETno role

Fracture-risk predictors (TO)

Index High risk
Bone cyst index (Kaelin & MacEwen) = cyst area ÷ diaphysis diameter² >4 humerus · >3.5 femur (MRI better than X-ray)
Bone cyst diameter (Ahn & Park) >85% on AP and lateral
Minimal cortical thickness <2 mm

Caveatpoor inter/intra-observer reliability · BCI remains best

Histology

Thin fibrous lining · fibrous tissue, giant cells, haemosiderin · few chronic inflammatory cells · cementum spherules (10%) · bland spindle cells · biopsy rarely needed (fracture callus may mimic osteosarcoma)

Differential

Bubbly lytic Usually aspiration + injection Occasionally curettage + graft
UBC ✓ ✓ ✓
ABC ✓ — —
NOF ✓ — ✓
Enchondroma (solitary) — — ✓
Eccentric vs central (TO) Features
SBC — central Least likely for an eccentric lytic lesion
ABC — eccentric 1–6% of primary bone tumours · <20 · slight F · femur > tibia, spine, pelvis · expansile, bulging cortex · wider than physis · MRI fluid levels (CT shows them in 1/3) · secondary, solid (giant cell reparative granuloma), soft-tissue forms
Fibrous cortical defect — eccentric 2–15 yr · commonest benign bone lesion (with NOF up to 40%) · M 2:1 · intracortical oval lucency, posteromedial distal femur
GCT — eccentric Epiphysis → metaphysis, closed physes, non-sclerotic margin, subchondral · fluid levels 10–14% (ABC component)
Other distractor Key feature
Telangiectatic OS Expansile, matrix, Codman, soft-tissue mass · ↑ WBC, platelets, LDH, ALP
FD Ground glass
OFD Anterior tibial cortex, osteoblastic rimming
EG Coffee-bean nuclei, Birbeck (tennis-racket) granules
ABC histology Many RBCs, giant cells, septa
Chondroblastoma · CMF · osteochondroma Epiphyseal · chondromyxoid lobules · cartilage cap

5Management

Pathologic fracture — sling & allow healing

UBC with pathologic fracture
  • Proximal humerus / non-weight-bearing→SLING (4–6 wk) — let fracture heal
    (no biopsy, staging, MRI or ORIF)
    • Cyst persists→ASPIRATION + INJECTION (steroid / marrow / DBM)
  • Proximal femur / weight-bearing, displaced, unstable, open→CURETTAGE + GRAFT + INTERNAL FIXATION

Immobilizationsling 4–6 wk (OB) · 3–6 wk (OB5683) · plaster cast (TO) · ~15% fill in after fracture (Orthobullets) · <10% heal (OB6098) · fracture healing usually does not resolve the cyst

✗ Wrong options (initially)biopsy, staging (CT chest, bone scan), MRI, ORIF, IM nail, steroid injection, curettage, RT/chemo, RFA

Proximal femur & calcaneus — surgical treatment

Proximal femur fracturehigh refracture + malunion/nonunion nonoperatively → ORIF + bone grafting (hip screw + proximal femoral plate)
✗ Wrong options: bed rest · spica · traction

Femoral neckvarus malunion, AVN, growth arrest (<10%)

Calcaneussurgery if symptomatic + 100% of ML width + ≥30% of AP length → open curettage + bone augmentation / cannulated screws
✗ Wrong options: MRI · bone scan · CT chest · wide excision

Persistent cyst — curettage & injection

Option Indication
Observation Small, asymptomatic, upper limb · X-ray at 6 mo · 42% success
Aspiration + methylprednisolone acetate Active cysts, proximal humerus · several injections (young children)
Bone marrow / DBM injection Effective, alone or combined
Curettage + bone graft (± fixation) Symptomatic latent cyst failing injections · displaced humeral fractures · proximal femur · recurrent / non-responding
Curettage + ESIN + graft Highest success (88%)
Decompression IM nail, cannulated screws, Rush rod (rarely needed in upper limb)

Not recommended (TO)polidocanol injection (✓ curettage, ✓ DBM injection, ✓ decompression)

Recurrence preventionintralesional corticosteroid + DBM + autologous marrow
✗ Wrong options: denosumab (GCT) · BMP-2 · pexidartinib (PVNS) · bisphosphonates

Adjuvantsliquid nitrogen / phenol → ABC, not UBC

Healing after surgery Criteria
Complete >95% opacification + cortical thickening + no pain
Partial 80–95% opacification ± cortical thickening
Incomplete <80% opacification, no cortical thickening

6Complications

Recurrence10–30% · risk: age ≤10 · follow all cysts with serial X-rays

LLD / angular deformityactive cyst crossing physis or into epiphysis · surgery in active phase

Prognosisfavourable · close follow-up in active phase (recurrence, fracture, growth arrest)

How it's asked

"5-yr-old, proximal humeral SBC with pathological fracture — next?"conservative (TO)
✗ Wrong options: curettage + graft · ORIF · nailing

"9-yr-old, painful snap throwing, humeral cyst fracture?"sling
✗ Wrong options: staging · biopsy · IM nail

"8-yr-old, car-door injury — diagnosis + treatment?"UBC, sling and swathe
✗ Wrong options: aspiration + cement · GCT curettage

"8-yr-old shoulder pain after collision?"sling immobilization
✗ Wrong options: steroid injection first · RFA

"10-yr-old skateboard fall, humeral cyst fracture?"immobilization alone
✗ Wrong options: MRI

"11-yr-old, benign-looking cyst on X-ray + CT with fracture?"immobilization
✗ Wrong options: biopsy

"Hockey player, sling from trainer, cyst fracture?"continue sling
✗ Wrong options: CT chest · MR arthrogram

"Lacrosse injury, non-displaced cyst fracture?"sling for comfort
✗ Wrong options: urgent biopsy · plate

"7-yr-old pathologic humeral fracture — initial?"sling and swathe
✗ Wrong options: needle biopsy (callus mimics OS)

"Fallen leaf sign — next?"let fracture heal, serial X-rays
✗ Wrong options: staging + biopsy

"5-yr-old fallen flake sign?"shoulder immobilizer
✗ Wrong options: MRI · oncology

"Gym-class throw, central lucency, thin cortex?"sling + closed fracture care

"7-yr-old softball, UBC histology?"immobilize 4–6 wk
✗ Wrong options: liquid nitrogen (ABC) · chemo

"7-yr-old ice-skater, proximal femoral cyst fracture?"ORIF + bone grafting
✗ Wrong options: bed rest · spica

"12-yr-old heel pain, large calcaneal cyst?"open curettage + bone augmentation
✗ Wrong options: MRI · wide excision

"NOT a recommended treatment of SBC?"polidocanol injection (TO)

"12-yr-old, healed fracture, now daily pain, recurrent cyst?"curettage + bone grafting
✗ Wrong options: IM nail · biopsy

"Adjunct to prevent UBC fracture recurrence?"steroid + DBM + autologous marrow injection
✗ Wrong options: denosumab

"Fallen fragment sign seen in?"simple bone cyst (TO)
✗ Wrong options: GCT · ABC · FD

"Trapdoor sign?"SBC (TO)

"Eccentric lytic lesion — least likely?"SBC (TO)
✗ Wrong options: ABC · GCT · FCD

"Fallen fragment sign is pathognomonic of?"fracture of UBC (TO)
✗ Wrong options: SBC before fracture

"15-yr-old calcaneal cyst — course at maturity?"decreases, may disappear

"Commonest site in children?"proximal humerus

"7-yr-old, prior biopsy, metaphyseal cyst?"UBC
✗ Wrong options: ABC (RBCs) · chondroblastoma · CMF

"13-yr-old distal femoral cyst, normal labs — next lab?"none required

"16-yr-old proximal femoral cyst, fibrous wall?"UBC
✗ Wrong options: ABC (double-density levels) · FD · OFD · EG

"Femoral cyst fracture fixed — commonest UBC site?"proximal humerus

"Tennis player, fallen leaf — histology?"fluid-filled fibrous-lined cavity, occasional giant cells
